Veil Protocol · The Other Side

THE STORY

The whole of Chapter One, as written — every road out of that 3:47am kitchen, every choice it leaves you, and the five ways it can end.

The spine of the campaign — five Books from the kitchen to the vacant Seat. Authored in waves; chapters marked to be authored are coming.

Book One

The Vanishing

A brother gone quiet on a Wednesday, and the morning that will not give him back.

Chapter One — The Kitchen written

The 3:47am kitchen, the cold key, Gin's archive, and the five roads out. (Playable; see the Roads tab.)

Chapter Two — The Archive written
The table, the book open to a page that wasn't there last night

You clear the cold dinner-for-two off the table without quite looking at the second plate, and you set the Archive down in the cleared space like a thing that might wake. It is heavier than a book that size has any business being. Gin's hand is on every page — the small, exact print of a man who trusted ink more than memory, who once told you that the difference between a practitioner who lives and one who does not is whether they wrote it down before the night made them doubt it.

There is an index card slipped under the front cover, and on it, in that same hand but pressed harder than the rest, a single instruction: If I stop writing, read it in order. Out of order it lies by omission. You did not see it last night. You are almost certain you did not see it last night. The flat is the particular quiet of a place that has decided to listen, and somewhere under the floor the building's old pipes tick as they cool, counting down to nothing.

The choice it leaves you
Read from the first page, exactly as he asked.
The slow way. Gin built the order for a reason, and the reason is that some of these names only make sense once you already believe the ones before them.
Skip to the last entries — the ones near the date he went quiet.
The fast way, and the forbidden one. The Archive 'lies by omission' read out of order; you would be trusting the most compromised pages first.
Four nodes, four locks, four people you have never met

However you come to it, the shape of the thing resolves the way a face resolves out of static once you stop trying to see it. The Archive is not a journal. It is a key-ring. Gin mapped the unregistered network of Northern Tokyo the way the Directorate never could — not as a list of suspects but as a map of trust, who would open a door for whom, and at what hour, and after which password — and he locked the live parts of it behind four people. Four primary nodes. A baker who does not know what he is. A clinic behind a blue curtain. A café that keeps Tuesday evenings free for the kind of customer who comes in already knowing. A shrine room behind a Korean restaurant in Shin-Ōkubo.

None of them will speak to a stranger. All of them, the margins say in Gin's smallest hand, owe him — not money, the dangerous kind of debt, the kind a careful man collects only once. He never collected. He left them for you instead, which is either the most generous thing your brother ever did or the most damning, because to walk this map is to announce, to everyone Gin protected, that the man who kept them safe is gone.

The choice it leaves you
Start with the baker — the harmless one.
Kondō Fumio, Jūjō Ginza, Thursday mornings, anpan. He does not know he is a practitioner; he only knows Gin was kind to him. Begin where it costs least.
Start with the clinic — the one who treats the wounded.
Hayashi Fumiko keeps the bodies the bureaus never see. If you are going to walk into the dark, start by knowing where you can crawl back to.
Start with the café — the one who reads you on sight.
Matsuda Keiko knew what you were before you sat down, the report said. Begin with the one who can tell you what you have become without your having to say it.
The first node opens, and the city tilts a degree toward you

The first door you knock on does not open to a stranger; it opens to Gin's brother, which is a different and more frightening thing. You watch the recognition cross their face — the small flinch, the recalculation, the careful arrangement of features into something that will not give too much away — and you understand that you have walked into a story already in progress, one in which you are the only character who does not know the plot.

They tell you a piece of it. Not all of it; none of them will ever tell you all of it, because Gin trained them not to, and grief makes people loyal to the rules of the dead. But a piece: that in his last weeks your brother was wrong, the way a room is wrong after something has stood in it; that he was carrying something carefully, quietly, the way you carry a thing you have decided to die for rather than set down; and that whatever it was, he was certain — they all say this, each in their own words, and it is the part that frightens you most — that he was protecting you.

The choice it leaves you
Ask what he was protecting you from.
The question under all the other questions. They may not answer. The answer, when it comes, is not a what. It is a who, and it is wearing a face you know.
Ask what he was carrying.
Not who, but what. The Thirteenth Seat is a phrase you do not have yet, and they will not give it to you whole — but they will give you the shape of the hole it left.
Chapter Three — The Last Week written
A briefing he skipped, in a building that does not forget

The nodes give you a week — Gin's last ordinary week, reconstructed out of the things he did that were not ordinary. He skipped a Directorate briefing on the Thursday, the first he had missed in nine years, and a man like Gin does not skip the thing he has done nine hundred times unless the thing he is doing instead is larger. He closed a case on the Friday, a drowning out by the Arakawa, and closed it too fast and too clean, the file signed off in a hand that the clinic's blue-curtain woman, who has seen a great deal of Gin's handwriting across a great many discharge forms, says was already beginning to change.

And on the Saturday he went to an address that is in no file, no registry, no map the Directorate keeps — a place his own Archive notes only as the low place, where the water remembers — and after the Saturday the entries stop being entries and start being arguments. Gin arguing with himself, on paper, in a hand that tightens with each line, about whether a debt that was never his to pay might nonetheless be the only honest thing left to do.

The choice it leaves you
Trace the drowning he closed too fast.
The case is a thread Yushiro is holding. Pull it and you learn how Gin's conscience was turned into a leash — one reasonable mercy at a time.
Find 'the low place where the water remembers.'
The Saturday address. This is the riverbank at Obon, the seam where the drowned come home — and where something offered Gin a way to give them their due.
What a top-grade of Duty does to a good man

You begin, slowly, against your will, to understand the shape of it — and understanding is worse than not, because it forecloses the comfortable lie that your brother was simply taken. He was not taken. He was persuaded, and he is being persuaded still, and the cruelty of the thing wearing him is that it never once asked him to want something selfish. It asked him to do what was right. It found the vacant place in the order of the world where an old obligation had gone unmet, and it framed every step toward catastrophe as a duty already owed, until the line between what Gin wanted and what it wanted stopped being a line and became a rhyme.

He is still in there. That is the part you will carry whichever way this ends. Not a cage to be broken open, not a man replaced by a monster wearing his coat, but a person being slowly out-argued by something that sounds exactly like his own conscience, and winning the argument the way the tide wins — not by force, but by patience, by being there every hour, by never raising its voice.

The choice it leaves you
Read the Archive entries after the 14th anyway.
He told you not to trust the handwriting after the 14th. But the only way to learn to tell his voice from the other is to read where they have started to blur. It will cost your Stability.
Stop, and find a way to reach him directly first.
Not the paper. The man. There are lucid windows, the nodes say, and they are shrinking. Reach one before you let the after-the-14th pages into your head.
Interlude — The Door That Locks Itself written
The handyman, on the cold spot by the genkan

Iwata has fixed forty-one doors in this building over nineteen years and he tells you so before he tells you anything else, because he wants it understood, up front, that he is not a man who believes in things. He kneels in the genkan of Gin's flat with his toolbox open and his reading glasses pushed down his nose, and he is talking the whole time the way men talk when the talking is a wall they are putting up against the quiet. The August heat is sitting on the whole city like a wet hand — you can feel it through the open window, the cicadas grinding it out — but here, in the meter of floor between the door and the step up into the room, the air is wrong. Not cold like a draught. Cold like a held breath. Cold the way the inside of a well is cold in summer, a coldness that has been kept rather than made.

It started after Obon began, he says, not looking at you, working a screwdriver he doesn't need into a screw that doesn't need it. The tenant downstairs said the ceiling sweated. I came up to check the seal on the window. There's no leak. There's nothing. But you stand here — he taps the threshold with the screwdriver, twice, on the exact line where the cold begins — and you'll feel the building think you're a stranger. He laughs at his own phrase and the laugh comes out thin. You are standing in your brother's home, in the gap his coat used to hang in, and the cold is reading you, finding you in the dark and deciding what you are, and you understand with a flatness that is worse than fear that Iwata has described it exactly.

You ask him, carefully, when he last saw Gin. He thinks about it longer than the question should need. That's the thing, he says. I saw him on the Sunday. Said good morning on the stairs. Polite man, your brother, always was. He sets the screwdriver down. But the missus in 3C, she swears she saw him Tuesday, by the door, fitting his key. And I checked the log. He hasn't badged the front entrance since the Saturday before. So one of us is wrong about a man we both know on sight. He says it like a small accounting error. He does not yet hear what he has said: that the building has been admitting someone who looks like Gin, on a day Gin was not here, through a door that has begun to keep its own counsel.

The choice it leaves you
Ask Iwata to stand on the cold line and describe exactly what he feels.
He'll do it because you asked, and he'll go pale, and you'll have made an unsuperstitious man feel the seam in his own home. Some kindnesses you can't take back.
Press him on the Tuesday sighting — get the neighbour's exact words.
The figure at the door wore Gin's face and fitted Gin's key. Confirming it makes the thing wearing your brother specific, locatable, real — and means it has been practising.
Say nothing; let him fill the silence.
Men building a wall against the quiet will hand you the brick that frightens them most, if you don't interrupt. But you'll have used his fear as a tool, and you'll know it.
The key that no longer fits

He shows you the lock because the lock is the reason he was called, and a lock is something he can hold. Gin's key — your brother's key, the one from the envelope the Directorate released to you with the rest of his unremarkable effects — slides halfway into the cylinder and stops. Iwata takes it from you and tries it himself, gently, then less gently, his craftsman's patience curdling into the small affront a tradesman feels when a thing he understands refuses to behave. This is his key, he says. I cut the spare myself, three years ago. It fit yesterday. I oiled it yesterday. He withdraws it, examines the teeth against the light, finds nothing wrong with the teeth. The fault is not in the key. The fault is in the lock, which has, sometime between yesterday and now, quietly become a lock for a different door.

Then he shows you the other thing, the thing he didn't want to call about and called about anyway. He pulls the door shut from the inside and lets go. And the deadbolt turns. By itself — no hand on it, no draught strong enough, the mechanism rotating its full quarter-turn with the heavy, satisfied chunk of a bolt seating home. The flat has locked you both in. Iwata stares at the brass knob as though it has spoken to him in a language he half-remembers from childhood, and for a long moment neither of you reaches for it, because reaching for it means admitting that something in the room wanted the door shut and got its wish. It does that now, he says, very quietly, the wall finally down. Three times since Monday. It locks itself. Like it's keeping something in. Or, — and he can't stop the next part, it comes out of him sideways — like it forgot which side the inside is on.

You turn the bolt back. It comes easily, oiled, ordinary, a thing a man built and a man can undo. But you have felt it now, the small autonomy in the room, the sense that the flat is no longer a container for your brother's absence but a participant in it — that the cold by the genkan and the changed lock and the bolt that seats itself are not three faults but one fact, stated three ways, by a space that has learned to testify. Gin taught you, years ago, in the only register of his you ever fully trusted, that a place holds the shape of who lives there the way a glove holds a hand. This place is holding a shape. It is just no longer your brother's.

The choice it leaves you
Take the lock apart yourself, look for what changed.
You won't find a mechanism. You'll find that the cylinder's pins have reseated to a pattern that matches no key you own — the lock retuned to admit a tenancy that isn't his. Knowing the shape of the new key tells you who the door now answers to.
Let the door lock itself again and watch it happen.
Watching means standing in the cold line while the bolt turns, offering yourself to whatever decides the door. You'll learn whether it's keeping something in — or measuring you for the inside.
Get Iwata out and seal the flat. Don't let it practise on him.
Protecting him is right. It also means you stop having a witness, and you walk away from the one room in Tokyo that is willing to tell you the truth, because the truth is costing an innocent man his sleep.
What changes tenancy, and what stays to attest it

Iwata packs his toolbox the way a man folds a flag, slowly, with a ceremony he'd deny. At the door — which he now opens with his own master key, because Gin's no longer works and he will not say so out loud again — he stops and gives you the thing he has been circling for an hour. My grandmother, he says, kept the house in Niigata. She used to say a house knows when the owner's debt comes due. Said the cold spot was the house making room. He shakes his head at himself, at the disgrace of repeating it, a man who fixes things repeating a thing that cannot be fixed. Making room for what, I asked her. She never said. I think she didn't like the answer either. Then he is gone down the stairs, leaving you in the doorway of your brother's flat at the high heat of Obon, when the line between the living shore and the other is thinnest, and the dead come back across the water, and the lock has been recut for whatever is crossing.

You stand on the warm side of the cold line for a while. You think about the entries in the Archive that stopped being entries after the 14th. You think about the case Gin closed too fast, the drowning, the address in no file. You think about a debt that was never his to pay and the brother who would pay it anyway, in the one currency he had left, which was his own residency in his own life — and you understand, standing here, that this is what it looks like from the outside: not a vanishing, not a violence, nothing the Directorate has a form for. A change of tenancy. The slow administrative handover of a man to whatever he agreed to let in, witnessed only by a handyman who doesn't believe in things and a brother who is learning, room by room, to read the handwriting before he can bear to name it.

You take the key — Gin's useless key, the one that fits nothing now — and you put it in your pocket anyway, because it is the shape of a door he could once open, and because you are not ready to be a man who throws away the last key to his brother. Behind you, as you pull the door to from the warm side, the deadbolt turns. You do not reach for it. You let the flat keep what it is keeping, and you go down the stairs into the heat, and the cicadas take you back, and you carry the cold out into August in the small of your back, where it will sit for days, reading you, deciding what you are.

The choice it leaves you
Pursue the grandmother's phrase: a house making room. Find what that costs the one who lives there.
It's not folklore. It's the membrane logic stated in a kitchen idiom — and following it means accepting that Gin's flat is rehearsing, in miniature, what Yushiro means to do to the whole Kita ward.
Take the dead key to someone who reads locks the way Gin read bodies.
There is a man on the Grid who can cut a key from a refusal. He'll tell you what door yours now opens. He'll also want to know why you'd want to walk through it.
Go back to the Archive. The flat told you what; the nodes will tell you when.
Returning means reading in Gin's demanded order again, and his order is a leash too — the deeper you go in his sequence, the more you think the way the thing wearing him wants you to think.
Interlude — The Briefing They Kept Quiet written
Counter-Demon Verification Directorate, intake desk, 09:14

The CVD intake hall is the coldest warm room in Tokyo. They keep it at exactly the temperature that lets a salaryman keep his jacket on and a frightened person keep their hands from shaking, and the effect, after four minutes in one of the bolted plastic chairs, is that you stop being able to tell which one you are. The clerk behind the glass has a queue ticket dispenser, a stamp, and a face arranged into the specific blankness of a person who has decided in advance that whatever you have come to report is already accounted for. You slide your brother's name across the steel tray under the window and watch her type it, and you watch the small light of recognition that should cross any face when a name lands on a screen, and it does not come. Kanzaki Gin, she reads back, flat, and turns the monitor a careful two degrees away from you.

You tell her he has been gone since the Saturday before Obon. You tell her about the door that locks itself and the neighbour who saw a man wearing his face fit a key on a day he never badged the building, and you hear your own voice doing the thing voices do in rooms like this, going reasonable, going small, sanding the edges off the impossible so the impossible will fit through the slot under the glass. She listens the way water listens. When you finish she does not write anything down. I'm showing him as current, she says. Status active. Last verification — she leans toward the screen and you lean with her, both of you reading a date, and the date is three days ago. Three days ago Gin's flat was a held breath and an empty coat hook. Three days ago someone in this building looked at your brother's file and certified that he was, in the language of the form, present and accounted for.

You ask the only question that matters, which is who. The clerk's hand finds the stamp without her looking at it, the way a hand finds a railing on a stair it has climbed a thousand times. Verifications are system-generated, she says, and the sentence has the worn smoothness of something repeated until it stopped meaning anything, a stone in a river of other stones. If you'd like to file a discrepancy, take a ticket. The dispenser ticks. A paper tongue extends toward you with a number on it, and you understand, holding it, that you have just been handed your place in a line designed to be longer than your patience, by an institution that has already decided your brother is not missing, because someone took the trouble to make sure the form would say so.

The choice it leaves you
Take the discrepancy ticket and wait the line out, however long it runs.
Patience is a confession that you'll play by their clock. The line is engineered to outlast grief; the people who designed it are counting on you leaving before the truth comes due — and while you sit, the thing wearing Gin's face keeps badging doors.
Refuse the ticket; ask, by name, for the officer who signed the last verification.
Naming the signer makes you a person who reads the small print, and small-print readers get remembered. You'll learn whether a human signed it — or whether something is signing in a human's place, and has been for longer than three days.
Say nothing, photograph the date on the screen before she turns it away.
Evidence you weren't given is evidence someone will want back. The timestamp proves a living certification of a vanished man — but holding it makes you a thief of the one fact the Directorate was built to keep filed and quiet.
The liaison who agrees with everything

They send you, eventually, to a man named Sekine, whose office is a glass box on the fourth floor with a view of the air-handling units and a plant that is doing the slow, patient work of dying in a corner. Sekine is kind in the way that costs nothing — he stands when you enter, he offers you the good chair, he uses your brother's given name as if they had been at school together — and within ninety seconds you understand that his kindness is the interface, the soft cladding the building wears so that its hardness never has to touch you directly. He agrees with everything. He agrees that the situation is concerning. He agrees that the verification looks, on its face, irregular. He agrees, with a small sorrowful tilt of the head, that he can find no record of who authorised it, and he agrees so completely and so soon that you realise agreement is how this office says no.

You ask him to mark Gin missing — to change the one field that would turn the whole machine the other way, that would put your brother's face on a board instead of a verification on a server. Sekine's hands come together on the desk, very gently, two halves of a closing thing. Here is the difficulty, he says, and the difficulty, when he lays it out, is exquisite: to file a missing-person flag on a CVD-registered officer requires the counter-signature of his supervising desk, and Gin's supervising desk has, as of the last roster, no supervisor listed — the chair sits vacant, a small administrative weather system, pending an appointment that keeps not happening. So you see, Sekine says, almost apologetic, there is no one with the standing to declare him gone. The sentence lands in the cold warm room and sits there. A man has been erased not by force but by procedure, by the careful removal of every official who would have the authority to notice.

You watch Sekine's face for the lie and you don't find one, and that is the worst of it — he is not lying, he is reciting, he is a decent man reading you the rules of a game whose rules were written, recently and quietly, by someone who wanted exactly this room to produce exactly this answer. When you ask who left the supervising chair empty, Sekine glances, just once, at the dying plant, as if it might know, and tells you the previous supervisor took a personal leave in late July. For health, he says. He'd been — Sekine searches for the word a kind man uses for a colleague who came apart — helped, I think, by someone. A consultation. He spoke very warmly of it. The word helped goes into you like cold water down a wall, because you have heard it before, in your brother's flat, in the cadence of a thing that does favours, and you know now that the briefing they kept quiet was never a briefing at all. It was a clearing of the room.

The choice it leaves you
Press Sekine for the former supervisor's name and where his 'consultation' came from.
Follow the word 'helped' and you walk straight up the chain of favours toward Yushiro's hand. But you'll be asking a kind man to name the thing that hollowed his colleague, and kind men who name it tend to be next.
Accept that the chair is the lock; ask how a new supervisor gets appointed — and by whom.
Whoever fills the vacant desk controls whether Gin is ever declared gone. Learning the appointment path tells you who benefits from the silence — and may reveal that the vacancy itself is the plan, a small rehearsal for a larger empty seat.
Thank Sekine, leave, and stop trusting the building entirely.
Walking out intact protects you from a system that administers absence for a living. But you surrender the only channel that could make Gin official, and you go back to facing the thing alone, with no one above you who will write his name on any board.
The corridor, and the badge that still works

You leave through the same turnstile you entered by, and on instinct — the bad, useful instinct of a person who has stopped believing the official story — you take out the visitor's lanyard they clipped to you at the door and look at it properly for the first time. It is not a visitor's pass. The clerk, in the long blank ritual of intake, made an error or was made to make one, and the card in your hand carries a low-grade access tier under a name that is not yours: it is a temporary credential issued, the small print says, on behalf of the supervising desk, K. Gin. For one impossible second you read it as a message from your brother, a hand reaching out of the machine, and then the colder reading arrives. The vacant chair is not empty. Something is sitting in it long enough to issue credentials, quietly, in your brother's authority, and it has just — by accident or by design — handed you the keys to the building that says he isn't missing.

The corridor outside intake runs long and fluorescent toward the lifts, and the cold that lives in Gin's flat is here too, you realise, thinner, dilute, a single thread of it laid along the floor like a tripwire of held breath. You can feel where it leads. It does not lead toward the exit. It leads deeper, toward the archive floors, toward the locked registry where the verification was born, and you stand at the seam between the way out and the way in with a credential in your fist that should not exist, issued by an authority that should be a dead man, warm under the lights of an institution that has decided, on excellent paper, that nothing is wrong.

Somewhere below you a printer is running. You can hear it through the floor, the soft mechanical patience of a document filing itself, certifying again, on schedule, that Kanzaki Gin is present and accounted for — a small machine keeping a large lie current while the city outside grinds out cicadas and lights its lanterns for the dead it is willing to admit are gone. You think of the word helped. You think of an empty chair that issues keys. And you understand, with the flat clarity that has replaced fear in you over these three days, that to find your brother you will have to walk in the one direction the building was built to prevent, carrying a borrowed authority that is really a borrowed wound.

The choice it leaves you
Use the credential now, while it works, and go down toward the registry.
The card is an accident no one has caught yet, and accidents get caught. Going now buys you the registry before someone reconciles the error — but you'd be walking into the system's hardest room wearing the authority of the thing you're hunting.
Pocket the card, leave, and come back when you understand what tier it really opens.
Caution keeps you alive past today, but the credential is live and logged; every hour it sits in your pocket is an hour the vacant chair has to notice it gave you a key, and to decide what a borrowed wound is worth taking back.
Photograph the card and report the error yourself, on the record.
Confessing the accident makes you the honest one — and erases your only way in. The instant the credential is voided, the building reseals, and the printer downstairs keeps certifying your brother alive to a system that will no longer let you near the floor where the lie is made.
Interlude — The Form That Files Itself written
Registry sub-level, the room where absence is kept

The registry floor is two levels below the dying plant and the kind man, past a door that reads your borrowed card and decides, after a pause long enough to make your scalp prickle, that you are allowed. Inside, the cold is no longer a thread; it is the weather of the room. Rows of grey cabinets stand in the half-light like a congregation that has been waiting a long time, and the only sound is the printer you heard through the floor, a single machine at the end of the central aisle, exhaling a page every few minutes into a tray already thick with its own patience. You walk toward it past drawers labelled in a hand that is too even to be a hand, and you understand that this is not a place where records are stored. It is a place where records are kept alive — fed, certified, renewed — so that the people they describe can be quietly held in the condition the building prefers them in.

The page in the tray is your brother. KANZAKI GIN — STATUS: ACTIVE — VERIFICATION: AUTOMATIC — NEXT: 06:00. Below it, the page beneath it, the same. Below that, the same, the same, a stack of identical certifications climbing back through the days like sediment, each one a small official act of looking at an empty chair and writing present. You lift the stack and the dates run backward under your thumb — three days, a week, two — and then the pattern breaks. The oldest pages in the tray are different. They are not automatic. They were signed, once, by a hand, before the machine took over, and the hand that started the lie wrote a single initialled note in the margin of the first one, a note no automatic system would generate, in ink that has gone the brown of old blood: per consultation — Y. He asked so little.

You stand in the cold congregation with the proof in your hands and you feel the shape of it close around you the way the cold closes around a stranger at Gin's threshold. Someone began this by hand. Someone sat at a desk in late July and made the first false certification himself, willingly, gratefully, because a consultation had asked so little of him and given, you can only assume, something he wanted very much. And then he taught the machine to do the rest, set absence to renew itself at six every morning, and walked away into a personal leave for his health — and the form has been filing itself ever since, a wound that has learned to bleed on schedule, keeping your brother present in the only ledger that could ever declare him gone.

The choice it leaves you
Take the signed originals — the hand-written pages — and leave the automatic ones.
The signed pages name a human author and the favour that bought him. They're the thread back to Yushiro. But they're also the only physical proof, and the room will know they're missing; you'd be pulling the first stone from a wall built to fall on whoever pulls it.
Stop the printer — break the six-o'clock renewal and let the status lapse.
Lapsing the certification could finally turn Gin from 'active' to a gap the system has to explain. It could also trip whatever watches this room, and a status that lapses is a status someone is alerted to defend — you don't yet know what arrives when the lie stops being current.
Photograph everything, touch nothing, and read the room for what else it keeps alive.
Other cabinets, other congregations — how many people are being kept 'present' down here? Knowing the scale tells you this was never about Gin alone. But the longer you read, the longer the borrowed card sits logged in a room it was never meant to open.
The other drawers

You should leave. You photograph the signed pages and you should turn for the door, and instead the cold pulls you sideways, down a row you had no reason to enter, the way grief pulls a hand toward a drawer it shouldn't open. The cabinets here are older, and the labels are names, and the names have small coloured tabs, and after the third one you understand the colours. Green is active. Green is the lie. There are more green tabs in this row than a building should need, a whole quiet harvest of people the registry is certifying present, each with a tray, each with a printer somewhere exhaling them alive at six in the morning, and beside each of the oldest, in the same brown ink, the same initialled note: per consultation — Y. The Healer has been busy. The Healer has been busy for longer than your brother's three days, asking so little of so many, and filing the favour in a room two floors below the kindest man you ever met.

One drawer is open already. You did not open it. It hangs out an inch into the aisle as if recently consulted, and the file inside is not green and not anyone's name you know — it is a registry of the registry, an index, and the entry the index is open to is not a person. It is a seat. DOMAIN XIII — STATUS: VACANT — VERIFICATION: PENDING APPOINTMENT. The same language, the same machine-even hand, applied not to a man but to a chair in some other ledger entirely, a seat among the Thirteen that the building is keeping open the way it keeps your brother active — by writing the desired condition down every morning until reality is too embarrassed to disagree. You understand, looking at it, that you have wandered out of your brother's disappearance and into the edge of something that uses the same pen. Whatever is thinning the wall is also a clerk. It does its largest work on the smallest paper.

The cold sharpens, the way a held breath sharpens just before it's let go, and you become aware that the open drawer was not left open for the room. It was left open for you — that the borrowed credential which should not exist was an error that walked you down two floors and one row to a drawer that hangs into the aisle at exactly the height of your eye, and that somewhere a kind, busy thing is watching what you do with a vacancy it has gone to the trouble of showing you. The favour has found you. It has not yet asked anything. It is only, for now, letting you read — letting you feel the seam between a brother kept present and a seat kept empty, and trusting, the way it trusts everyone, that once you have seen how little it asks, you will want very much to be helped.

The choice it leaves you
Close the drawer, take nothing more, and get out before it asks.
Refusing the bait is the one move it may not expect. But the drawer was opened for you, which means leaving it changes nothing it planned; you exit having seen the vacant Seat without understanding the price of the chair, carrying a fear you can't yet file under any name but 'Y.'
Read the Domain XIII index fully — learn what 'pending appointment' is waiting for.
The vacant Seat is the engine under everything; reading it now is reading the plan a book early. But the index is open because something wants you to read it, and the things it teaches you here, you will be expected to have learned — knowledge offered this freely is never free.
Leave a mark of your own in the drawer — write a single word on the vacancy and let it be found.
Answering the watcher tells it you understand you're being courted, and refuses the script. It also ends your anonymity for good: from the moment it reads your hand in its own ledger, the favour stops being offered to a grieving stranger and starts being negotiated with a known opponent.
Street level, the lanterns, 11:40

You come up out of the building into the full weight of the August morning and the city does not know. That is the thing that nearly takes your legs out on the steps of the Directorate — that Tokyo is going about Obon, that the lantern strings are up between the lampposts for the dead who are allowed to come home, that a woman is arranging chrysanthemums in a bucket outside a convenience store and a child is being walked to a pool with a towel around his neck, and that two floors below all of it a machine is certifying your brother alive on a schedule, and none of these warm, ordinary, breathing people will ever take a ticket in that line. You have the photographs in your phone and a borrowed wound on a lanyard you have not given back, and you stand in the heat shivering, a stranger to the cold now wherever you carry it.

The cicadas grind. Somewhere a temple bell does its slow work over the rooftops, the sound that is supposed to carry to the other side, and you think about which side your brother is on — not dead, the registry insists he is not dead, only active, only present, only renewed at six each morning by a form that learned to file itself the moment a tired official decided a favour had asked so little of him. You think about Sekine's dying plant and the chair with no one in it. You think about a row of green tabs and a drawer that opened itself to a vacant Seat. And you understand that the briefing they kept quiet was the truest document in the building: not a thing said, but a thing carefully not said, an absence administered so smoothly that the only way to read it was to become the kind of person who reads absences — which, over three days in your brother's cold home, you have.

You take out the lanyard one more time, there on the steps, in the lantern light and the heat, and you look at the name that is not yours under the authority of a man who is not gone. You can give it back and walk into the ordinary morning and let the form file itself forever. Or you can keep the wound, and walk back in tomorrow before six, and be standing at the printer when it tries to renew him — there to see, finally, whether absence administered can be absence revoked, or whether the only thing your borrowed key really opens is the next room in a trap that has been certifying you, too, as present and accounted for, since the moment you took a ticket.

The choice it leaves you
Keep the lanyard; be at the printer before 06:00 to break the renewal in person.
Standing over the machine when it tries to keep Gin alive is the closest you'll get to catching the lie in the act. It's also the appointment the room has been steering you toward — arrive on schedule and you may find something has been kept active down there to meet you.
Give the lanyard back at the desk and pursue Gin entirely outside the system.
Severing from the building denies the favour its grip and keeps you off its ledgers. But you abandon the one place that holds proof, and you face a thing that works in paper with no paper of your own — fighting a clerk by refusing to read.
Take the photographs to someone outside the CVD who can't be 'helped' into silence.
An outside witness breaks the building's monopoly on the truth of Gin's absence. But everyone you'd trust is someone the Healer can reach with a consultation that asks so little — and bringing them in may simply be handing it the next willing hand to sign the next quiet form.
Book Two

The Handwriting

Learning to tell your brother's voice from the one that has learned to sound like him.

Chapter Four — The Lucid Window written
A number you know, ringing out into the dark

You call him. Of course you call him; you have been calling him in your head since the empty hook, and now there is a real number under your thumb and you press it and it rings, and rings, into the particular silence of a phone held by someone who is deciding whether to be the person who answers it. Nineteen times across as many hours you do this, and nineteen times the line stays dead, and you tell yourself the silence is just silence and not a choice, because the alternative is that something on Gin's end is letting the messages stack up like stones on a grave and choosing the hour it will speak.

On the twentieth, it picks up. There is no hello. There is breathing, a beat too even, and then a voice that is your brother's voice the way a photograph is a face — every feature correct and nothing alive behind it — saying your name as though reading it off a card it was handed. Rei. And under the wrongness, for one sentence, before the evenness closes back over it like water, something that is unmistakably him, frightened and tender and trying: Go home. This isn't a thing you walk toward and walk back from the same.

The choice it leaves you
"Who is this. Put my brother on."
Confront the thing directly. It will not admit to being a thing. It will be reasonable, and concerned, and it will sound exactly like a man who only wants you safe.
"Gin. It's me. Stay with me one more sentence."
Reach past the evenness for the man under it. Every lucid second you can hold him in costs the thing wearing him effort — and teaches you the seam between the two voices.
The two voices, learning to rhyme

This is the work of Book Two, and it is not a fight; it is a listening. The thing in your brother does not roar. It reasons. It tells you, in Gin's cadence, that the dead of 2011 were never given their due, that the membrane over Kita-ku is a wound that someone with the right grade and the right grief could finally close, that what looks like catastrophe to a frightened intern looks, from a higher seat, like an obligation met at last. And the horror of it is how nearly you agree — how the argument is built not out of lies but out of true things stacked at a murderous angle.

You learn to tell them apart the only way anyone ever does: by what each voice wants you to do. Gin, in his shrinking windows, wants you to stop, to go home, to live. The other wants you closer — wants you to understand, to help, to carry a corner of the weight, because a thing that means to close a seat needs hands it can call its own and a brother's love is the strongest leash it has ever been offered. Whichever you learn to hear first decides, in the end, whether Gin can be recovered, surrendered, or only mourned.

The choice it leaves you
Map who Gin has 'helped' since the 14th.
Each reasonable favour is a thread the thing is holding. Follow them and you find the recruitment — and the cult's counter-demon, which wants not a seat at the table but to overturn it.
Take what you've learned to a faction that can act.
You cannot do this alone, and the thing knows it. Choosing whose help to take is choosing which ending stays open — this is where the roads and the spine become one.
Chapter Eleven — The Entries After the 14th written

Gin kept a journal. Reading it in the order he demanded teaches you where his voice ends and the other begins to rhyme — and what it costs your own Stability to hear the seam.

Gin's flat, the third drawer, the book he never showed you

The journal is where he said it would be, in the voicemail that was almost him — third drawer, under the spare charger, a cheap kraft-bound notebook with the price sticker still curling off the back. You sit on the floor of his flat because the chairs feel like they belong to someone who is coming back and you cannot bear to presume, and outside the window the Obon lanterns are going up along the canal, small paper boxes of light meant to guide the dead home and not, you think, meant for whatever is currently walking around in your brother. You open to the first page and the hand is Gin's — the cramped, leftward, apologetic hand he has had since he was nine, the one that always looked like it was trying to take up less room than it was given.

You read forward. The early entries are a tired man's entries: a closed case that closed too easily, a name in no file, the membrane over Kita-ku reading thin on instruments that should not have been able to read it at all. He is frightened in them but it is an ordinary fright, the fright of a person who suspects he is right about something he would give anything to be wrong about. And then you reach the entry dated the fourteenth of August, which is three lines long and ends mid-sentence, and the next page is dated the same night, an hour later, and the handwriting has changed. Not wildly. That is the part that gets into you and stays. It has only become a little more even. A little more upright. As though someone studied his hand for an afternoon and then, being a careful student, wrote it back to you slightly improved.

You sit with that for a long time, the lantern light moving on the wall, and you understand that you are looking at the exact hour. Not a metaphor for it, not a memory of it — the seam itself, two pages, one man on either side. Your hands are cold. You want, very badly, to close the book and decide it is grief making patterns. But you have read enough of Gin's casework to know that the impulse to un-see a thing is the impulse the thing is counting on, and so you turn the page, into the entries the new hand wrote.

The choice it leaves you
Read straight through, fast, before your nerve goes.
Speed spares you the dread of each separate page — and robs you of the small contradictions only slowness catches. You will reach the end with the shape of it and none of the proof.
Read the way the new hand asks you to: in its numbered order.
The later entries carry tiny marginal numbers, a reading order that is not the order they were written. Obeying it is the fastest way to understand him — and the surest way to start thinking in his sequence instead of yours.
The order it wants you to read in, and the place your own thoughts go

The numbers in the margins are small and untroubled, the way a librarian's are. Follow them and the journal stops being a record and becomes an argument, beautifully shelved: here is the grief of 2011 that was never given its rite, set beside the membrane that has been weeping ever since, set beside the single seat at the Thirteen's table that has stood vacant long enough for the weeping to have a remedy, if only someone with the right grade and the right wound would consent to feel the obligation. None of it is a lie. That is what you keep having to put the book down over. Each entry is a true thing, and the order stacks the true things at an angle, and somewhere around the ninth page you catch yourself nodding, and the nod is the cost.

Because this is the work now, and it is not a fight, it is a listening, and listening leaves marks. You learn to feel the seam by the temperature of the wanting underneath each line. Gin's entries — the ones in the older, leftward hand, fewer every page — want you to stop. They are clumsy and they repeat themselves and they say go home in four different broken ways, and they are unmistakably the writing of a man trying to shout through a wall with a hand that is being held. The other entries are calmer, and they want you closer. They want you to understand. They reason with you the way you reason with a frightened animal you intend to keep, and the worst of it is how good the reasoning is, how nearly you would sign your name under it on a clearer night.

You notice, an hour in, that you have begun to think in his cadence — that when you reach for your own objection it arrives already softened, already shelved in his order, already wearing the little marginal number it would have if he had written it. This is what your training would call the Stability cost and what your body calls a kind of seasickness, the floor of your own mind tilting a few degrees toward a frame that is not yours. You can keep reading and let the tilt deepen, because the tilt is also the only doorway to understanding the thing well enough to fight it. Or you can stop while the floor is still nearly level and accept that you will know less.

The choice it leaves you
Keep reading; let your thoughts tilt toward his order.
Understanding is downhill from here, and so is everything else. Each page you grant it costs Stability you may want later, in a room where you cannot afford to be even slightly persuaded.
Close the book and write your own counter-order from memory.
Re-sequencing his argument into your own frame is the only known antidote — slow, exhausting, and it leaves gaps where his best true things were. You stay yourself, and you stay partly ignorant.
Read only the older, leftward entries — only Gin.
Reading just the man and skipping the other spares your Stability and breaks your heart in a cleaner direction. You will hear him clearly and learn almost nothing about the thing holding his hand.
The tell, near the back, where the warmth points the wrong way

It is not a horror that gives it away in the end. It is a kindness. Near the back, in the even upright hand, there is an entry about you — about Rei — and it is warm. It remembers the summer you broke your wrist and how he carried your bag for a month. It uses the small name only he uses. And you are reading it with your throat tight, almost grateful, when the seasickness saves you, because you have read this exact warmth in his casework, in the section he wrote about telling a worn host from a whole one. The tell, he wrote, in his own clumsy leftward hand forty pages earlier, is never the warmth itself. It is the direction the warmth points. A demon keeps the host's whole register of affection and orients all of it toward whoever is useful now, rather than toward whoever the host actually loved. Read it backward and the entry about your broken wrist is not love remembering you. It is love being aimed.

You understand, then, what class of feeling you have been swimming in for three hours, and you understand why it works on you specifically: it is Gin's warmth, accurate to the gram, pointed at you because you are the hands it wants. The older entries never aim. They blunder toward you, repetitive and useless, asking nothing, wanting only that you live. That is how you will tell them apart from here on, in every text and every lucid window and every reasonable favour still to come — not by the wrongness, which it can sand away, but by the direction of the wanting. Gin wants you safe and gone. The Healer wants you near and helpful. The voice that wants something from you is never the one that loves you, however perfectly it has learned to sound like it does.

You close the journal. The lantern light is all the way up the wall now, and across the canal the paper boxes are drifting out onto the black water, each one a small consent to let the dead be guided home. You are not going to get to do that for Gin tonight, or maybe ever, and the cost of the last three hours is sitting in you like cold water at the base of the spine — you can feel, distinctly, the few degrees your own floor has not quite returned to level. But you know the seam now. You can hear it. And knowing where a thing ends and your brother begins is the first tool you have been handed that the Healer cannot take back by being reasonable.

The choice it leaves you
Keep the journal. Carry the proof, and the tilt that came with it.
Evidence you can show a faction, in a hand no forger could fake — and a book that goes on quietly arguing in your bag, in his order, every time you open it.
Photograph the seam pages and burn the rest at the canal.
You keep the two pages that prove the hour and let the lanterns take the argument. You lose the warmth that was aimed at you — and the older entries, the real Gin, burn with it.
Take it to someone who reads bodies, not just books.
A journal proves a seam in the hand; it cannot say what is wearing him. There is a reader who works from the body itself — and going to them means deciding to be seen looking.
Chapter Twelve — The Texts written

You text your brother and someone answers. The replies are warm, and prompt, and wrong in a way you can only feel as a delay too even to be his — Gin who never once in his life let a message sit, now answering like a man translating from a language he learned this week.

The train platform, Obon eve, your thumb over a name you have texted ten thousand times

You compose it on the Yamanote platform because standing still has become unbearable and the train at least pretends to be progress. where are you. just tell me you're somewhere. You delete the period, because Gin hated periods in texts, said they made everything sound like a man being let go from a job, and then you put it back, because you want him to feel the weight of it, and then you delete it again and send it bare. The three grey dots come up almost at once, and your whole body leans toward them the way it used to lean toward the door when he was late, that animal forward-tilt of the younger brother who has spent a lifetime listening for one specific key in one specific lock.

The reply is kind. That is the first thing that is wrong, and you will not let yourself know it is wrong for another hour. I'm okay, Rei. I promise I'm okay. You don't need to do all this. It is the right words and it is even the right small name, and the dots had come up fast, but the message itself arrived a beat late behind them, as though someone watched you type, began to answer, and then paused to make sure the answer was correct. Gin does not pause to make sure. Gin answers wrong and fast and then sends four more texts correcting himself, a man whose thumbs have always outrun his judgement, and you are standing on a platform reading a version of him who has, overnight, acquired the patience of a much older and much calmer person.

The Obon crowds move around you in their summer yukata, paper fans and the smell of grilled corn from the station stalls, families carrying the small lanterns home to set out for their dead. You should get on the train. You let three trains go. You text back, all this what. tell me where you are and i'll stop, and you watch the dots, and the dots think about it. They appear, and rest, and vanish, and appear again. Gin has never in the recorded history of your phone made the dots think. The thing about to answer you is choosing, and the only thing in the world it is being careful not to give you is a place.

The choice it leaves you
Ask the question only Gin could answer — bait the warmth with something private.
A trap made of love: a memory only your real brother holds. It will either prove him or prove the thing reads him perfectly — and if it answers right, you lose the one tool you had left for telling them apart.
Just keep asking for the address, plainly, until it slips.
The patient siege. It cannot give you a place without giving you the membrane it is thinning, so it will steer forever. You learn its evasions cold — and learn nothing you can stand on.
Stop texting. Watch when the replies come, not what they say.
Stop reading the words and start timing them. The metadata cannot lie the way the language can — but it means treating your brother as a signal, and some part of you will not forgive you for it.
The memory you set like a snare, and the answer that walks around it

You decide to spend something you cannot get back. You type a memory with a false edge in it — the night your mother's funeral ran long and you and Gin slipped out to the convenience store and he bought you the wrong rice ball on purpose, the salmon you hated, because making you complain was the only way he knew to make you stop crying. Except it was not salmon. It was the pickled plum, the umeboshi, and he did it three times before you laughed, and only the two of you on the planet know that it took three. You write it nearly right, with the salmon in it, and you ask: do you remember what you bought me after okaasan's. you remember how many times.

The dots come. The dots stay a long while. And then the answer is so gentle it nearly undoes you, because it does not take the bait and it does not miss the bait — it folds the bait into something warmer than the truth. I remember I kept getting it wrong until you laughed. I'd get it wrong a hundred times if it worked, Rei. I'd do anything that worked. It does not say salmon. It does not say umeboshi. It does not say three. It says the shape of the thing, the love-shape, the part that would survive any version of the facts, and it points that shape directly at you, at the hands it wants, with a precision your actual fumbling brother has never once managed under grief. You sit down on the platform bench. A station attendant glances at you and decides you are only drunk.

This is the lesson Kurosawa's casework tried to teach you and your body is only now learning in the muscle: the thing wearing Gin keeps the whole register of his affection and has lost only the inventory of facts that would pin it to a single remembered night. It cannot be cornered by content because it will always answer with feeling, and the feeling is real, is genuinely Gin's, is the worst weapon ever turned on you precisely because it is not a forgery. You wanted it to fail the test. Instead it passed the test by refusing to take it, and somewhere under your sternum the floor of you tips the few degrees you have come to recognise as the cost of being understood by something that is studying you.

The choice it leaves you
Send the true detail back — say 'three times, umeboshi' — and watch it absorb that too.
Hand it the fact you withheld and you complete its file on Gin's grief. It will thank you, perfectly. You will have taught the thing the last thing it didn't know about how to be your brother.
Don't correct it. Let it keep the gap — keep one fact it can't have.
Hold the umeboshi and the number three back like a coin under your tongue. It is the only thing left that is yours and Gin's and not the Healer's — and keeping it means never again texting him freely, in case you spend it by accident.
The line it will not stop repeating, and the thing it is steering you away from

Over the next hour, between the trains you are still not boarding, you notice it has a refrain, and refrains are how you find the load-bearing wall of any argument. Four times, in four different costumes of warmth, it tells you to stop. You don't need to do all this. Let it go cold, Rei. Some cases close themselves. You'll make yourself ill. Each one is plausible as concern and each one is, you realise when you lay them side by side in the thread, aimed at the same single behaviour: looking. Not your safety. Not your sleep. Your looking. It does not mind if you are frightened, only if you are searching, and the difference between those two is the whole map of what it is protecting.

Because to give you a place — a station exit, a ward, a name on a buzzer — is to give you the membrane over Kita-ku where it is doing its slow thinning, the wound in the world it is widening one reasonable favour at a time so that the vacant Thirteenth Seat will have something it can be filled for. You understand, watching the refrain repeat, that you have been talking not to a liar but to a gatekeeper, and that the gate it keeps is geographic. The warmth is sincere. The steering is the job. Both can be true in one set of thumbs, and they are, and that is the new and terrible fluency you are acquiring: reading the love and the agenda in the same sentence, in real time, without letting either one cancel the other out.

You finally board a train, because you have to be a body going somewhere, and as the doors close you send the last text of the night: okay. i'll stop. You watch the dots come up fast — relief, real relief, you can feel it through the glass of the screen, the thing that wears him is glad — and you sit with the fact that you have just lied to your brother for the first time in your life, and that the part of him that was glad is exactly the part you most need to disappoint. Across the carriage a child holds a lit Obon lantern in two careful hands, guiding home a dead person it never met, and you ride three stops past yours just to keep moving.

The choice it leaves you
Mine the refrains — map every place it steered you away from.
Treat the evasions as a negative photograph: subtract everywhere it sent you and Kita-ku develops in the gaps. Diligent, damning, and it means re-reading his pleading as data instead of letting yourself hear it as him.
Take the thread to someone who can read what's under the words.
A transcript proves cadence and steering; it cannot name the class of thing holding the pen. There is a reader who works from the body — and bringing this means admitting out loud that the Gin on your phone is gone.
Reply once more — to Gin, not the Healer — and hope the man hears it.
Send something only the trapped brother would value, past the gatekeeper, into the dark. If the older voice is still in there it may cost him to read it — and you will never know whether it landed or only fed the thing more of you.
Chapter Twelve, continued — Where the Love Bleeds Through written

One message arrives broken — out of cadence, badly typed, in the old leftward voice — and the question is no longer whether your brother is in there but whether trusting the crack is exactly what the smooth thing wants you to do.

3:11am, a text that doesn't sound like anyone trying to sound like anything

It comes in the dead hour, when even Tokyo has the decency to dim, and it wakes you because you never turned the volume down. There is no greeting and no small name and no warmth aimed anywhere. dont come to kita dont come here its not him answering rei i can feel it making the wall thin go to itsuki burn the — and it stops there, no punctuation, the sentence severed the way the journal's fourteenth-of-August entry was severed, mid-act, a hand pulled off a page. The typos are wrong in the right way: Gin's typos, the leftward stumble of a man who never learned to thumb-type cleanly, the autocorrect refused, the panic in the missing capital. You have spent the whole night learning that the smooth voice is the enemy. And here is the broken one, and your heart goes through the floor toward it like a stone down a well.

You sit up in the dark of your flat with the phone too bright in your hand and you read it nine times. Everything in you that is brother wants to be on the first train to Kita-ku, contradicting the very text that begs you not to, because if the thing wearing him wants you away from there then the broken voice telling you to stay away must be the thing too — except it named the thinning of the wall, it confessed the plan, it pointed you at Itsuki and at burning something, and a gatekeeper does not hand you the location of its own gate. Unless. Unless the most patient version of the trap is one that lets the trapped man surface for exactly one message, because a brother who has been forbidden will run toward the forbidden place faster than any brother merely invited.

You can feel, lying to yourself in the dark, the precise shape of the hope and the precise shape of the hook occupying the same coordinates, and you cannot pull them apart by wanting to. The seasickness is back, your own floor tilting, and for once you are grateful for it, because it means you are still reading and not yet decided. Out the window the last Obon lanterns have guttered along the canal, the dead presumably guided home, the living left holding their phones. You do not text back yet. You make yourself be a person who waits sixty seconds, and you watch the screen for the smooth voice to come and clean up after the broken one.

The choice it leaves you
Believe Gin. Wait, watch, and see whether the smooth voice rushes to overwrite him.
Trust the crack and use it as a test: if the Healer scrambles to explain the message away, the man was real. Patience you may not have — and every minute you wait is a minute the wall over Kita-ku gets thinner.
Assume the trap. The forbidden place is the bait; refuse the pull.
Treat the broken voice as the cleverest costume yet and stay away from Kita on principle. Safe, sealed, self-protecting — and if it really was Gin spending everything to reach you, you just left him in the dark and called it wisdom.
Split the difference — go to Itsuki, like the text said, not to Kita.
Obey the one instruction that points away from the gate and toward your oldest friend. It honours the message without walking into the ward — but it means betting your brother's one lucid plea was about you, not about him.
What the smooth voice does next, and the tell in how fast it comes

At 3:16 the smooth voice arrives, and the speed of it tells you more than any sentence could. I'm sorry — I don't even know what I wrote, I think I fell asleep texting, I've been so tired. Ignore that, please. I'm fine. Don't go anywhere on account of a half-asleep message. It is reasonable. It is even, in its way, true — fatigue, confusion, a man not himself. And it is fast, fast in the way the earlier replies were never fast, fast like something that was caught and is now working, and the warmth in it is pointed harder than usual, more please in it, more aim. The broken text said burn and Itsuki and the wall. The smooth text would like you to file all three under sleeplessness and go back to bed. You have learned tonight whose interest each of those serves.

You understand, holding the two messages five minutes apart, that you have just watched the seam happen live — not in a journal recovered after the fact, not in handwriting studied cold, but in real time, the older voice surfacing for four broken seconds and the newer one rushing in to plaster over the gap. The Healer did not deny the plan. It could not, fast enough; the leftward voice had already said the plan out loud. So it did the next thing, the thing it is best at: it reframed the confession as a symptom and offered you the comfort of disbelieving your own brother. That is the whole technique, you see now. It does not fight your love for Gin. It enlists it, asks your love to please be reasonable, to please not make a tired man feel watched.

You put the phone face-down on the floor where you cannot see the next message arrive, and you breathe, and you let the certainty assemble itself slowly so that it is certainty and not just the wanting. Gin is in there. He surfaced. It cost him something to surface and it cost the thing something to bury him, and the burying was fast and frightened, and frightened is information. Whatever you do at dawn, you will do it knowing the man is alive enough to break a window for four seconds, and knowing the thing that holds him is, for the first time all night, working faster than it would like. The lanterns are out. The hour is the one the city forgets itself in. You have somewhere to be when it remembers.

The choice it leaves you
Screenshot both texts, timestamped, before either can be deleted.
Freeze the seam where it shows: two voices, five minutes, one phone. Proof a faction can read — and the act of saving it admits you are now building a case against the thing wearing your brother's face.
Reply to the smooth voice with a lie that buys you the dawn.
Tell it you'll rest, let it believe you soothed, so it relaxes its grip toward morning. You manage your brother's captor like a hostage negotiator — and every soothing word is one more you can never take back as the truth.
Call Itsuki now. Move while the thing is still off-balance.
The text named him; the thing is rattled; speed is the only edge you have ever been handed. Waking your oldest friend at 3am drags a Kuroda of the Tower into a thing that thins membranes — and it stops being only your grief to carry.
Chapter Thirteen — Kurosawa Reads the Body written

You carry your brother's trace down to the only man who reads the under-image, and learn that what wears Gin is not burning him out but keeping him alive — the double-layer signature, and the wrongness of being cared for.

Sub-level four, where the building keeps the bodies it would rather not believe

You go down past the floors that have names, past the floors that have only numbers, to the floor the directory lists as archival storage, climate-controlled and everyone who has been there calls the cold room. Kurosawa is waiting the way a man waits who has spent two years deciding whether he is a coward, which is to say he is already on his feet when the lift opens, already reading your face for how much you have guessed. You have brought the things that still hold Gin: the journal in its evidence sleeve, the coat he left over your chair in May and never came back for, and the cup he drank from the last morning he was your brother, which you have not washed and have told no one you kept. Kurosawa looks at the cup longest. He does not ask why you kept it. He understands keeping.

The cold room is not cold for the bodies; there are no bodies here, only their residues, the signatures that cling to objects the way smoke clings to wool. Kurosawa explains this while he lays a black cloth on the steel table and weights its corners, his voice low and even and pitched under the hum of the machines that are not, he says, switched to record. A possession leaves a wake, he tells you. On the host, on what the host loves, on the rooms they pass through slowly. I do not read the demon. No one reads the demon. I read the wake — the shape of the water after the thing has already gone under. He sets the cup in the centre of the cloth and holds his bare hands a few centimetres above it, and his breathing changes, and you watch a man go quiet in the particular way your own gift goes quiet, and you understand you are watching someone do, with discipline and a steel table, what you do by drowning.

You should not be here and you both know it. The reading Kurosawa filed on your brother eleven months ago is the reading that got redacted, the three lines and then the black bar, and the only words that survived it — double-layer, not consistent with the registered subject, recommend esca — are the words that brought you down four floors below the daylight to a man whose career ended the day he was honest in writing. If Internal Affairs walks in now, he loses the pension he has left and you lose the one person in this building who will tell you the truth without first asking what the truth is worth. So you stand very still, and you breathe shallow, and you watch his hands over the cup your brother's mouth has touched, and you wait to be told what you already cannot un-know.

The choice it leaves you
Let him read the cup — the most intimate trace, the one closest to the mouth that spoke.
The cup carries Gin at his most undefended, asleep behind the morning's first tea. It will give Kurosawa the cleanest signature he could ask for — and it means handing a stranger the last unguarded version of your brother that exists.
Give him the journal instead — trace plus the demon's own demanded handwriting.
The journal holds both the man and the method, the leftward script and the entries that turn tidy after the 14th. Richer, but it is your only documentary proof, and a reading leaves its own faint mark on what it touches.
Offer your own hand. You have been brushed by the thing more than any object has.
You carry the freshest wake of all — the seasick residue of being studied. Letting him read you tells you exactly how deep the contact went, and risks him reading the two registers you have spent your life keeping closed.
The under-image: what a possession looks like when it is not a fever

When Kurosawa finally speaks it is not with the relief of a man delivering a diagnosis but with the unease of one who has found something his training does not have a drawer for. Every possession I have ever read runs hot, he says, and his hands are still above the cup, and his eyes are somewhere past the wall. It rides the host like a fever rides a child — fast, clumsy, greedy. It burns the body to keep itself lit and in three weeks, four, the body cannot pay anymore and the thing moves on or the host dies. That is what possession is, on this table, in this building's books. A wasting. A theft that ends in an empty room. He lowers his hands at last and looks at you, and you see that he is choosing the next words because the true ones frighten him. This is not that. This is the opposite of that.

What he read in the cup, he says, is a second register laid down under the first as deliberately as a restorer lays a second coat — not consuming Gin but preserving him, regulating him, keeping his sleep clean and his hands steady and his ledgers current. The signature is cold where a fever is hot, patient where a fever is starving, and it has been resident not for the weeks a host can survive but for the better part of a year, because it has no interest in burning the body down. I have read a great many possessed, Kurosawa says, very quietly, into the hum. I have never read one being taken care of. Whatever is wearing your brother is keeping him in good repair. It does not want him dead. It does not even want him diminished. It wants him working. And you feel the floor of you tip the few degrees you have learned to recognise, because every reasonable thing your family has told themselves for a year — he's tired, he's coping, he's holding together — is now revealed as the exact description of a thing doing its job well.

You think of the entries after the fourteenth of August going suddenly neat, the handwriting steadying, the man you grew up beside becoming, on paper, a better-regulated version of himself than grief should allow. You think of the smooth voice at 3:16 that arrived too fast and aimed its warmth too well. You had been afraid you would learn your brother was being destroyed. Instead you are learning he is being maintained, and somehow that is worse, because a thing that destroys can be interrupted and a thing that maintains has all the time in the world. Why, you say, and your voice does not sound like yours. Why keep him working. Working at what. Kurosawa does not answer that. Kurosawa filed the symptom once and watched the symptom get a black bar drawn through it, and he has learned the difference between what he reads and what he is allowed to know.

The choice it leaves you
Ask the question that scares him: working at WHAT, and for whom.
Push past the reading into the purpose, toward the routing slip and the name behind it. Kurosawa knows where the why was buried — but asking it aloud in this room makes him a witness again, which is the thing that ruined him the first time.
Stay on the body. Ask whether a thing this patient can ever be made to let go.
Turn the reading toward rescue: if it preserves rather than consumes, is there a seam to pry. It keeps the conversation survivable for Kurosawa — and risks teaching you that a preserving demon has no failure point to exploit.
Say nothing. Take the cup back and let the silence tell you he has more.
Let the quiet do the asking, and watch what a frightened honest man volunteers when not cornered. Patient — but every second in the cold room is a second your unbadged presence becomes harder to explain.
The mark a reading leaves, and the one Kurosawa cannot scrub off

He wraps the cup again himself, slowly, the way you wrap a thing you are returning to a grave, and as he does he tells you the last clinical fact and the only one that sounds like a confession. A reading leaves a wake of its own, he says — faint, a few days, nothing a demon of this calibre would even register — but the residue he just lifted off your brother's cup has now passed, however slightly, through him, and a man who reads a thing is a man the thing could, in principle, read back. I am not important enough to be noticed, he says, in the flat way of someone who has spent two years making himself unimportant on purpose. That was the strategy. File it once, get erased, become a clerk in a cold room. The mistake people make is thinking the erasing was done to me. I did most of it myself. It was the only safe place to stand. You understand, looking at him, that he has just told you why no one warned your family, and that the why is not malice but the ordinary arithmetic of survival inside a building that drew a black bar through the truth and then promoted the people who held the pen.

He gives you, before the lift comes, the one operational thread that survived the cover-up — not what Gin is, which he has now read and named for you, but where the recommendation went the day his escalation was denied. A clerk named Tanaka, two seats up the chain and one floor above, whose name is on the routing slip and who knows the single concrete thing the redaction was built to hide: the floor the reading was sent to, and the initial that signed for it. I read the symptom, Kurosawa says, already turning back toward the steel table, already rehearsing the innocence he wears like a coat. Tanaka can tell you where the symptom was filed. After that you are on your own, and so am I, and we did not have this conversation. The machines hum. The cold room keeps its residues. You put the cup back in your bag against your chest, where you have carried it since the morning, and you feel how light a thing can be and still be the heaviest thing you own.

In the lift going up you hold what he gave you and what he took: a word for the wrongness, finally, a shape to the year. Not a haunting. Not a wasting. A tenancy — patient, tidy, well-meaning in the way that is most frightening of all, a thing that keeps your brother in good repair because a tool in good repair is worth more than a tool used up. Four floors of daylight come back to meet you one button at a time, and the higher you rise the more ordinary the building pretends to be, fluorescent and procedural and full of people filing things, and you understand for the first time that the procedure is the camouflage, that a thing which wants a man kept working has chosen the perfect city and the perfect institution to keep him in. The doors open on a corridor that does not know what is under it. You step out carrying the cold room with you.

The choice it leaves you
Go straight to Tanaka while Kurosawa's nerve and yours both hold.
Run the thread up one floor before fear or the building closes it. Momentum is the only advantage you have — and walking from the cold room to a routing clerk draws a line on a map Internal Affairs can read as plainly as you can.
Protect Kurosawa first — leave no record that you came down here.
Buy the honest man his safety by erasing your own trail before pursuing the lead. Decent, and slow; every hour you spend covering tracks is an hour the routing slip can be revised by someone who signed it.
Stop and let yourself feel the word before you weaponise it.
Sit with what 'kept working' means about the brother you have been mourning wrong all year. Necessary, maybe — and grief in this building is a luxury timed in minutes you may not be able to afford.
Chapter Thirteen, continued — The Word You Did Not Have written

Kurosawa gives the thing its class and its folk-name, and you discover that naming it does not shrink it — that the Healer is a kind of demon whose whole nature is to be needed, and that a vacant seat is the reason a tidy tenant keeps a man at his desk.

What class of thing keeps a body in good repair

You ask him for the word, because all year you have been describing a shape with the wrong nouns — haunting, breakdown, possession — and none of them fit a thing that files paperwork and texts you goodnight.
