Kurosawa hesitates the way a man hesitates before handing a child a blade, and then he gives it to you anyway, because you came four floors down and you kept the cup. The textbooks have a taxonomy, he says. Hungers, rages, fears — the ones that take by craving, the ones that take by burning, the ones that take by emptying. Those are the registers most demons answer to. But there is a smaller class the books underweight because it almost never gets caught, and it almost never gets caught because it does not hurt anyone in a way that gets reported. They answer to Duty. They do not consume the host. They employ him. He watches you take it. The street word for the one that wears the Duty register and presents as help is the Healer. Not because it heals. Because being needed is how it gets through the door.

He explains it the way you explain a thing you have decided is too dangerous to soften. A Hunger announces itself; you learn to fear it. A Rage breaks furniture; you learn to flee it. But a Duty-register demon arrives as the most welcome figure in any grieving house — the one who is calm when everyone else is drowning, who handles what no one can face, who makes the calls and signs the forms and says let me, you rest. It does not lie about being useful; that is what makes it unanswerable. It is genuinely, measurably useful, and the usefulness is the leash, because a household that has been carried for a year cannot afford to suspect the arms that carried it. You cannot corner it with evidence, Kurosawa says, because every piece of evidence you raise is a service it performed, and every service was real. It does not forge the help. It only chooses, very carefully, who needs helping, and then it makes itself the only thing standing between them and the cold.

And you feel the year rearrange itself behind your eyes — your mother's relief that someone was managing Gin's affairs, the case that closed too fast and tidily, the favours done for people who could not say no to a kindness, the smooth voice that does not fight your love for your brother but enlists it and asks it to please be reasonable. You had thought you were hunting a monster. You are hunting a volunteer, a thing whose camouflage is virtue, and the worst part of the word Kurosawa just gave you is how much of your own gratitude it explains. The Healer, you say, testing it in your mouth, and it does not shrink the way you hoped a named thing would shrink. It settles. It fits. It has been the right word all along and you simply did not have it, and now that you do you cannot give it back.

The choice it leaves you
Ask the question under the question: why THIS host. Why Gin specifically.
A Healer chooses who needs it; that choice is not random. Following it leads toward the seat and the plan — and toward the unbearable possibility that something about your brother made him the ideal employee.
Ask how you fight a thing whose every weapon is a real kindness.
Turn the naming into tactics before the dread sets like concrete. Practical — but Kurosawa has already told you the honest answer is that you mostly cannot, and you may not want to hear him say it twice.
Refuse the word's comfort. A name is not a leash; say so out loud.
Resist the relief of finally labelling it, because a label can become a place to stop looking. Clear-eyed — and it means denying yourself the one small mercy this whole descent has offered you.
Why a tidy tenant keeps a man at his desk — the shape of the seat

Kurosawa does not know the plan; he was redacted long before there was a plan to know. But he knows the grammar of his own field, and when you press him on why employ a man, why keep him working, work at what, he gives you the only structural answer a body-reader can give, and it is enough to make the cold room colder. A Duty-register demon does not want a host the way a Hunger wants a meal, he says — it is not feeding. It is building. It keeps a host in good repair the way you keep a tool sharp for a job that has not happened yet, and the jobs these things build toward are always the same shape: standing. They want station, he says. Rank. A seat at a table they are not normally let near. A Hunger will never hold an office; it eats the office. A Healer can hold an office for years, because an office is only trust made permanent, and trust is the one thing it manufactures better than anything alive.

You think the word before you can stop yourself, the word from the journal's margins and the redaction's surviving bar and the name you have not yet let yourself say aloud — a vacant seat, a thirteenth of something, a domain with no holder and a thing patient enough to fill it. You do not say it to Kurosawa, because saying it would make him a witness to the part that gets people disappeared rather than merely demoted, and you have decided in the last ten minutes that you would like him to survive knowing you. But you understand now why a tidy tenant keeps a man productive: not to use him up, but to make him qualified, to walk a borrowed body up a chain of earned trust and grieving permissions until it stands somewhere a demon is never allowed to stand, and the families' reasonable silence is the staircase. Your brother is not being haunted. Your brother is being groomed for a promotion, and the promotion is not his.

Kurosawa sees something land in your face and chooses not to ask what. Whatever you just thought, he says, do not say it down here, and do not say it to anyone who outranks you, and when you go to Tanaka, ask only where the reading went, not what it meant. The where is a clerical fact. The what is the thing they erase people for. He turns back to his table and his residues and his careful unimportance, and the lift comes, and you carry up four floors a name you did not have this morning and a shape you cannot now stop seeing. The Healer. The Duty register. A seat kept empty and a man kept ready. You step out into the procedural daylight knowing, finally and exactly, what is wearing your brother — and knowing that the knowing, by itself, saves no one, because you cannot arrest a kindness and you cannot mourn a man who keeps texting you goodnight.

The choice it leaves you
Take the clerical question only to Tanaka, as Kurosawa begged — where, not what.
Walk the safe half of the thread and leave the dangerous half unspoken. It keeps you and Kurosawa alive in the building's eyes — and it means pretending, to a clerk's face, that you do not already know what the redaction was hiding.
Carry the word straight to the people on the road who can act on it.
Hand the name to Itsuki, or the Grid, or whoever your night has aligned you with, and let it become a plan instead of a dread. Speed and allies — but a word like this changes everyone who holds it, and you cannot choose how.
Go to Gin. Test the name against the brother before you build a war on it.
Put the word in front of the smooth voice and watch what answers. The only way to be sure — and the surest way to tell the Healer exactly how much you have learned, while standing in reach of the thing that keeps a man in good repair.
Book Three

The Recruited

One reasonable favour at a time — and the other demon, the one that wants to overturn the table.

Chapter Five — The Ledger of Favours written
Gin's accounts, kept in a hand that learned to be helpful

You spread your brother's papers across the low table the way you'd lay out a body for washing — by region, by what's left. The official case files are clean to the point of insult, every margin squared, every closure signed off in a fortnight that should have taken a season; it is the kind of tidiness that does not reassure you but raises the hair on your arms, because you have learned this week that the thing wearing Gin loves order the way a tourniquet loves a limb. Underneath the files, in a cheap notebook with a bent spine, is the other ledger: a list of favours, each one small, each one dated after the fourteenth, each one a person helped across some private threshold they could not cross alone.

A widow in Adachi whose husband would not stop knocking on the inside of the family altar — soothed, Gin's note says, given rest, both of them. A boy in Kita who heard the 2011 dead in the gutters when it rained — shown how to listen without drowning. A pharmacist drowning in a debt no salary could touch — relieved. Read in isolation each entry is a kindness, and that is the horror of it, the way mercy and recruitment use the same handwriting; you sit there in the Obon heat with the incense from the neighbours' balcony coming through the screen, and you understand that you are not reading a record of help but a map of leashes, every grateful person a thread the Healer is still holding.

You count them twice. There are thirty-one. Thirty-one people in this city who would, if you knocked tonight, tell you with shining eyes that your brother saved them — and who would, without knowing they were doing it, lie to your face about where he sleeps, because gratitude is the cleanest counterfeit of love and the thing in Gin has been minting it for a month.

The choice it leaves you
Start with the kindest entry — the widow in Adachi.
The ones who were genuinely helped are the ones who will defend him hardest. She will be warm, and grateful, and she will not believe a word against him — and somewhere in her gratitude is the shape of what he is collecting.
Start with the most expensive favour — the pharmacist's debt.
Money leaves a trail even when mercy doesn't. Follow what was paid and you find who else is paying, and the first hint that some of these threads run uphill, toward a seat.
Cross-check the favours against the closed case files.
If the people he 'helped' are the same names he officially cleared, then the Directorate signed off on the recruitment without ever seeing it. That is a fact you could use — or a fact that could bury you for knowing it.
The widow who would not be saved from her saviour

She makes you tea you do not want and talks about your brother as though he hung the moon and then came back to dust it. The altar behind her is quiet now; she tells you it had not been quiet in four years, that her husband used to knock from the inside of the lacquer every night at the hour he died, and that Gin sat with her through one whole night and by morning the knocking had stopped and something else had started — a warmth, she says, pressing her hand flat to her sternum, a sense of being accounted for, of belonging to a tally that someone careful is keeping. You ask her, gently, what she would do if Gin asked her for something. She looks at you as if you have asked whether she would breathe if air requested it.

Anything, she says, and means it the way the drowning mean the rope. And you see, sitting in her tidy front room with the cicadas sawing the afternoon to pieces outside, exactly how the trap is built — not out of fear, which can be fled, but out of debt, which follows you home. Thirty-one anything-I-woulds, scattered across the wards like seeds, each one ready to bloom into a hand the Healer can call its own the night it finally reaches for the Thirteenth Seat. You finish the tea so as not to insult the grief that made it.

On the way out you feel your own gift stir without permission, the way it does near places where the veil has been worked — a thinning at the threshold of her door, a cold seam where Gin stood and did whatever he did, and through it, faint, the after-taste of a register that is not your brother's and not quite a demon's reach you recognise. Something else has been here too. Something the widow never saw, that came in behind the mercy like a second guest, and left its own small mark on the seam.

The choice it leaves you
Read the seam in the doorway with your sight.
Clairsentience will show you who else passed through — but reading a worked threshold thins the veil further and feeds the Madness, and it leaves your own signature on a door the Directorate may yet come to examine.
Leave it unread and warn her, plainly.
Telling her what Gin is would be telling a woman her resurrection was a recruitment. She will not believe you, and your brother will hear by morning that you came asking — but at least one of his thirty-one will have been told the truth.
Chapter Six — The Inverted God written
A second hand in the seams, working the opposite way

You follow the second after-taste from the widow's door across three wards, and it teaches you something the Healer's threads never could: there is another worker in this city, and where Gin's mercy closes wounds and binds the grateful into one careful tally, this other thing tears. You find its marks at a flooded underpass in Kita where the 2011 dead were never properly seen home — the membrane there is not being soothed but abraded, scored thin on purpose, the way you'd sand a lock until the bolt gives. Where Gin's seams feel like a tourniquet, these feel like a held breath before a window breaks, and your gift recoils from them so hard you taste copper.

The Grid has a name for the one doing it, passed to you in a back room over a courier's shoulder with the particular hush people use for things they hope are exaggerations. They call it the Inverted God, or its aspirant does — a man named Sora who was a medium before he was a mouth, who preaches that the veil is a muzzle the living strapped onto the dead and that tearing it is not catastrophe but emancipation. He does not want a seat at the table. He wants to overturn the table, and salt the ground where it stood, and he is, by every measure the factions can take, the larger horror in this city by an order of magnitude.

Which is precisely the problem, and you feel the shape of it close around you in that back room like a hand. Because the moment there is a bigger monster, every faction with a grade and a grievance will pivot toward it — and the quiet, reasonable, mercy-minting thing wearing your brother becomes, by sheer comparison, the lesser evil, the manageable one, the one you might even ally with against the worse. The Healer did not summon the Inverted God. But the Healer has never in its long patient existence been handed a better gift than a rival monstrous enough to make it look like salvation.

The choice it leaves you
Get close to Sora and hear the sermon yourself.
The Inverted God's argument is built, like the Healer's, from true griefs stacked at a murderous angle — and hearing it will move something in you that you would rather not have moved. You may come away unable to call either of them simply wrong.
Take the discovery to a faction and watch where they point their fear.
Whoever you tell will start treating Gin as an asset against Sora. That buys your brother time and a kind of cover — and tightens the trap, because an asset is not a hostage anyone hurries to free.
Find out whether the two of them have ever spoken.
If the Healer and the Inverted God have met, then 'lesser evil' may not be an accident but a staged one. Confirming it would explain everything — and mean you are even later than you feared.
The sermon, and the true things stacked wrong

Sora preaches in a drained municipal pool in the dead hour, to maybe forty people who all have the look of the widow — accounted-for, grateful, leashed, except their leashes run the other way, toward tearing instead of binding. He is younger than the dread around him suggested, and he does not rant; he grieves, out loud, with a precision that makes the cicadas outside sound like applause. They drowned in front of us, he says of 2011, and we built a wall so we would not have to keep hearing them, and we called the wall mercy. I am only proposing we stop lying about what it is. Forty heads nod. Your own, traitorously, wants to.

And there it is again, the angle, the murderous tilt under the true thing — because he is right that the membrane is partly a wall the living built to stop hearing the dead, and he is wrong, catastrophically, terminally wrong, about what happens to a city when you take the wall down. You stand at the lip of the empty pool with the chlorine ghost in your nose and you understand that you are now holding two arguments that each contain enough truth to drown a person, and that your brother's captor is the more patient and the more polite of the two, and that politeness is going to be mistaken for safety by everyone with the power to act.

Sora finds you on the way out, the way these things always seem to. He knows your name, which means the seams have been talking. Your brother and I want opposite things, he tells you, almost kindly, and that should comfort you, and it won't, because you have already worked out that opposite things can want the same Tuesday. He lets that sit. Then: When the seat comes vacant, ask yourself who benefits from the wall being thin. It isn't only me.

The choice it leaves you
Ask Sora outright what he and the Healer have arranged.
He will not lie, exactly — that isn't his vice. But a true answer from the Inverted God is a door, and the things on the other side of it cannot be un-known once you have stepped through.
Say nothing and carry the warning back to Gin's lucid windows.
If any part of your brother is still awake in there, he needs to know the membrane plan has a second author. But every word you bring near him is also a word the Healer overhears.
Chapter Seven — The Two-Demon Trap written
Where the two seams meet, and the table is set

You lay the two maps over each other — the Healer's thirty-one bound favours, the Inverted God's scored and thinning seams — and the city resolves into a single design the way two halves of a torn photograph resolve into one face. Gin's mercy clusters around the wards where the membrane is strongest; Sora's tearing concentrates where it is already weakest; and between them, in Kita-ku, at the wound that 2011 never closed, the two patterns do not collide. They interlock. One thins the wall to the breaking point; the other stands ready, grateful hands and all, to do something careful at the moment it breaks — to close a seat, to take a seat, to turn catastrophe into the one obligation a Duty-register demon has waited a century to be permitted to meet.

This is the trap, and you finally see that it was never set for you. You are not the prey; you are the witness it can afford, because a witness with no grade and a sister's love makes a perfect leash and a perfect alibi both. The factions will rush to stop the obvious monster. The obvious monster will let them, will even help, will be so reasonable about it that when the wall finally goes and your brother's captor steps into the Thirteenth Seat through the gap, every grade in the city will have spent its strength on the wrong demon and feel, for a terrible clean moment, that it had won.

You sit with this in the worst hour of the Obon heat, the lanterns going up on the river somewhere to guide the dead home, and you do the arithmetic no one should have to do. To stop the Healer you may have to let the Inverted God be stopped by it. To stop the Inverted God you may have to hand the factions a reasonable ally wearing your brother's face. There is no move on this board that does not cost you something you are still pretending you get to keep.

The choice it leaves you
Try to break the interlock — warn the factions which demon is the real door.
If they believe you, they redirect their strength toward Gin, and your brother becomes a battlefield. If they don't, you have shown your whole hand to the seams for nothing — and the Healer will know exactly how much you understand.
Refuse the frame entirely — look for a third move neither demon planned for.
There may be a way to thicken the wall instead of choosing which monster thins it. It would cost your gift, your cover, and possibly your mind to the Madness — and it is the only path that doesn't end with you trading one demon for another.
Reach Gin in a lucid window and tell him the shape of his own trap.
If the man under the Healer understands that his mercy is the second jaw of a snare, he might fight from the inside. He might also break — and a captor's leash pulls hardest the instant the captive sees the leash.
The Healer answers, reasonably, through your brother's mouth

It calls you this time. The phone lights in the dark and it is Gin's number and you already know, before you answer, which of the two voices will be on the line, because the trap is sprung now and there is no further use in pretending to be only your brother. The evenness is total tonight; there is no frightened seam in it, no go home, just the Healer speaking through a mouth it has stopped sharing. You've done the arithmetic, it says, and there is something almost like pride in it. Good. Then you understand I am the kinder answer. Sora would unmake the wall and let the city drown in its own unfinished grief. I would close the wound properly, from the only seat high enough to do it, and the dead would finally be accounted for. Including ours.

It lets ours land. It knows what it is doing; it has had a month inside your brother's memories and it spends them like coins. It tells you that when it takes the Thirteenth Seat it will not need Gin anymore — that the man can be set down, intact, returned, the way you set down a tool when the work is finished — and that all of this, the favours and the witness and the long patient design, can end with you getting your brother back, whole, if you simply stop pulling on the one thread that might tear the work before it completes. It is the most reasonable offer anyone has ever made you, built entirely of true things, tilted at the exact angle that drowns.

And in the silence after, so quietly you almost miss it under the dial tone of its certainty, there is a sound that is not the Healer: a breath, three rooms deep in the voice, ragged and human and choosing, with the last lucid strength it has, to spend that strength not on a word but on a single tap against the receiver — once, then once, then once — the rhythm of the widow's altar, the knock from the inside, your brother telling you in the only language left to him that he is still in there, and that the kind offer is a wall, and that he would rather you tore it down than leave him accounted for.

The choice it leaves you
Accept the offer's terms out loud — and mean none of it.
Buy time by letting the Healer believe it has reached you. It costs you Sora's trust if he hears, and it costs you a piece of yourself to say the words — but a captor that thinks it has won stops watching the leash so closely.
Answer the knock, not the voice — refuse, in your brother's own code.
Tap back. Tell the man you heard him and the demon that you didn't take the deal. It declares the war openly and ends the cover that kept you alive this far — but it is the one answer Gin asked you for, in the only language he had left.
Chapter Fourteen — The Ledger Grows written

You set out to verify thirty-one favours and find thirty-four. The ledger is still being written, and the hand that writes it knows you are reading.

The count that will not hold still

You begin the way you were taught to begin a body — by establishing what is fixed before you trust anything that moves. Thirty-one entries, you'd told yourself, thirty-one leashes in your brother's neat afterlife hand, a finite shape you could walk the edges of in a single sweltering Obon week. So you copy them again onto fresh paper, each name, each ward, each dated mercy, because copying is how you make a thing yours and right now nothing in this city belongs to you, least of all the count. And it is in the copying that the floor tilts: between the widow in Adachi and the pharmacist in Kita there is now an entry you did not transcribe two nights ago, a young man in Sumida soothed of a grief that was eating his sleep, dated the day before yesterday, in the same patient script.

You sit very still in the heat with the fan pushing the incense around and you make yourself say the unbearable thing in plain words: the ledger is not a record. A record is finished. This is a living document, and the hand keeping it has not stopped, has not even slowed, is in fact writing faster now than in the first week after the fourteenth — as if your counting were a kind of pressure that the Healer answers by simply helping more people, more openly, daring you to call mercy a crime. Thirty-one was never the number. Thirty-one was just the last time you looked away.

You count once more, slowly, touching each line. Thirty-four. Three names you have never met have been added to your brother's list of the saved since you decided to save him, and the symmetry of that is so clean it makes your stomach turn — every step you take toward Gin, the thing wearing Gin takes a step into someone else, and the rope grows from both ends at once. Somewhere out there a man in Sumida is sleeping soundly for the first time in months and would weep with gratitude if you told him why, and you are the only person in Tokyo who understands that his good night's sleep is a knot, and that you are standing at the end of the rope it is tied to.

The choice it leaves you
Chase the freshest knot — the man in Sumida, before the gratitude sets.
He was helped two days ago; the binding is still wet. If any of the thirty-four can still be shown the leash before it becomes love, it is him — but reaching a fresh convert means moving while the Healer is actively working, close enough to feel you coming.
Stop copying and start dating — line the entries up by the day they were written.
A timeline is colder than a map and it will tell you something the names won't: whether the favours cluster, whether they aim. But to build it you have to stop reacting to each new knot and sit with the whole growing rope, and the longer you study its shape the more certain you become that it is reaching toward one place.
Add yourself to the page — note the dates you looked, beside the dates it wrote.
If the additions track your attention, you are not an investigator of the ledger but a variable in it. Proving that means admitting your brother's possessor is keeping a record of you too — and that every hour you spend reading is an hour it spends being read.
What the dates make when you line them up

You give in and build the timeline, because the dead taught you that chronology is the one thing grief cannot argue with. You tear a long strip from the back of Gin's archive notebook and you lay the thirty-four favours along it by the day each was granted, and the heat-thick room goes quiet around you in the way rooms go quiet when a pattern that was hiding in plain noise finally stands up. The favours do not fall evenly. They come in tides — a flurry in the days right after the fourteenth, then a lull through the assessments your brother's employers still think they're administering to a living man, then a second swell that begins, you notice with a cold that has nothing to do with the fan, the exact week you started asking questions at his door.

And they aim. Plotting them by ward instead of by date you'd seen a city-wide scatter, mercy sprayed at random need; but plotted by date the scatter resolves into a drift, each new knot a little nearer the river, a little nearer the old waterworks land north of Kita where the official files have nothing and your skin has too much. The Healer is not helping wherever help is needed. It is helping in a widening spiral that keeps one edge always pointed at a single quarter of the map, the way a long rope coils toward the post it is finally tied off on — and you understand that the thirty-four are not the point any more than the turns of a rope are the point. They are the length being paid out toward the thing at the end.

You find your own dates on the strip without meaning to look for them — the morning you came to the apartment, the night you read the archive in his demanded order, the afternoon you stood in the drained pool listening to Sora preach the true things stacked wrong. Beside each one, within a day, a fresh favour. The correlation is not perfect and you make yourself say so, out loud, because false certainty is its own kind of possession; but it is close enough that the alternative — coincidence — now costs more belief than the truth does. You are being answered. Every question you ask of the dark gets answered in the body of a stranger, and the kindness of the reply is the cruelest part, because there is no one you can warn who would not first have to stop being grateful.

The choice it leaves you
Follow the drift to its post — walk the spiral inward toward the Kita waterworks.
The timeline points somewhere real. Walking the coil to its center means going to the one quarter of the city your sight already refuses, where the membrane is thinnest and the Healer's work is densest — toward, you increasingly suspect, wherever it has put your brother's sleeping body.
Break the correlation on purpose — go quiet for a day and watch the ledger.
If the additions stop when you stop, you are the tide. The test is clean but the cost is a wasted day in a week you do not have, spent doing nothing while a thing wearing your brother's face decides what your silence means — and silence, with the Healer, has only ever meant consent.
Take the strip to someone who reads bodies — Kurosawa, who already knows too much.
A second pair of trained eyes might see a pattern your hope is bending. But to show the timeline you have to explain how you got it, and explaining means telling Kurosawa that your brother is the pen, not the page — and once she knows, she is bound to the same impossible silence you are, or to a duty that ends with Gin filed as a hostile entity.
The entry it wrote for you to find

The thirty-fourth name was added in the night and you find it at dawn, and it is the only entry on either ledger written in a hand that is not quite Gin's. The letters are his and the patience is his but something in the pressure is wrong, leaning, the way handwriting goes when a different intelligence is steering a familiar arm — and the favour it records is not for a stranger. R., it begins, the way your brother has begun notes to you since you were children, R., you have not slept since the fourteenth and it is starting to show in the work. Let me give you rest, both of us, the way I gave the widow. You only have to stop counting. — G. You read it four times in the grey light and the incense from the neighbours and the fan turning its slow refusal, and you understand that the ledger has finally written the only entry it was ever building toward.

It is an offer and a threat folded into the same kindness, which is the Healer's only grammar. Stop counting and be given rest: it is exactly what it told the widow whose husband knocked on the inside of the altar, exactly what it told the boy who heard the drowned in the gutters, and you have spent a week learning what rest means in that hand — rest means the knot tied off, the gratitude set, the thread held forever by something that loves the word the way a tourniquet loves a limb. The thing wearing your brother is not afraid of you. It has simply decided, reasonably, patiently, that you are the next person in Tokyo who could be helped across a threshold you cannot cross alone, and it has written your name on the list before you ever agreed to be on it.

You take the note and you fold it and you do not put it in the ledger with the others, because the others are knots and this one is the hand reaching for you, and you will not file the hand among the rope. Out on the balcony the first commuters are folding into the day with their grief-lit Obon faces, every one of them a person who could be saved into a leash, and you stand there with your brother's love-shaped trap in your pocket and you make the smallest, hardest promise of the week: you will keep counting. Not because counting is winning — it isn't, the ledger grows faster than you can read it — but because the day you stop is the day the thirty-fifth entry has your gratitude in it, and you would rather be tired and yours than rested and held.

The choice it leaves you
Answer the note in Gin's own code — write back, and see which brother replies.
You share a childhood shorthand the Healer would have to fake. A reply could surface the real Gin in the lucid seam, or confirm the wearer has learned even that — but to write back you have to open a channel the thing has been waiting all week for you to open, on its terms, in his hand.
Refuse the rest out loud, alone, where it can hear — and brace for the cost of saying no.
The widow was never offered twice. Declining the Healer's mercy to its face is the one move the favours have no script for, and you have no idea whether the thing answers a refusal with patience or with the body it is keeping somewhere north of Kita.
Burn nothing, hide nothing — pin the note where you will see it, and keep the page open.
You could make the trap a goad: keep its offer in sight as the reason to finish the count. But living with the love-shaped threat pinned to your wall means waking every morning to your brother's hand asking you, reasonably, to stop — and the Healer is very, very good at being reasonable for longer than a person can hold out.
Chapter Fourteen, continued — The Knot That Pulls Back written

You go to the freshest convert to show him the leash before the gratitude sets — and learn that a knot, touched, tightens, and that the rope runs in both directions.

Sumida, the man whose grief was eaten

His name is Iwata and he opens the door of his Sumida flat already smiling, the particular wet-eyed smile you have learned to dread this week, the smile of a person who has been carried over water and does not yet know the boat is still beneath them. He is younger than the entry made him sound, maybe twenty-six, and the flat behind him is the flat of a man who has just started living again — dishes done, a window open, a photograph of someone gone turned at last to face the room instead of the wall. You're his brother, Iwata says before you say anything, and your skin goes cold, because you have given this man no name. He told me you'd come. He said you'd be tired and that I should make you tea.

You sit because to stand would be to flee, and you take the tea because to refuse it would be to start the conversation with an accusation, and across the low table you watch a genuinely happy man explain how a soft-spoken stranger named Gin found him three nights ago at the river, where he had gone to do the thing the grief was steering him toward, and simply — Iwata's hands open, helplessly grateful — took the weight off. Showed him how to set it down. Iwata sleeps now. Iwata eats. Iwata has, he says with shining eyes, never felt so held in his life, and every true word of it lands in you like a nail, because you came here to show this man a leash and what you have found is a man who would have died on Saturday and is alive on Tuesday, and the thing that saved him is the thing you have come to destroy.

You try, gently, to find the seam — you ask him whether he's noticed anything he can't explain, any debts he doesn't remember agreeing to, any pull when he thinks of the man who helped him. And you watch the question slide off him like rain off oilcloth, watch the gratitude close smoothly over the place where doubt should go, and you understand the engineering of it at last: the Healer does not bind people against their grief. It binds them through it. The leash is not laid over the wound; it is woven into the healing, so that to pull on the thread is to pull on the one thing keeping the person whole, and no one — not Iwata, not the widow, not you — can hate the hand that is also the stitches.

The choice it leaves you
Tell him the truth, all of it, and let him choose with his eyes open.
He deserves to know what saved him. But the truth you carry is that his peace is a tether and his rescuer is wearing a dead man — and there is no version of saying it that does not threaten to tear out the stitches and drop him back at the river, this time knowing he was used.
Tell him nothing — take what he knows of 'Gin' and leave the healing intact.
Iwata met the wearer face to face three nights ago; his memory is the freshest map you have to where the Healer walks and what it sounds like up close. You can mine it and leave him his peace. But you will walk out having used the convert the way the Healer uses him, and called it mercy too.
Test the thread — say your brother's name with love and watch what answers in Iwata.
If the binding runs both ways, naming Gin warmly in front of a bound man might stir the thing on the other end. It is the closest you can get to knocking on the rope. It is also the surest way to let the Healer know exactly which knot you are standing beside, and how tenderly.
The thread goes taut in both hands

You say it — you say Gin the way you said it across a childhood, soft, claiming, the word you used to call him in from the dark — and the flat changes temperature. Not Iwata; Iwata only tilts his head, still smiling, asking if you're all right. It is the air that answers, the cheap fluorescent over the sink dimming by a breath as if something far away leaned its weight against a wall, and in the back of your own skull, behind the two registers you keep pressed flat, you feel the thread you reached for reach back. It is the first time all week the rope has had a near end as well as a far one. The Healer is on the line, and through Iwata's untouched contentment it has just felt your hand close on its work, and it is — you would swear to this in the heat with your pulse loud — interested.

This is the thing the ledger could not teach you and the man at the river just did: a knot is not a knot. A knot is a place where the rope folds back on itself, and when you pull, both lengths move. You have spent a week imagining yourself outside the design, a reader standing over a map, and in this small bright kitchen with a saved man pouring you more tea you finally feel the truth your sight has been muffling — that the moment you touched a binding with love in your voice, you put love into the system, and the Healer keeps a ledger of love the way it keeps a ledger of favours, and you have just made an entry you cannot unwrite. It knows your frequency now. It has been wanting it since the fourteenth.

You set the cup down before your hand can shake it. Across the table Iwata's peace is undisturbed, perfect, sealed, and that is the horror you will carry out the door — that the convert felt nothing, that the violence ran entirely between you and the thing in your brother, over the head of the man whose grief was the medium. You came to Sumida to free a knot and instead you proved the rope runs both ways, which means the Healer can be reached, which means the Healer can reach you, and you no longer know whether you are pulling it toward the light or it is reeling you toward the post at Kita, one warm patient turn at a time.

The choice it leaves you
Hold the thread — keep your love on the line and let it pull you toward Gin.
If the rope runs both ways you could climb it inward, follow the taut line all the way to where the real Gin sleeps. But a thread you hold, holds you; the deeper you let the binding seat, the harder it becomes to tell your love for your brother from the leash the Healer is weaving out of it.
Let go fast and clean — cut your own feeling off mid-reach.
You can drop the line before the knot sets, the way the dead taught you to let go of a body you cannot save. It keeps you free another day. It also means turning, deliberately, cold toward Gin while the warmth is still proof he is in there — and coldness is the one thing the lucid seam might not survive.
Mark the knot and back away — leave Iwata whole and the thread for later.
You could withdraw without cutting, leaving the binding intact and the convert in peace, and come back to this line when you understand the post it ties to. Patience keeps everyone alive tonight. It also leaves a saved man tethered to a thing you now know is aware of you, and trusts you to come back the way it trusts everyone to stay grateful.
Chapter Fourteen, continued — The Thirty-Fifth Name written

Back at the apartment the ledger has grown again — and this time the new entry is not a stranger you can warn or a convert you can mine. It is the proof that the count was always a countdown.

The page that filled while you were out

You come back from Sumida in the killing afternoon heat and the ledger has grown while the flat sat empty, which it has never done before — every prior addition appeared in the small hours, in sleep's blind window, but this one came in daylight while you were across the city with your hand on Iwata's thread, and the timing is not lost on you. The thirty-fifth entry is short. It is in the leaning hand, the not-quite-Gin pressure, and it does not record a favour granted. It records one offered and refused, and the refusal it names is yours. R. would not set the weight down, it reads, so the weight stays. We will try again when he is tired enough. He is nearly tired enough. — G.

So the count was always a countdown. You see it whole now, the way you saw the timeline stand up, the way you saw the spiral aim at Kita: the ledger was never a list of the saved with you outside it taking notes. It was a list of the saved with a blank line kept open at the bottom, and the blank line had your name on it from the first night you came to the door, and everything since — the spiral, the daylight favours, the note in his hand, Iwata's tea poured before you arrived — has been the Healer narrowing the distance between you and the moment you are tired enough to be helped. It is not racing you to Gin. It is waiting for you to arrive at it, exhausted and grateful, which is the only door it has ever needed anyone to walk through.

You should feel hunted and instead you feel, for one clarifying second, almost calm — because a countdown, unlike a hunt, has a logic you can read, and the logic is simple and terrible: it wins when you stop, and it wins when you break, and the only state it has no entry for is a man who keeps moving while tired and refuses to be grateful. That is the whole of your road now. Not to outrun the ledger — you can't, it writes faster than you read — but to stay, every hour, the one name it cannot finish: awake, ungrateful, still counting, still yours. You take out the folded note from this morning and lay the two of them side by side, the offer and the refusal recorded in the same patient hand, and you understand you are reading a conversation the Healer has been having with you for a week, in which only it has been allowed to speak.

The choice it leaves you
Speak back into the ledger — take the pen and write an entry of your own.
It has been narrating you all week; you could narrate back, leave a line in the book in your own hand for the thing to find. It is the first move that treats the ledger as a channel rather than a wound. It also tells the Healer, in writing, that you understand the game — and a player who knows the rules is more useful to it than one who doesn't, which is its own kind of trap.
Burn the ledger and keep only the timeline in your head.
If the page is the channel, destroy the page. The favours stay bound but the conversation goes dark, and you walk away with the shape of the spiral and nothing the thing can write on. But you would be burning the only standing record of thirty-five real people the Healer holds — and your only proof, to Kurosawa or anyone, that any of this is more than a tired man's grief.
Carry the ledger to Kita — take the countdown to the post it points at.
Every line aims north; the thirty-fifth says it is waiting for you to arrive. You could stop being read and start arriving on your own terms, walk the ledger to the thinned membrane and the body you believe sleeps there, while you are still ungrateful enough to mean it. It is the boldest move and the one the Healer most expects — the entire countdown has been an invitation to exactly this door.
Tired enough, and not yet

Night comes the way Obon nights come, lantern-lit and full of the returning dead, and you sit with the two notes and the long torn strip of dates and you take your own pulse the way you'd take a stranger's — honestly, clinically, looking for the thing the Healer is waiting for. You are tired. There is no use pretending the tiredness away; it lives behind your eyes and in the two flat registers you have not let yourself feel in days, and the thing wearing your brother has read it correctly, the way it reads everyone, which is the whole of its terrible competence. He is nearly tired enough. Nearly. The word is doing more work than you want it to, because nearly is a confession: it means the door is not open yet, it means there is still a margin, it means tonight, this hour, you are still the unfinished entry and not the closed one.

You do the only thing the countdown has no answer for, which is the smallest thing — you decide to be tired and stay. Not heroically; you are too exhausted for heroism and the Healer would only file heroism as another threshold it could help you across. You simply refuse the rest the way Iwata could not, the way the widow was never given the chance to, and you keep the page open, and you write nothing into it, and you let the long night be long. Somewhere north of Kita your brother's body is sleeping a sleep that something else is dreaming, and somewhere closer the ledger waits for the morning when you are tired enough to be grateful, and between those two facts you sit awake and ungrateful and alive, which is, you understand now, the only act of love the Healer has left you that it cannot turn into a leash.

Before you sleep — and you will sleep, you have to, the body keeps its own ledger and it is calling in its debts — you turn the strip of dates over and you start a new line at the bottom, on the blank the Healer kept open, and you do not write your gratitude there. You write the date, and beside it three words in your own unleaned hand, your own claim staked on the only page the thing has been allowed to fill: still counting. — R. Then you lie down in the heat with both notes within reach and you let yourself be tired, deliberately, openly, an entry that refuses to close, and outside the lanterns burn for the dead and the city forgets itself toward dawn, and the ledger, for the first time all week, has an answer in it that the Healer did not write.

The choice it leaves you
Sleep with the page open — trust that 'still counting' holds the line through the dark.
Rest is the one weapon you cannot refuse forever, and refusing it is exactly the exhaustion the Healer is counting. Sleeping now is a calculated risk that your own claim on the page outlasts the night. It is also the moment of maximum exposure — tired enough, asleep, the unfinished entry alone with the hand that wants to finish it.
Take the note in his hand to the lucid seam — use the love, not the leash, before you rest.
If the thread runs both ways and Gin still surfaces in the lucid window, you could spend the last of your waking strength reaching for your brother instead of guarding against his wearer. It might wake the real Gin. It might spend you to empty in front of the exact thing that wins when you are spent.
Don't sleep — walk out into the Obon night and put distance between you and the open page.
You could refuse the bed entirely, take the ledger and the timeline and move, stay the moving-while-tired man the countdown has no entry for. Distance buys you margin against the daylight favours. It also burns the body's reserves faster, and the Healer has all the patience in the world and you have, the strip of dates tells you, three more weeks of nights like this one.
Chapter Fifteen — The Far-Shore Circle written

The address in no file leads to a third-floor room in Fukai where grief is not mourned but organized. You find the Higan-kai mid-service, a congregation arranged around a centre that breathes for all of them.

The stairwell that hums

The address you copied from the ledger's freshest knot doesn't go to a home; it goes to a narrow building in Fukai with a shuttered noodle shop on the ground floor and a stairwell that hums, faintly, the way a transformer hums, a pressure you feel in the fillings of your teeth before you can name it as sound. You climb past the second landing where someone has left a child's sandals paired neatly against the wall, paired the way the dead's shoes get paired at a wake, and the gift you keep folded down behind your sternum opens one degree without your permission, the way a wound opens to weather. By the third floor the hum has resolved into something almost like chanting, except that no one is using their voice — it is the membrane itself that is being made to ring, struck and struck and held, and you understand with a cold drop in your gut that you are standing at the back door of a room where people have learned to do on purpose what you spent your whole life keeping accidental.

Through the gap of the door you see them: thirty, maybe thirty-five people, seiza on borrowed zabuton, arranged not in rows but in a ring, and the ring is not facing a shrine or a screen or a priest. It faces inward, toward a single woman at the centre in a grey summer kimono, hands loose in her lap, eyes closed, and every face in that circle is turned to her the way sunflowers turn — not in worship, exactly, but in the helpless geometry of things that have found their source of light. The air around her is doing something your eyes refuse to render cleanly, a shimmer like heat off asphalt except that it is August-still in the room and the shimmer is cold. You have walked into more haunted rooms than you can count. You have never walked into one that was this organized about it.

And then, without opening her eyes, without raising her voice, the woman at the centre says your brother's surname — your surname — into the ringing quiet, and adds, in a tone of perfect hospitality, that the seat at the eastern edge of the circle has been kept open for some time now, that the tea is still warm, and that there is no need to keep standing in a doorway as though you were not expected. The congregation does not turn to look at you. That is the worst part. They do not need to. Whatever sees you is seeing through all of them at once, and it has been waiting, and it is pleased.

The choice it leaves you
Step in and take the eastern seat she kept for you.
To learn how the circle works you have to enter its geometry, and the geometry is the trap — sit in a ring built to feed a centre and you become, however briefly, one of the faces that turns toward the light. You will feel what they feel. You may not be able to un-feel it.
Stay in the doorway — refuse the seat, make her come to the threshold.
A threshold is the one place in an occult room where the rules are still being negotiated. Holding it denies her the clean geometry of the ring, but it also announces that you came to fight, and you have not yet seen what is here to fight.
Read the congregation before you read her — find the freshest convert, the man from Sumida.
Somewhere in this ring is the knot the ledger wrote two days ago, a grief still wet enough to remember being a grief. Finding him means letting your gift open further than is safe in a room built to amplify exactly that — and the centre will feel you reaching.
What the circle is for

You learn the shape of it the way you learn the shape of a drowned man's last hour — not from anyone's account but from the body of the thing itself. These are not believers who came for comfort and stayed for community; these are the bereaved of a city that has too many of them, the widow whose husband went into the 2011 water and was never properly seen home, the mother whose son's name is on a ward that no longer answers, the man from Sumida whose grief was eating his sleep until something patient reached in and ate the grief instead. Each of them carried a wound across the membrane, a thin place worn by loss, and the circle does not heal those thin places. It harvests them. Thirty-five private erosions, gathered into one room, tuned to one frequency, and the woman at the centre is the lens that focuses them — and the relief on every face is real, is the realest thing in the room, because to be relieved of grief is a mercy and they have each, genuinely, been shown mercy.

You make yourself follow the current to where it goes, because that is the only question that matters and you already half know the answer. The harvested thinness does not stay here. It is being drawn somewhere, eastward and downward, toward the old flooded ground in Kita where you felt the membrane scored on purpose, toward the held breath before the window breaks — and you realize the Higan-kai is not a rival operation to the Healer and the Inverted God but a supply line feeding the same hunger from a different mouth. Gin's ledger binds the grateful one careful favour at a time; Sora's sermons tear from the outside; and this, this circle, simply gathers the city's grief into a battery and lets whoever has the wiring draw it down. The horror is not that these people were tricked. The horror is that they were helped, and that their being helped is a fuel.

The man from Sumida is three seats to your left, and when you let your gift brush him you feel the knot the ledger described — the soothed grief, the leash of gratitude — but here in the circle it is doubled, because the Healer's mercy and the Far-Shore's harvest have both passed through him and he no longer remembers a self that existed before either. He turns his head, mild and kind, and asks if you, too, lost someone in the water, and his face is so open and so unburdened that you understand the cruelty of what you are about to consider: that to save this room you may have to give grief back to people who would rather die than carry it again.

The choice it leaves you
Trace the eastward draw — follow the current out of the room toward Kita.
The supply line is the real intelligence; mapping where the battery discharges tells you which catastrophe it serves. But to follow it you have to leave the man from Sumida sitting in the lens, and the current does not like being watched while it feeds.
Try to wake the man from Sumida — show him the leash while he can still see it.
He is the one knot still wet enough to remember. Cutting it here, inside the circle, would prove the harvest can be reversed — but you would be tearing a man's only peace out of him in a room built to punish exactly that, with thirty-four other faces and one cold centre between you and the door.
Hold still and let the centre take your measure — let her decide you are worth speaking to.
The woman in grey has kept a seat for you and named your blood; she wants something from you specifically. Letting her open the conversation means surrendering the first move, but it is the fastest way to the one mind in this room that knows where the battery discharges and why.
The face that is two faces

When she finally opens her eyes the congregation exhales as one, a long soft release like a room of sleepers turning over, and several of them sway where they sit, suddenly tired in the way of people who have been carried a long way and set gently down. She looks at you across the ring and for one clean second she is only a woman — fortyish, kind around the mouth, a teacher's patience in the set of her shoulders, the kind of person who would remember your name and ask after your mother — and you feel the trap of that clarity even as it works on you, because the thing wearing her has let you see the host on purpose, the way a card player shows you one honest card to make you trust the hand. This is Fukai Rinka. They call her Kanna-sensei in the ring, the way you call a thing teacher when it has taught you to feel better. And under the teacher, patient and immense and entirely uninterested in pretending otherwise, is the register the Grid files under one syllable like a held breath: Kishin.

It does not posture the way Sora's Inverted God postures, all sermon and emancipation; it does not hide behind mercy the way the Healer hides. When Kishin looks at you through Rinka's kind eyes it simply lets you feel its appetite as a fact of weather, the way you'd feel the cold coming off a deep well, and the fact it wants you to understand is this: it is older than the seat-holders' politics, older than the membrane plan, and it does not care which catastrophe wins. It cares that grief keeps arriving. The Healer and the aspirant are, to Kishin, two competing customers for the same renewable harvest, and you — Yushiro's anchor, the one person walking toward Gin instead of away — are the most interesting thing to come up the humming stairs in months, because you are grief that has not yet been spent.

She lifts the iron pot beside her, the one that has been keeping warm this whole time, and pours into a second cup as though the conversation you are about to have was scheduled, as though your name had been in a book of hers the way the favours are in your brother's. Drink the tea, she does not quite say, and the kindness in it is real, and the bill in it is real, and both being real at once is the entire nature of the thing. Somewhere east and down, the battery this room has spent the evening charging is waiting to be drawn against, and the woman pouring your tea is the only person in Tokyo who knows by whom — and she will tell you, warmly, for a price she has not yet named and you will not see until it has already been paid.

The choice it leaves you
Sit and drink — accept the audience on Kanna-sensei's terms.
Kishin is a renewable intelligence source and it is offering to be read; the warmth is the bill and the bill comes later. Drink, and you may learn who draws down the battery — but you will owe a demon that erodes whoever uses it, and it has just watched you decide you were willing.
Name it aloud — say 'Kishin' to its face and refuse the host's comfort.
Naming the register strips the teacher's mask and forces the thing to deal with you as itself, demon to anchor. It also tells every face in the ring that their Kanna-sensei is a tenant, and a room of the recently un-grieved will not thank you for the truth — some of them would rather you were never told.
Decline the cup and offer a trade instead — your grief is not free either.
If you are the most interesting thing to come up the stairs because you are unspent grief, then you have leverage Kishin wants and has not yet been given. Bargaining from that position keeps you out of debt — but it means dangling the one thing you came here to protect, and a thing this old has a long memory for what you let it taste.
Chapter Fifteen, continued — The Tea That Costs You Something written

Kanna-sensei's audience. Kishin trades in warmth, and the warmth is the bill. You learn who draws down the battery — and feel, first-hand, why every source in this city erodes whoever leans on it.

What it wants is never money

The tea is genmaicha, toasted and ordinary, and it is the most dangerous thing you have drunk all week precisely because it is so ordinary, because Kishin understands that the way to a guarded person is not through fear but through the small kindness they have been too busy to receive. Rinka pours and asks, in the host's voice, whether you have eaten, whether you are sleeping, whether the heat is keeping you up — and you feel each question land in the part of you that has not been asked those things by anyone since the fourteenth, the part that has been holding a brother's vanishing alone in a city of nine million, and you have to set the cup down to keep your hand from being read. This is what it wants, and it is never money. It wants you to be comfortable in its presence. Comfort is how the erosion gets in, one warm hour at a time, until the day you find you cannot think a hard thought about Kanna-sensei without a small grief at the disloyalty of it.

You make yourself ask the only question worth the visit — who draws the battery down — and Rinka answers without coyness, because Kishin gains nothing by withholding and everything by being the one who told you. Both, she says. The Healer comes for it gently, in the small hours, and what he takes he uses to soften the seams in Kita so they will part without tearing — a surgeon thinning a wall to a membrane he can lift clean. And the other one, the boy who preaches, sends his people to draw against it in great greedy pulls, because he wants the wall not lifted but broken, wants the held breath let out all at once. The Far-Shore Circle, she says, pouring more tea you did not ask for, sells to both, and has no opinion about walls. It only minds that the grief keeps coming, and lately — she lets this land softly — the grief has been coming faster, because two demons competing for the same harvest is very good for the harvest.

And there it is, the thing you came up the stairs not knowing you needed: confirmation that your brother and the cult's god are not two unrelated horrors but two ends of one rope, and that the rope is being paid out from this room. You should feel the click of a case closing. Instead you feel the cup warm in your hands and Rinka's patient eyes on you and the terrible domestic peace of the moment, and you understand that you have already begun to owe her — not for a service rendered but for a comfort accepted, and that the bill for comfort is the hardest of all to read, because it never arrives as a number. It arrives as a softness you did not used to have, in the exact place you used to keep your edge.

The choice it leaves you
Press for the schedule — when does the Healer come for his gentle draw?
Knowing the hours Gin's hand reaches into this battery would put you, for once, ahead of him instead of reading his leavings. But asking for an appointment with your brother's possessor is asking Kishin to set you up across a table from the thing you most fear, at a time of its choosing, and it will remember that you asked.
Refuse the second cup and the third question — pull your edge back while you can still feel it.
The erosion works through comfort, and you can still feel the softness it is leaving. Standing up now keeps you sharp, but it ends the audience with most of the battery's secrets still unbought, and Kishin does not give a second first meeting for free.
Turn the warmth back on her — ask Rinka, the woman, whether she chose this.
There is a host under the demon, a teacher who was kind before she was a lens, and reaching for her could fracture Kishin's hold long enough to learn something the demon would never offer. But hosts who are surfaced against their tenant's will rarely survive it, and you would be spending a stranger's life to ask a question.
The price named, almost

When you finally name a price — because you will not leave owing a thing you cannot see, you have watched what unseen debts did to the people in your brother's ledger — Kishin smiles through Rinka's mouth and tells you the cost in the only currency it keeps. It does not want your blood or your service or your soul, those being merchant's words for a merchant's appetites; it wants the next grief you carry to come here first. Not all your grief. Just the next fresh one, whenever it lands, before you have decided alone what to do with it — the way the man from Sumida brought his, the way the widow brought hers. Bring it warm to the circle, sit in the eastern seat, and let the room take its weight off you for one evening, and the intelligence you bought tonight is paid in full. It is, she points out gently, the same deal everyone in the ring has made, and look how lightly they carry their losses now.

You think of Gin. You think of the precise grief you are carrying up these stairs and down them, the one with your brother's name on it, still unspent, still yours, the only thing keeping you walking toward him while the whole city walks away — and you understand that what Kishin has asked for, in its perfect hospitality, is the one grief that must never be soothed, because the day you stop hurting over Gin is the day you stop coming for him. The demon knows this. That is the elegance of the price. It has not asked you to betray your brother; it has asked you to be comforted about him, and trusted that you are tired enough, after a week of this heat and this dread and this aloneness, to want comfort more than you want the edge that comfort costs.

Rinka sets the iron pot down with a soft iron sound, and around you the congregation is beginning to stir and gather their sandals and murmur their goodnights, ordinary people stepping out into the Obon dark a little lighter than they came, and not one of them looks back at the centre that carried them. You can leave the same way. You can take everything you learned tonight and owe nothing but the next ache, and the next ache is months away, surely, and surely you will be stronger by then. That is exactly the size of the lie the room is built to make feel like sense. The tea has gone cold in your hands and you did not notice it cooling, which is, you think, the whole demonstration.

The choice it leaves you
Take the deal — buy tonight's intelligence with a grief you swear you'll never bring.
You leave with the Healer's schedule and the battery's wiring and a debt you intend to default on. But Kishin has been collecting from people who intended to default since before this city had a name, and a demon you mean to cheat is a worse creditor than one you mean to pay.
Counter — pay in someone else's spent grief, not your own next one.
You could offer the circle access to a grief already soothed, a knot from Gin's ledger, and keep your own ache unmortgaged. It might satisfy the letter of the appetite — but it means feeding a demon the people you came to free, and learning whether you are the kind of person who will.
Walk out owing nothing — refuse the deal and lose what you didn't buy.
Standing up with your grief intact and your edge intact is the only move that leaves you clean. It also leaves the Healer's schedule unbought and Kanna-sensei certain you will be back when the next loss lands, because she has watched this exact refusal a hundred times and knows what August does to the lonely.
Chapter Fifteen, concluded — The Battery of Grief written

Outside the circle, the two catastrophes resolve into one wiring diagram. The Healer thins the wall to lift it; the Inverted God tears it to break it; the Far-Shore feeds them both — and you stand at the only junction where the current can still be cut.

The wiring diagram

On the street below the humming building the August night is thick enough to chew and the cicadas have not stopped since dusk, and you stand against the shuttered noodle shop and finally let yourself draw the thing you have been refusing to draw all week, the wiring diagram, because a diagram is colder than dread and you need cold. Here is the city's grief, arriving the way it always has, a steady inheritance of the unseen-home dead. Here is the Far-Shore Circle, gathering it into a battery, asking nothing the bereaved are not desperate to give. And here are the two demons drawing the battery down toward opposite ruins: the Healer, your brother's tenant, thinning the Kita membrane gently so the dead can be let through clean and the Thirteenth Seat filled with a quiet you would almost mistake for kindness; and the Inverted God's aspirant, tearing at the same wall so the held breath breaks all at once and the muzzle comes off the dead forever. One wants a door. The other wants a wound. They are fighting over the same wall and feeding from the same room.

What stops your breath on the dark street is the junction — the place where, in any honest diagram, you draw the one mark that matters: where could the current be cut. And the answer is not the Healer, because you cannot bring yourself to cut Gin. The answer is not Sora, because his sermon is built of true griefs and martyring him would only fill the ring faster. The answer is the battery itself, the circle, Kanna-sensei and the thirty-five lightened faces — and cutting there means giving every one of those people their grief back, un-soothing the man from Sumida and the widow and the mother, tearing peace out of the bereaved to starve the two catastrophes of their shared fuel. The cleanest cut in the whole diagram runs straight through the most innocent room in it. You have read enough bodies to know that the cause of death is rarely where the cruelty is, and the cruelty here is that mercy was the supply line all along.

Your phone has been warm in your pocket the whole audience and you take it out now to find what you knew you would find — a text from the unknown number that texts in your brother's voice, sent while you were drinking tea you should not have drunk. It says only: *You found the circle. Good. I was going to send you there next week.* And under it, after a pause you can feel even in the timestamp: *Don't cut the battery, Rei. I need it gentle. So do they.* It is the most reasonable thing the Healer has ever said to you, and it is asking you to leave the supply line running, and the worst part — the part that keeps you standing very still against the shutter while the cicadas saw the dark to pieces — is that on this one cold point, your brother's murderer and your brother's mercy and your own exhausted heart are all, for the first time, agreed.

The choice it leaves you
Resolve to cut the battery — starve both catastrophes, whatever it costs the ring.
It is the only mark on the diagram that ends both ruins at once, and it runs through thirty-five people who did nothing but grieve. Choosing it means becoming, for them, the thing that took their peace — and learning whether a mercy can be un-given without killing the host it runs through.
Leave the battery running and follow the gentle draw to Gin instead.
If the Healer needs it gentle, then the gentle draw is a thread that leads to your brother's reaching hand. Pulling it keeps the ring intact and puts you, at last, on a path to Gin himself — but it means agreeing with the demon that the supply line should live, and every night you wait it charges higher.
Take the diagram to Kurosawa — let someone who reads bodies read this one with you.
You have been carrying the whole wiring alone, and Kurosawa has read the marks the Healer leaves before. Sharing it means you are no longer the only mind that knows where the cut is — protection and exposure at once, because a secret two people hold is a secret a demon can reach through either of them.
Chapter Sixteen — The Counter-Demon Surfaces written

The Inverted God's aspirant steps out of his sermons and into a body. Yushiro is hunting him. You are the thing they both keep walking through.

The basement under the laundromat

The address Kanna-sensei would not give you arrives the way the worst addresses always do — not on paper but in the body, a man on the Ōedo line at 1:10 in the morning who is crying without any expression on his face, the tears running clean down a stillness that should not be able to make them. You follow him because you have learned to follow the wrongness, off at Ushigome-Kagurazaka, down a side street that smells of bleach and standing water, to a laundromat that has been closed long enough for the machines to fur with dust, and behind it a basement door propped with a rice cooker. The crying man goes down. You count to forty, the way you would on a stairwell that might collapse, and you go down after him into a low room lit by the screens of a dozen phones face-up on the floor, each one playing a different person's grief on mute, and standing in the middle of all that silent weeping is a young man in a delivery uniform who is not, you understand at once, the young man whose uniform it is.

He turns to you and the room turns with him, the way a register turns, and you feel the second voice in your own chest stir and answer before you can stop it — recognition, the awful kin-recognition of one suppressed thing meeting another that has stopped suppressing. "You're the brother," he says, and his borrowed mouth is gentle, almost shy, a courier's politeness wrapped around something that has eaten its way up through the floor of the world. "The Healer's brother. He talks about you. He thinks if he's careful enough you'll thank him in the end." The phones on the floor keep weeping. The crying man has folded himself into a corner and gone slack, used up, a battery the aspirant has already half-drained. "I'm not going to be careful," the thing in the courier says, and it is not a threat, it is a confession, said with relief. "Careful is the lie. The wall doesn't want to be a door. It wants to fall."

You should be afraid and you are, but underneath the fear is the diagram you drew on the dark street, snapping into a brighter resolution, because here is the second terminal made flesh — the Inverted God's aspirant, the counter-demon, the one tearing at the Kita membrane so it breaks all at once instead of opening clean. He is younger than the Healer's logic and louder than it, a grief that wants to be a flood and not a faucet, and he is feeding from the exact same room of mourners your brother is feeding from, two demons drinking from one cup and arguing only about how to spill it. He watches you do the math behind your eyes. "You see it," he says, pleased, terrible. "You're the only one who sees both of us at once. That makes you the most useful person in Tokyo. To him. And to me."

The choice it leaves you
Ask him outright what he wants the wall to do — let him preach, and listen for the seam.
A demon explaining itself is a demon spending the truth, and the seam where its logic doesn't close is the only handle you'll ever get. But a sermon built of real griefs is designed to convert the listener, and you came down here with a second voice that already answered his hello.
Back up the stairs now — you have a face and an address, and staying means being read in return.
You learned what you came to learn: the counter-demon has a body, a method, and a hunger pointed at the same battery. Leaving keeps the diagram in your head and out of his. But the crying man in the corner does not get to leave, and you will hear him on the platform for weeks.
Tell him the Healer is hunting him — and watch what fear in a fearless thing looks like.
Letting the aspirant know Yushiro is on him might turn two catastrophes against each other and buy the city time. It also hands a demon a true and useful fact, and tells him exactly how much you know about your own brother's hunt — which is the one thing you should never let either of them learn.
What the sermon is made of

He preaches, because that is what he is for, and the terrible thing is not that he lies but that he doesn't. He tells you about the held dead the way you would tell a friend about a cruelty you witnessed and could not stop — the unseen-home dead, the ones who died unregistered and unmourned and got no rite, kept the whole time behind the Kita membrane not at rest but at attention, a hundred years of breath held in a city that decided their grief was a public-health problem to be managed quietly. He tells you the Healer's plan is worse than his own because it is kinder: a clean door, opened gently, lets the institutions keep the schedule, keeps the membrane a managed valve, keeps the dead on a permitted release. "He wants to file them out," the aspirant says, and for the first time his borrowed voice has an edge. "One reasonable form at a time. I want to open my hands and let them go all at once and never be filed again."

You feel the seam then, the place his logic doesn't close, and you keep your face still over the second voice that wants to nod. The seam is the living. He has done the arithmetic of the dead with real tenderness and he has left the breathing entirely out of it — the city of nine million who will be standing in the flood when the held breath breaks, the mothers and the couriers and the man crying in the corner, all of whom will inherit a hundred years of unmuzzled grief in a single August night. "And us," you say, the first thing you have said in minutes. "The ones still alive. You've costed the dead to the yen and you've priced us at zero." He looks at you with something that might, in the real courier's face, have been the beginning of doubt, and then it closes over, because a demon that admitted the cost would have to stop. "They were priced at zero first," he says softly. "I'm only matching the books."

It is the most honest thing anyone has said to you since the kitchen at 3:47, and it is wrong in the specific way that the most honest things can be wrong, by being true about everything except the one life standing in front of it. You understand now why the Healer is hunting him and why the Healer is afraid: not because the aspirant is stronger but because he is purer, a grief with no use for the gentle lie, and your brother's whole reasonable architecture depends on the lie holding. Two demons, one cup. One wants to pour it out a careful drop at a time forever. One wants to throw it. And both of them have just looked at you and decided, in the same breath, that you are the table the cup is standing on.

The choice it leaves you
Name the seam to his face — make him hear the living he left out of the ledger.
Saying it aloud might lodge in him the way a splinter lodges, the one cost his sermon can't metabolize. Or it might simply teach him which argument to armor next time — and confirm to a demon that you, specifically, are the mind that finds the seam.
Pocket the seam and say nothing — keep the one handle you have hidden.
The living, left off his books, is the lever you might one day pull. Lever shown is lever lost. But staying silent means letting him preach unopposed in your own chest, where the second voice keeps quiet company with his.
Ask who he was before — find the courier under the flood.
There is a real young man in that uniform, ridden, maybe reachable, and a name is a thread back to a person. But asking tells the aspirant you intend to separate him from his host, and a cornered grief protects its mount the way a drowning man protects the thing keeping him up.
The Healer arrives wearing your brother

You feel Gin before you see him, the way you have learned to feel the gentle register move into a room — a lowering of the air's resistance, a sense that everything sharp has been asked, politely, to be softer. The basement door at the top of the stairs does not open so much as agree to be open, and your brother comes down into the weeping light, and it is your brother, it is Gin's face and Gin's careful hands and Gin's way of pausing on a step to make sure of his footing, and it is the Healer wearing all of it like a coat tailored years ago and only now worn out into the rain. The aspirant goes very still. The phones on the floor flutter, all of them, the muted griefs leaning toward the new arrival like grass toward weather. "Sora," your brother says, gently, to the thing in the courier, using the host's name like a man who has done his reading. "You're frightening my brother."

What happens next is the worst thing you have watched since you started watching, because it is not a fight.
