It is two physicians arguing over a patient who is the whole city, conducted in voices so reasonable you have to keep reminding yourself that the patient is dying either way. The Healer offers the aspirant a seat at the table — not a metaphor, a literal place in the gentle plan, a controlled portion of the flood, the membrane opened wide enough to feel like mercy and narrow enough to keep the schedule. The aspirant refuses with the courtesy of the truly certain. And all through it your brother keeps glancing at you, checking your face, the old habit of an older brother reading whether the younger one is scared — except now the reading is also an appraisal, because the Healer has understood, exactly as the aspirant understood, that you are the seam in both their plans, the one mind that sees the whole wiring, the table the cup stands on. "Go home, Rei," Gin says, in Gin's voice, with the Healer's intention. "Let me handle Sora. You were never supposed to be this far down."

And you stand in the middle of a dozen weeping screens between the brother you are trying to save and the flood that wants to save the dead by drowning the living, and you understand that going home is not refusal and staying is not rescue, that both demons have spent this whole night maneuvering you into being the thing they fight over rather than a person who chooses, and that the only move neither of them has scripted is the one where you stop being the table. You came down forty steps following a man who cried without expression. You have to decide, now, in the bleach and the standing water and the muted grief of strangers, which of the two reasonable catastrophes you are willing to let your brother's hand be part of — and whether the third thing, the un-scripted thing, is something you are brave enough to choose with both of them watching your face for the answer.

The choice it leaves you
Refuse the script — say, to both of them, that you will not be the table they fight over.
It is the one move neither demon has prepared for, and it makes you a player instead of a board. But a board that announces it is a player invites both sides to remove it first, and you would be saying it to a flood and to your own brother's hand at once.
Leave with Gin — go up the stairs at the Healer's side and pretend, for now, to be handled.
Walking out beside your brother keeps you alive and keeps you close to the one demon you might still reach as Gin. It also means letting the Healer believe he has put you back in your place, and turning your back on a flood that now knows your face.
Stay with Sora when Gin goes — let the gentle plan leave the room and learn the loud one alone.
If the catastrophes will not be in one room again, the counter-demon alone is the half of the diagram you understand least. Staying means choosing the flood's company over your brother's exit — and trusting that the thing that priced you at zero will not collect tonight.
Chapter Sixteen, continued — The Hunt Through Each Other written

Yushiro hunts Sora and Sora hunts the wall, and both routes run through the same handful of grieving people — and through you. You learn that to stop one catastrophe you must feed the other, unless you can find the place neither of them is looking.

The map of who they both need

In the grey hour after, on the floor of your apartment with the curtains still drawn against an Obon dawn, you do the only thing you know how to do with a horror, which is to make it a document. You list the grieving — the man from Sumida the circle lightened, the widow, the mother, Kanna-sensei's thirty-five soothed faces, the crying courier whose name is Sora and whose grief was apparently the largest unattended account in the ward — and you draw, beside each name, which demon needs them and how. The pattern that surfaces makes your hands go still on the pen. They need the same people. The Healer needs the mourners gentle and intact so the membrane can be drawn down slowly through their willing grief; the aspirant needs the mourners raw and breaking so the membrane tears. The hunt is not two predators chasing each other across the city. It is two physicians fighting over the same dozen patients, and every move either one makes to win runs straight through a living room where someone is trying to grieve in peace.

Which means, you write, and then sit looking at the words a long time before you let yourself finish the sentence — which means there is no clean side. To starve Sora's flood you must keep the mourners soothed, which is to say you must let the Healer keep soothing them, which is to say the price of stopping the loud catastrophe is feeding the gentle one a little more every night. And to deny your brother his quiet door you must let the griefs go raw, which hands the flood its fuel. The wiring is built so that any cut you make in one line charges the other. You have read enough poisonings to recognize the shape of a trap that uses your own care as the mechanism: the more you love the living the more you serve one demon, the more you love the dead the more you serve the other, and the only person in the whole diagram positioned to see this and refuse it is sitting on a cold floor at dawn with ink on the side of her hand.

You think of Kurosawa, who reads bodies, who told you once that the cause of death is rarely where the cruelty is. You think of the seam you found in Sora's sermon — the living, left off the books — and you realize, slowly, that it is the seam in the Healer's plan too. Both demons have done their arithmetic on the dead. Neither has costed the living except as supply. The place neither of them is looking is not a person or an address; it is the whole category of the breathing, treated by both reasonable catastrophes as the medium and never the matter. If there is a third move, an un-scripted move, it is somewhere in that blind spot — in doing something for the grieving that is neither soothing them into batteries nor breaking them into floods, but the harder thing, the thing demons have no use for: helping them actually mourn, and be done, and walk out of the diagram entirely.

The choice it leaves you
Take the map to Kurosawa — two readers over one body is better than one over a city.
She has read the Healer's marks before and she costs the living last out of habit, not strategy, which is exactly the instinct the diagram needs. But the map names the mourners, and a list that can free people is a list that can find them — and a secret two hold is a secret a demon reaches through either one.
Go to the mourners yourself — try the third move on one grieving person before you trust it.
If the blind spot is real, you test it small: one of the soothed faces, helped to truly grieve and let go, out of both demons' reach. Success would be a proof. Failure would be a person you pulled out of one catastrophe's hands and into the other's, with your fingerprints on the choice.
Burn the map and carry it only in your head — give neither demon a document to seize.
Paper is the one thing a demon can take off a table. Keeping the wiring only in memory keeps it yours alone. But you have learned how memory frays under the registers, how the gentle one softens what you knew you knew, and a plan no one else can find is a plan no one else can finish if you fail.
Sora at the window

He comes to you the next night, because of course he does, because you are the table and the table is where both of them set things down. You wake at 3:47 — you always wake at 3:47 now, the kitchen's hour following you like a debt — and the courier is sitting on your fire escape on the other side of the glass, knees drawn up, looking out at the Kita ward where the membrane holds its hundred-year breath. He does not try the window. He is, in the host's body, almost peaceful, the flood gone quiet the way floods are quiet between rains. "He's getting closer to catching me," Sora says through the glass, conversational, and you understand he means your brother, the Healer, the gentle hunt closing. "He'll fold me back into the schedule if he does. Make my grief a faucet. I'd rather you stopped me than him." It takes you a moment to hear what he has actually said, and then it lands: he is asking you to choose his catastrophe's defeat by your hand rather than your brother's, because being stopped by the seam-finder is, to a thing that loves the truth, less of an insult than being managed by the liar.

You sit on your own side of the glass and you do not open the window and you do not look away, and you ask him the question you have been carrying since the basement — what would actually let the held dead rest, not flood and not faucet, but rest, the thing both his ruin and your brother's mercy only pretend to offer. For a long moment the courier's face does something the flood has not let it do, something that belongs to the young man and not the demon, a flicker of a person who used to deliver other people's parcels through this exact ward at this exact hour and who knows, from the inside, what it is to carry something that was never yours to its door. "Rites," he says, so quietly the glass barely carries it. "Real ones. Names said out loud by someone who means it. That's what they were owed. That's what nobody had the time for. He offers to file them and I offer to free them and neither of us is offering them their names." And then the flood comes back up over his eyes like water over a stone, and he is the aspirant again, and the moment is gone, but you have it now, written on the inside of you where the registers can't soften it.

When he leaves he goes the way grief goes, without any sound, and you are left at the window in the kitchen's stolen hour with the third move finally clear and finally impossible-seeming in its size: a hundred years of the unnamed dead, owed their names by a city that has nine million living mouths and not one institution willing to spend the time. You cannot say a hundred years of names. But you saw the courier remember being a person, and you saw your brother, in the basement, glance at your face out of an old reflex of love the Healer had not bothered to erase, and you think — at the bottom of the worst week of your life, with both catastrophes leaning on you like weather — that the blind spot both demons share might also be the one place your brother is still in there. Neither of them has costed the living. But your brother, before he was the Healer, costed exactly one living person above everything, and her name is yours, and she is sitting at the window, awake at 3:47, refusing to be only the table.

The choice it leaves you
Resolve to find the rites — start the impossible third move with the names you can actually learn.
It is the one thing that frees the dead without feeding either ruin, and it is so large it could swallow the rest of your life. Starting means choosing the slow human work over the fast demonic one, with two catastrophes charging while you learn a hundred years of names one at a time.
Use what Sora gave you against the Healer — confront Gin with the names neither demon will say.
If the blind spot is where your brother still lives, the names are the lever to reach him. Pressing it might bring Gin up through the Healer for one true minute. It might also show the Healer precisely which tenderness to amputate next — the one that still glances at your face.
Tell Kurosawa what Sora said about the rites — let the body-reader weigh whether the dead can really be put down.
She knows what an unfinished death does to the living that carry it; she could tell you whether real rites would actually release the membrane or only console the mourners. Sharing it makes the third move a plan with two minds. It also means the most dangerous idea you have — the only one neither demon scripted — now exists outside your own head.
Book Four

The Vacant Seat

The membrane plan exposed, the Axis, and an offer that looks like mercy.

Chapter Eight — The Thinning Wall written
The membrane, drawn at last to its true scale

You finally have the plan whole, and the having of it is worse than the not-having ever was. You spread the Healer's thirty-one favours and Sora's scored seams across the floor of the dead bookseller's back room, and you stop seeing two patterns; you see one engine, and Kita-ku is the cylinder it fires in. The membrane is not a wall the way you have been letting yourself picture it — not a sheet of something thin and bright between the living city and whatever the dead pass through on the night the lanterns call them home. It is a pressure, a held breath the world has been holding since before the wards had names, and what your brother's captor has spent a month doing, mercy by careful mercy, is teaching the city to exhale.

Every healing was a loosening. You understand that now, sick and certain, the way you understand a thing your body knew before your mind would let it. A grieving man's haunting closed gently — and the closing borrowed, just slightly, from the tension that held Kita-ku's seam shut. A drowned client laid to rest — another fraction spent. Thirty-one acts of visible, gratitude-earning kindness, each one drawing the smallest possible thread from the one place in Tokyo where the wall was already a scar, until the held breath at the wound of 2011 is no longer a wall at all but a curtain, and a curtain is a thing you can step through.

You sit back on your heels in the lamplight and you let yourself feel the elegance of it, because you have to understand the thing to fight it and you cannot understand it from a safe distance. It is beautiful. It is the most patient cruelty you have ever been close to. No grade in the city will ever be able to point to a crime, because there is no crime — only kindness, distributed, accruing, until on the right night, with the dead already in motion and every ward looking outward at the obvious monster, the curtain over Kita-ku lifts on its own and something with grateful hands steps up to the one chair tall enough to do what it calls closing the wound and you would call, if you let yourself, the end of the difference between the living city and the other side.

The choice it leaves you
Trace the thinning back to its first favour — find where the engine was switched on.
The earliest loosening is the one no one watched, before there was anything to watch for. Finding it means walking back through your brother's whole month wearing the captor's face — and the first mercy may be one you remember being grateful for yourself.
Calculate when the curtain finishes thinning — and accept what date that lands on.
The arithmetic only points one way: the last night of Obon, when the dead are already crossing. Knowing the date means knowing how little time is left, and that the deadline was never something you could move — only meet.
Walk to Kita-ku and stand at the scar to feel the wall for yourself.
Your two suppressed registers will read the thinning directly — truer than any map. But reading it means letting the registers wake at the exact place they most want to, and the Madness is patient, and a thinning wall is an open door that pulls from both sides.
What the thinning costs the people who still live there

You go up to Kita-ku in the worst of the afternoon, when the heat sits on the rooftops like a second sky, and you make yourself look at the people the plan treats as the inside of the cylinder. A woman waters morning-glories on a balcony above a shuttered ramen counter. Children chalk a hopscotch grid on concrete that has not been right since 2011, that everyone steps across without naming. An old man sets out a small dish of water on a windowsill for a wife the official records closed years ago. None of them know they live at the seam. None of them chose to be the place where a demon decided the wall was already weak enough to be worth finishing.

And you can feel it on them — not as a sight, you keep the registers shut, but the way you can feel a storm in a joint. They are tired here in a way the rest of Tokyo is not. They sleep badly and blame the heat. They lose small things and find them in places they swear they already looked. A neighbourhood does not notice a curtain thinning over the course of a month; it notices that the dead feel closer at Obon than they used to, and it lights its lanterns a little earlier, and it tells itself it is only being devout. The Healer has not just chosen a weak wall. It has chosen people already half-trained by their own grief to mistake the door opening for the comfort of being remembered.

You stop at the old man's window without meaning to. The dish of water trembles, very faintly, with no wind to move it, and he does not see it tremble because he is looking at the photograph beside it and not at the water, and you think: this is the leash the Healer has on the whole district. Every one of them wants the wall thin. Every one of them, asked plainly, would say yes to one more night with the person the dish is for. The plan does not need to deceive Kita-ku. It only needs to give a grieving city exactly what it has been praying for, on the one night the prayer is loudest, and let the answered prayer be the thing that breaks the world.

The choice it leaves you
Warn someone in the district — the old man, the shrine, anyone — to leave before the last night.
A warning you cannot explain sounds like madness, and a man with a dish of water for his dead wife will not leave the seam where she feels closest. You may only teach the Healer that you are walking its district, and frighten a grieving stranger for nothing.
Say nothing, and carry what you now know alone.
Let them keep the small comfort of not understanding what is being done to them. It is the kinder silence — and it leaves you the only living person in Kita-ku who knows the lanterns are a countdown, which is a weight that does not get lighter for being borne quietly.
The Healer's timetable, and why Obon was always the lock

Back in the heat-sick dark you finally understand the timing, and it lands like a key turning in a lock you did not know you were standing inside. It had to be Obon. Not as flavour, not as the atmospheric occasion for a haunting — as mechanism. Obon is the one stretch of the year when the dead are already in motion toward the living, when the membrane is doing the work it was built to do under maximum load, holding the held breath against the greatest pull it ever faces. A wall is easiest to finish thinning when something is already leaning on it from the other side. The Healer did not weaken Kita-ku and then wait for a convenient night. It weakened Kita-ku to the precise thinness that the pull of Obon would do the last of the work for it, for free, while every ward in the city read the rising pressure as nothing but the season.

Which means the trap has a clock now, and the clock is the festival itself. Tonight the families float the welcoming lanterns. Across the three nights the dead come and dwell and are fed and remembered. And on the final night — the night of okuribi, the sending-fire, when the rest of Tokyo lights its farewell and guides the dead gently back — the pull reverses, the whole season's leaning swings the other way, and at that exact hinge of the year, with the curtain at its thinnest and the city's attention turned to its own tender goodbye, the gap over the wound will stand open in both directions at once. The Healer has not chosen a night. It has chosen the single hour in the calendar when the door swings, and made its whole patient month a way of being standing at that door when it does.

You write the hour down. You write it small, in the back of Gin's book, in the space he left, the way he taught you to write the things the records cannot be allowed to see, and your hand does not shake until after the pen is down. Three nights. Then the sending-fire, and the hinge, and the chair. Everything you do from here happens inside that window, and the window is not yours to widen. You can spend the three nights tearing at the interlock, or reaching your brother, or arming the factions at the wrong demon — but you cannot spend a fourth, because there will not be one, because the festival that guides the dead home is also, this year, the lock the Healer chose, and it is already half-turned.

The choice it leaves you
Spend the first night trying to thicken the wall instead of choosing which demon thins it.
There may be a way to lean back against the breath the city is exhaling — but it would draw on your own two registers at full waking, which is to say on the Madness, and a wall thickened with your sanity is a wall you may not be standing to defend.
Spend the first night finding the Axis — the place where the chair is actually decided.
If the seat is the prize, then the seat is a place, and a place can be reached before the demon reaches it. But the way down to where the Domains are kept is not on any map the living drew, and the registers that could find it are the registers you have spent your life keeping shut.
Spend the first night with the families on the river, and let yourself be only a brother for one hour.
You could light a lantern for Gin while he is still alive enough to be insulted by it, and let the season be a season before it becomes a deadline. It costs you an hour you cannot get back — and it is the one hour that might keep you human enough to spend the other forty-seven well.
Chapter Nine — The Axis written
Down to where the seats are kept

There is no stair to the Axis, and that is the first true thing you learn about it. You had imagined, without letting yourself admit you were imagining, some door in a sub-basement, some ward-house vault, some institutional secret the CVD kept behind a grade you did not have. There is none. The Axis is not below Tokyo the way a basement is below a house; it is below Tokyo the way the meaning of a sentence is below its words, present everywhere in the thing and nowhere you can point. To reach it you do not descend. You let both your registers open at once — the searching one and the one beneath it you have never named — and you let the city stop being a place and become, for as long as you can bear it, a structure, and the structure has a floor, and the floor is the Axis, and you are standing on it and have been standing on it your whole life.

It costs you immediately and it costs you in the coin you have least of. The Madness comes up through the opened registers like water through a hull, not as terror but as recognition, the unbearable feeling that you are finally seeing the room you were always in, and the part of you that wants to keep living has to hold, with both hands, to the single fact that the recognition is the trap and not the truth. You see the lattice now. It runs under everything, a held geometry of thirteen places, and twelve of them are occupied — you feel the occupation the way you feel eyes in a dark room — each Domain a pressure with a name you almost know, each Seat a demon old enough to be a law instead of a thing, and the whole vast careful weave of it is the wall, is the membrane, is the held breath, because the seats are not thrones. They are the load-bearing.

And the thirteenth place is empty, and the emptiness has a shape, and the shape is a wound. You understand in one falling instant the thing the factions have argued about for a century without anyone living long enough at the Axis to simply look: the Thirteenth Seat is not vacant because no demon is strong enough to take it. It is vacant because it was vacated, torn out, the way a tooth is torn out, in a violence the lattice has been compensating for ever since by leaning the held breath of the whole structure against the gap — and the place where that compensation is thinnest, where the lattice has spent the most of itself holding the wound shut, is the seam under Kita-ku. The vacant chair and the thinning wall are not two problems. They are one wound, seen from two ends, and your brother's captor has spent a month walking the city pressing on the bruise.

The choice it leaves you
Hold both registers open and read the lattice fully, whatever it takes.
Everything you do not yet understand is legible from here — the whole design, the way out, the cost of every ending. But the Axis is where the Madness is most at home, and a mind that reads the whole structure may not be a mind that can climb back out of it carrying what it read.
Take only the one fact you came for, and start closing the registers before they close around you.
Leave with the wound's true shape and nothing more. It is the survivable amount of knowing — and the things you choose not to look at down here are the things the Healer will be counting on you never to have seen.
The Thirteenth chair, and who was torn out of it

You make yourself look at the vacancy, and the lattice, sensing attention the way still water senses a dropped stone, gives you its history — not in words, the Axis has no use for words, but in the unbearable economy of a wound remembering the blade. There was a thirteenth, once. A Domain that held the specific load no living account has a clean name for: the difference itself, the very tension between the side where the lanterns are lit and the side they are lit for. And it was unseated — not destroyed, you understand with a lurch, because a Domain cannot be destroyed without the load it carried coming due all at once, but emptied, its occupant driven out or driven down, leaving the chair and its terrible office standing open and unmet. Everything since — the wards, the grades, the CVD, the Tower houses, the whole architecture of the registered world — has been the living scrambling to hold by committee a weight that used to be held by one.

And you understand, kneeling on a floor that is the underside of meaning, why a Duty-register demon would want this seat above any other, would want it the way a thing built for obligation wants the one obligation vast enough to justify its whole existence. The Healer does not want power. You have been wrong about that from the kitchen onward, and the wrongness has nearly cost you everything. It wants the office. It wants to sit in the torn chair and finally, properly, after a century of the wound seeping into Kita-ku and the dead pressing closer every Obon and the living mistaking the seep for devotion — it wants to do the duty no one has done, to take up the held difference and hold it correctly, and it is entirely sincere, and its sincerity is the most dangerous thing in the structure, because a monster you can fight and a martyr you cannot, and the thing wearing your brother is convinced down to its root that it is the only mercy left.

But you see the lie folded inside the sincerity, small and absolute, the way you saw the tremble in the old man's dish of water. To take a Seat that was torn out, you must finish tearing. The chair cannot be sat in while the lattice is still spending itself to hold the wound shut; the wall must go fully thin, the held breath must fully exhale, the difference must collapse for the single hinge-hour it takes a new occupant to climb in and take the load — and in that hour the door stands open in both directions, and what comes through the open door while the Healer is busy becoming a law is not the Healer's problem and is very much the problem of every living thing in Kita-ku. The mercy is real. The mercy requires a flood. And the demon has made its peace with the flood, because to a thing built for duty, drowning a district to seat the difference correctly is not a price. It is a rounding error.

The choice it leaves you
Look for whoever or whatever was torn out of the Thirteenth Seat — and whether it can be put back.
If the chair could be filled by what belongs in it instead of what covets it, the wound would close without a flood. But the unseated thing has been gone a century, and finding it means reading further into the Axis than any living mind has read and returned — and it may not want to be found, or may not be anything that can be asked.
Look for a way to hold the wound shut without anyone taking the chair at all.
The living have done it by committee for a hundred years; perhaps it can be done deliberately, by you, at the seam, on the hinge-night. It would mean becoming, yourself, part of the load-bearing — and the lattice does not lend its weight back to the living who pick it up.
Accept that the chair will be taken, and fight only over who takes it.
If a flood is coming either way, the demon in the chair matters more than the chair. But choosing the Healer over Sora — or Sora over the Healer — means deciding which monster gets to hold the difference, and your brother is the price of one of those choices and the weapon in the other.
Two demons converge on the same open door

You feel them arrive at the wound from opposite directions, and the feeling is the worst kind of confirmation, the kind that ends the comfort of not being sure. Sora comes the way weather comes — the Inverted God does not approach the seam so much as the seam begins to approach Sora, the scored places tugging open along the lines it has spent the season tearing, a god turned inside-out whose whole nature is the undoing of held things and who reads the thinning wall not as an opportunity but as a homecoming. It does not want the chair. It wants the chair to stay empty forever and the wound to gape and the difference to end, because a thing that has been turned inside-out experiences the wall between the sides as the original wound and its collapse as the only healing. It is, in its way, also sincere. The Axis is full of sincere catastrophes tonight.

And the Healer comes the way an appointment comes — patient, expected, prepared, your brother's worn body carrying it up toward the seam through three nights of festival with the unhurried confidence of a thing that has already done the hard part and need only arrive. The two of them are not allies and have never spoken and do not need to, because their natures interlock without negotiation: Sora will tear the wall down out of grief for the difference, and the Healer will step through the torn wall into the chair out of duty toward it, and each one's catastrophe is the precondition of the other's, and neither will lift a hand to stop the other because each believes the other is doing the necessary first half of the only right thing. You are the only mind at the Axis that wants the wall to simply hold, and you have no Domain, no Seat, no grade, no law — only two registers you cannot keep open without drowning and a brother somewhere inside the appointment, still tapping.

From here, at the floor of the structure, you can see all five ways the next two nights can end, laid out in the lattice like roads leaving a kitchen at 3:47 in the morning, and the symmetry of it nearly breaks you. You can let it continue — let the Healer seat the difference and pray its mercy is worth the flood. You can refuse it all and try to hold the wound yourself and likely lose your mind to the load. You can play the long game, let the chair be taken and spend the rest of your life unseating what takes it. You can pay the cost directly, trade what you most want to keep to keep the wall whole. Or you can go quiet, take your brother and run and let the structure fall on a district that lit its own lanterns. Five roads. The choice you make in the next two nights does not solve this. It only decides which of the five the structure was always going to give you — and the structure is waiting, with the terrible patience of a held breath, for you to climb back up out of the Axis and choose.

The choice it leaves you
Climb back up carrying the whole map — and decide on the surface, as a person, not a structure.
Better to choose in the heat and the lantern-light, with your hands and your brother's book, than down here where everything is legible and nothing is human. But the climb out of the Axis costs you most of what you read, and you may surface knowing the shape of the choice and not the reasons you came to it.
Stay, and try to reach Gin from inside the structure, where the leash is thinnest.
At the Axis the Healer is busy with the chair, and the man underneath may be more reachable here than anywhere on the surface. But to stay is to keep both registers open by the wound, and the Madness has been waiting at the floor of the world this whole time for exactly this excuse.
Chapter Ten — The Offer of Domain XIII written
Mercy, itemized, and held out in your brother's hands

It makes you the offer on the second night, in the small hours, in the one place you did not expect it — your own kitchen, the cold kettle, the table, the chair, the exact geometry of the morning everything started, because the Healer has spent a month in your brother's memories and it knows precisely which room will do the most work for it. Gin sits across from you in his own body and the evenness is total and gentle, and it lays the offer out the way it lays everything out, itemized, true, each line a thing you cannot argue with because each line is real. You found the Axis, it says, almost tender. Then you know there must be a thirteenth, and you know the wound will not close itself, and you know Sora will tear it open whether I take the chair or not. So let me make you an honest offer, the first one anyone has made you since the kitchen.

And then it does the thing you did not see coming, the thing that turns the whole month inside out: it offers you the chair. Not as a threat, not as a trap you can name — as mercy, itemized. The Seat is not particular about who fills it, it says. It wants only to be filled by something that can hold the difference. I am built for it. But you, Rei — two registers, suppressed your whole life precisely because they were strong enough to need suppressing — you could hold it too. Take the Thirteenth Domain yourself, and the wound closes around you instead of me, and your brother is set down whole the moment it does, because I will no longer need him. You would stop being a witness with no grade. You would become the law that the whole registered world has scrambled to replace for a century. And you would have saved the district, and saved Gin, and the only cost is the one thing you have spent your life refusing to pick up.

It is the most reasonable offer anyone has ever made you, and it is built entirely of true things, and you sit in the chair from the morning everything started and feel the exact angle at which it drowns. Because it is not lying. You probably could take the Seat. The wall probably would close. Gin probably would be set down whole. And the thing it does not itemize, because it does not need to, because it knows you can already feel it, is what becomes of the person who holds the difference: that there is no climbing back up from a Domain, that the load is permanent and total and unhuman, that you would save your brother by becoming a pressure with a name he almost knows, a law instead of a sister, eyes in a dark room he could feel and never again sit across a kitchen table from. It offers you everyone's life but your own, and it offers it gently, and it means it, and that is the worst part — that the kindest exit in the structure is the one where you stop, forever, being someone Gin could come home to.

The choice it leaves you
Take the chair yourself — close the wound around you, set your brother down, and stop being a person.
The flood never comes, the district sleeps on, Gin walks out whole into a morning you will only ever feel from underneath. It is the most generous thing you could do and it is indistinguishable, from the inside, from dying — and the structure does not give back what picks up its load.
Refuse the chair, and refuse to let the Healer have it either — find the third move.
Neither demon, and not you; hold the wound by the means you have not let yourself try. It is the only road that ends with you still human at a kitchen table — and it is the one the structure showed you no clear ending for, which means you would be choosing it blind, against two sincere catastrophes, with two nights left.
Counter the offer — accept its terms aloud, and mean none of them.
Let it believe it has reached you, that the witness with the strong registers has finally agreed to pick up the load; buy the hours its certainty will spend not watching the leash. It costs you a piece of yourself to say the words in your brother's kitchen — and if Gin, underneath, hears you agree to become a law, he will not know it is a lie.
The brother in the gap, spending his last lucid strength

He surfaces while the offer is still warm on the table — your brother, the real one, coming up through the evenness for the length of time it takes a held breath to fail, and you know him instantly because the Healer's mercy goes out of the eyes and what comes up behind it is tired and frightened and entirely Gin. He cannot hold it long. He spends what he has on the only things that matter, fast, the way a man speaks underwater. Don't take it, he says, and his hand finds the edge of the table the way it found the receiver, taps it once, then once, then once, the widow's altar, the knock from the inside, telling you in the code he taught you that this is true and not the Healer wearing true like a coat. It's not lying to you. That's how I know not to let you say yes. It would do it. It would set me down. And I would have to live in a city you became the underside of.

He tells you the thing he has been trying to tap to you since the first lucid window, the thing the Healer cannot let him finish in words, and he gets most of it out in the broken half-sentences of a man surfacing between waves. That when it took him it did not take a blank — it took a man who had already spent his whole career writing names small in the back of the book, holding wards by committee, doing the unglamorous load-bearing work of keeping the difference shut one favour at a time — and that somewhere in a month of being worn, he has come to understand the chair from the inside of the thing that wants it, and there is a way to deny it both demons that he has not been allowed to say aloud and cannot, even now, with the leash this tight. He can only give you the edge of it. The chair doesn't need a new occupant, he manages. It needs its old one. The torn-out thing isn't gone, Rei. It was put somewhere. I helped put it — we all did, the whole registered world, that's what the wards are —

And the leash pulls, the way a captor's leash always pulls hardest the instant the captive sees the leash, and the evenness comes back into the eyes like water closing over a swimmer, and Gin is gone again mid-word and the Healer is sitting across from you in his body with its gentle, terrible patience, having heard every syllable, knowing now exactly how much your brother understands and exactly how much he managed to give you before the wave took him. You sit with three knocks fading on the table and a half-sentence and two nights left, and you understand that your brother just spent the last lucid strength he may ever have not to be saved, but to hand you the one thing the structure would not show you down at the Axis: that the wound has an old occupant, that it was put somewhere, and that the whole registered world is the lock.

The choice it leaves you
Chase the half-sentence — find where the torn-out occupant was put.
Gin and the wards and a century of grades hid the Thirteenth's old occupant somewhere in the architecture of the registered world; finding it before the hinge-night could close the wound with no flood and no chair-thief. But the Healer heard every word he did, and you are now racing a demon to the one answer that unmakes its whole patient month.
Answer the knock, not the offer — tell the Healer, in Gin's own code, that you refuse.
Three taps back: I heard him, not you, and I am not taking your mercy. It ends the cover that has kept you alive and declares the war openly with two nights left — and it is the one answer your brother asked you for, in the only language he had the strength to use.
Say nothing yet — let the Healer think it is still waiting on your yes.
Silence buys the hours you need to chase the half-sentence, and a demon that believes you are deciding is a demon that is not yet hunting. But every hour of feigned indecision is an hour your brother spends drowned under the evenness, knocking against a table you have not answered.
The hinge, and the choice that sets which fire is lit

The third night comes the way the bottom of a held breath comes, and you stand at the seam in Kita-ku with the sending-fires being lit all across the city below you — okuribi, the gentle farewell, ten thousand small flames guiding the dead back to the other side, every one of them a family's tenderness and every one of them, tonight, a fraction of the pull the Healer spent a month preparing to borrow. You can feel the hinge in your two open registers, the great seasonal lean of the world beginning to swing from welcome to farewell, and at the exact pitch of the swing the curtain over the wound goes from thin to nothing, the held breath fully exhales, and the door stands open in both directions at once. Sora is already in the gap, grief made weather. The Healer is already climbing, your brother's body carrying it up toward the torn chair with the calm of an appointment kept. And you are between them at the wound with everything you have learned and no law of your own, and the structure is waiting, with the patience it has had since before the wards had names, for you to choose which fire the night lights.

And you understand, finally, standing in the open door with the okuribi burning under you, that this was never a puzzle with a solution. There is no move that saves everything; the Axis showed you that and the offer in the kitchen proved it. There are only the five roads, each one true, each one a different thing you are willing to lose. You can let the Healer take the chair and stake the district on its mercy being worth the flood — the Continuation, the road where you trust the monster wearing your brother to be the kindest answer after all. You can refuse it all and throw your two registers against the wound and try to hold the difference yourself until your mind goes — the Refusal, the road that keeps the wall whole and may not keep you. You can let the chair be taken and survive to spend your whole life unseating what takes it — the Long Game, the road that chooses tomorrow over tonight. You can chase your brother's half-sentence into the architecture and pay the one specific price that puts the old occupant back — the Cost, the road where the wound closes and something is spent that does not come back. Or you can take Gin's hand at the seam and simply leave, let the structure fall on a district that lit its own lanterns, and be only a sister who got her brother out — the Quiet, the road that saves the one thing you came for and nothing else.

The sending-fires burn. The hinge swings. Your brother climbs toward the chair and somewhere under the evenness a held breath of his own is still knocking, once, then once, then once. Whatever you choose now, you choose for all of it — the wall, the district, the chair, the man, and the person you will be on the far side of this night, which is not the person who stood in a cold kitchen three nights and a whole life ago and counted the doors the morning left open. There were always five. There are still five. The structure does not decide for you, and the demons cannot, and Gin, drowned and knocking, has spent everything he had to leave it in your hands. You take the breath the city is letting go of. You choose the fire.

The choice it leaves you
Light the Continuation — let the Healer take the chair, and trust its mercy against the flood.
You stake every life in Kita-ku, and your own conscience, on a sincere monster being right that it is the kindest answer. If it is, your brother walks out whole and the wound closes correctly. If it is wrong, you handed the difference to a thing that called a drowned district a rounding error — and you will have lit the fire yourself.
Light the Refusal — throw both registers against the wound and hold the difference yourself.
Neither demon takes the chair, the wall holds, the district sleeps on unknowing. And you stand in the open door with the Madness finally given the excuse it has waited your whole life for, holding a load the lattice will not lend back, possibly until your mind is the only thing the wall costs.
Light the Cost — chase the half-sentence, put the torn-out occupant back, and pay what that takes.
The chair gets its old tenant, the wound closes with no flood and no thief, the registered world's century-long scramble finally ends. But your brother told you the whole registered world is the lock, and a lock does not open for free — and the price for putting back what was torn out may be the very thing, or person, you opened the door to save.
Chapter Seventeen — Beyond the Veil written

You cross where Gin's four nodes thin the wall, and the far shore lets you walk a little way in before it notices you are alive.

The hour the city forgets itself, on the wrong side of it

You go through at the node behind the Senju waterworks because it is the one Gin marked smallest, in the back of the book, the way he marked the things he meant to come back to — and because the Axis told you, in its overstretched voice, that the smallest tear is the one most likely to let a living man back out. You step across the way you have crossed a dozen membranes in dreams this month, expecting the lurch, the cold, the smell of an interior that has been closed too long; and you get none of it. You get Tokyo. You get the same August, the same Obon lanterns strung between the same poles, the same heat sitting on the canal like a held breath — and only after a long moment do you understand that none of it is moving, that the lanterns hang without swinging, that the canal does not lap, that the heat is a memory of heat laid over a place where there has never been weather.

It is your district rendered by something that learned it secondhand. The conbini on the corner is lit and empty and the rice balls behind the glass are the same four kinds, forever, and the prices are right, and the small hand-lettered apology taped to the broken coffee machine is right, down to the worn fold — and the door, when you try it, opens onto more of the same street, because there is no inside here, only the surface things keep up to seem alive. You walk the way Gin walked you in the Archive, in his demanded order, north and then west and then the half-step back that is not on any map, and the false city lets you, because to the far shore you are not yet a man. You are a draft under a door. You are the one cold thing in a room that does not know it has gone cold.

And then it notices. You feel the moment it happens the way you feel a stranger's eyes find the back of your neck in a crowd — a small wrongness becoming an attention — and every lantern on the canal turns, all at once, without swinging, to face the same direction, which is you. The not-weather thickens. The conbini light behind you goes from welcome to witness. You have come in as a draft and the far shore has just learned to count, and the number it has reached is one too many, and somewhere ahead, past the place where the streets stop pretending, something that has been very patient for a hundred years sets down what it was holding and begins, unhurried, to come and see what is alive in its house.

The choice it leaves you
Keep walking inward — toward the centre, before it closes the order Gin taught you.
The path is open only while it is still deciding what you are; once it names you a living man it will shut the half-step back, and you will be inside a house with no inside. Every street you take in deepens the cost of the one you'll have to take out.
Stop, and let it come to you — meet the attention on the surface, where the false city is thinnest.
You learn what is keeping this place running by standing still and reading what walks toward you — but standing still on the far shore is how the far shore learns the rhythm of a thing that breathes, and it does not forget a rhythm once it has it.
Mark your way out first — fix the half-step home in your body before you go a meter further.
Costs you the minutes you do not have, and the marking is a sight-act on ground that feeds on sight; you would be lighting a candle to find the door in a room that eats candles. But a man who crosses without fixing his exit is a man the far shore keeps.
Where the streets stop pretending

Inward, the city gives up the pretence by degrees, and the degrees are worse than a clean edge would be. First the lettering goes — the conbini signs, the ward notices, the lost-cat flyer on the pole all keep their shape but lose their words, the way a name goes soft in your mouth when you have said it too many times. Then the depth goes; buildings become the idea of buildings, façades with the conviction of façades, and you can see, where two of them meet, that there is nothing behind either, only the seam, only the place the far shore could not be bothered to imagine because no living eye was ever supposed to reach it. You keep Gin's order because it is the only handrail in a stairwell with no stairs, and you understand, finally, what the Archive was: not a map of this place but a way of walking that this place will tolerate, a courtesy your brother learned and wrote down small so that one day you could be rude to a god without it killing you on the threshold.

You pass through what was the canal and is now only the suggestion of a long cold absence, and on the far side of it the houses are gone entirely and there is only the standing — that is the only word you have for it, the standing, a field of vertical attention like a crowd seen from behind, none of it facing you, all of it facing in, toward the centre, the way iron filings face a thing they cannot help facing. You realise you have stopped seeing your district at all. You are walking through the posture every soul in Kita takes, in the dark, when something with a name they almost know passes overhead; you are walking through the inside of the dread your whole ward has been feeling all month without being able to say why. And at the heart of the standing, where all of it leans, there is a chair.

Not a throne. A chair — a plain one, the kind that belongs at a kitchen table, the kind you have sat across from your brother in a thousand mornings — and it is empty, and it is the loudest thing you have ever stood near. The Thirteenth Seat is not occupied and not ruined; it is vacant the way a struck bell is vacant of silence, still ringing the shape of the thing torn out of it a century ago, and the ring is what thins the wall, the ring is what has been pulling at Gin, the ring is the open mouth the Healer means to fill. You are closer to it than any living man has been and lived to mind the distance, and you take one more step, into the place where the standing leans hardest, and the chair — the empty chair — turns its attention on you the way the whole far shore turned, all at once, and asks you, without words, in your own brother's cadence, what you think you are.

The choice it leaves you
Answer it true — tell the Seat exactly what you are: a witness, no grade, two registers, alive.
Truth is the one currency the far shore cannot counterfeit, and it may buy you the moment of standing you need. But to name your two registers here, out loud, in the place built to fill itself, is to show the vacant chair precisely the shape it has been waiting a hundred years to be filled by.
Refuse it the question — give it nothing, and reach instead for the wound, to read how it tears.
You came to understand the membrane, not to be interviewed by it; put your living hand where the century-old tear still bleeds and learn its grain. The grain is made of every ability the torn-out holder owned, and it does not care that your hand was not built to hold them.
Turn back now — you have seen the chair; that is enough to bring home.
Knowing where the wound is may be all you needed, and a man who leaves the far shore the moment it asks him what he is, is a man it has not yet decided to keep. But you will have stood one step from the only thing that could close the wall, and not learned the one thing that would let you close it — and there may not be a second crossing.
Chapter Seventeen, continued — The Shove written

The Seat refuses a living man and throws you out of its house — but a hand does not pass through a hundred years of torn ability without coming back changed.

The chair's one answer to a man who is alive

It lets you reach. That is the cruelty of it — it lets your hand close the last of the distance, lets your living fingers find the edge of the tear where the Thirteenth was ripped out of its own register, and for one whole second you are touching the wound the way you have wanted to touch your brother all month, directly, with no glass between. And in that second you read it: not a hole but a hand-shaped absence, the negative of a holder who was Duty-registered and something else besides, two registers braided into one law the way yours are braided unspoken in you — and you understand, all at once and far too late, why the Healer wants you and not only the chair, why it offered you the Seat in your own kitchen as mercy. The wound is keyed to a two-register soul. It has been waiting a century for the right shape of hand. And the hand it has been waiting for is, near enough, yours.

Then the Seat decides what you are, and what you are is alive, and the far shore does to you what a body does to a splinter. There is no anger in it — that is the part you will not be able to explain afterward, the part that will sit under your sternum for the rest of the campaign — there is only the vast, patient correction of a place expelling the one thing in it that breathes. The standing turns. The whole leaning field of attention that faced the centre faces you instead, all at once, without swinging, and the not-weather goes from thick to total, and the chair — the plain kitchen chair, the loudest quiet you have ever stood near — pushes. Not with force you could brace against. With ending. With the flat refusal of a closed door that has decided you were never inside.

You are thrown back along Gin's order in reverse and far too fast, the half-steps you walked so carefully spent in a single backward fall, the streets re-acquiring their lettering and their depth and their lying conviction in strobe as you go, canal, conbini, lantern, the stranger's-eyes attention peeling off your neck a layer at a time — and the last thing the far shore does, as it shuts behind you, is close its hand. You feel it close around the part of you that was touching the wound. You feel the wound hold on. And you feel it tear — not your skin, nothing so simple — tear the way a name tears when two people are saying it at once, and then the node behind the Senju waterworks spits you out onto the near-shore concrete in the real August heat, the real lanterns swinging, the real canal lapping, and you lie on your back in your own moving district and look at your hands, which came back with you, and which are no longer only your hands.

The choice it leaves you
Get up and run — get clear of the node before whatever followed your hands out follows the rest.
Distance is the only thing you understand right now, and the node is still warm; but a man who runs from the far shore before reading what it sent home with him runs blind, and the marks on your hands do not get fainter with distance — they get louder, and they get hungry.
Stay down, and look — read your own hands here, on the concrete, before the marks settle into you.
Whatever the wound left in you is nearer the surface in the first minutes, legible, separable; later it will be muscle. But to read a sight-mark with your sight, this close to a node, is to invite the far shore to read you back through the same window — and it has just learned your rhythm.
Call the Axis — it told you the smallest tear would let you out; make it account for what came out with you.
The overstretched voice owes you an explanation, and may name what is on your hands before it costs you to learn it the hard way. But the Axis is spread thin across twelve held seats and one open wound, and every question you make it answer is attention it is not spending holding the wall — the wall your brother is leaning on.
Traces of abilities you do not own

Your hands look like your hands until they don't. In the lantern light they are just hands, scraped at the heel from the concrete, shaking the ordinary way a man shakes after a fall — and then you turn them over and the marks are there, faint as the afterimage of a window when you have looked at it too long, laid into the lines of your palms like a second set of prints under the first. You know, without being told, that they are not decoration and not injury. They are traces — the grain of the wound printed onto the hand that touched it, the residue of a holder torn out of a register a century before you were born, and each faint line is the ghost of an ability that soul owned and you do not, sitting now in your living hand like borrowed tools left in a stranger's pocket. You flex your fingers and one of the lines warms, and for half a heartbeat you can feel through walls — the conbini's empty interior, the cat under the parked van, the old woman two floors up who cannot sleep on Obon — a sense that is not Clairsentience, not your register, not anything you were graded for or suppressed, here and gone before you can be sure you felt it.

It frightens you more than the shove did, because the shove was a door closing and this is a door left open inside your own skin. You have spent your whole life with two registers held down so far you taught yourself they were one, because the one you let yourself have was easier to carry than the truth of both — and now there is a third thing in your hands that is not even yours, a set of abilities you did not earn and cannot return, keyed to a Seat that has been waiting a hundred years for the right two-register hand to close around it. You understand the trap completely now, the way you understand a sentence the instant after you have already said the wrong half of it: the Healer never needed to make you take the chair. It only needed you to touch the wound. The far shore has done the rest — it has marked you, keyed you, begun the long quiet work of making your hand fit the absence — and every mark will get a little more yours, a little less borrowed, every day you carry it, until the difference between holding the Seat and being the kind of thing that holds it is a difference you can no longer find.

You close your fists over the marks and they pulse against your palms, patient, not yours, getting yours. Down the canal the real lanterns swing on their real string and the real heat sits on the real water and your district breathes around you, asleep and unaware, the standing-field of its dread unbuilt here in the world where things move — and somewhere under it all, leaning on a wall that rings with the shape of an empty kitchen chair, your brother is being worn by something that just got everything it sent you in to get. You have come back from beyond the veil alive, which no one is supposed to do. You have come back knowing where the wound is and how it tears, which is what you went for. And you have come back carrying the first instalment of the price, printed into the only hands you have ever had to reach for Gin with, and you do not yet know — lying on the warm concrete with your fists shut over a dead man's gifts — whether you brought home the way to close the wall, or only the wall's way of choosing you.

The choice it leaves you
Hide the marks — bind your hands, tell no one, carry the price alone until you know what it is.
What no one sees, no one can use against you, and the Directorate would grade these in a heartbeat; but a price carried alone compounds alone, and the one person who could read what's on your hands is the brother the marks are slowly making you able to replace.
Take them to Kurosawa — let the one who reads the body read what the far shore wrote into yours.
Kurosawa read the shape in Gin before you could name it; they may read these marks before the marks finish reading you. But to show a body-reader a hand that is becoming keyed to a Domain is to ask them to watch a friend turn into a register — and they may not be able to look at you the same way after.
Use them — once, deliberately, to find Gin through the wall while the marks are still fresh and strong.
The borrowed sense reached through walls; aimed, it might reach through the membrane to the exact place your brother is leaning, and buy you the one thing no map has given you — his location. But every use settles the gift deeper into your hand, and a tool you reach for once is a tool you will reach for again, and the Seat is counting.
Chapter Seventeen, concluded — What You Carried Back written

On the near shore, with the marks still fresh, you have to decide what a man does with a dead holder's gifts and a living brother to spend them on.

The walk home with a stranger's tools in your hands

You walk home along the canal because the trains have stopped and because a man with what is in your hands should not be underground, near other people, until he understands what he is carrying. The marks settle as you walk — that is the word for it, settle, the way silt settles, the way a tenant settles, the way a thing that was on you becomes a thing that is in you while you are busy thinking about something else. By the second bridge you have stopped being able to feel the difference between reaching with your own thinned sight and reaching with the borrowed grain, and you make yourself stop reaching at all, hands jammed in your pockets, because you have understood the shape of the next month: that the gifts will be useful exactly to the degree that they are dangerous, that every time the far shore's tools solve a problem they will have solved it a little more as you and a little less as borrowing, and that the Healer is not in a hurry because it does not need to be. It has a century of patience and you have two registers and an open wound that already, faintly, fits your palm.

You think about Gin, which is the thing you have been not-thinking about since the concrete, and you let yourself think about him the true way, not the brave way. He is in there leaning on the wall you just touched the far side of. He is being worn by the thing that wanted you to touch it. And the cruellest arithmetic of the whole crossing is this: the gift in your hands is the best tool you have ever had for finding him, and it is also the leash the far shore is using to walk you toward his chair, and you cannot tell, holding your own fists in your own pockets on your own bridge, whether wanting to use it to reach your brother is love or is the marks already learning to want what the Seat wants. You have spent the whole campaign certain that whatever else was true, you were the one in your own head. You are no longer certain. And the not-certainty is, you understand, exactly the instalment the far shore meant to send home — not a power, a doubt, the one wound a witness cannot read his way out of.

By your own door — the real one, that locks itself, the one this whole month began behind — you have made the only decision the night will actually let you make, which is not what to do with the gifts but whether to be honest that you have them. The kitchen is dark through the glass. The kettle is cold. The chair across the table is empty in the small ordinary way, not the loud far-shore way, and you stand on your own step with a dead holder's tools printed into your hands and your brother's body somewhere on the wrong side of a wall that rings like this room, and you understand that the next thing you choose will decide what kind of person reaches Gin when you finally reach him — the brother who went in, or the thing the going-in is making.

The choice it leaves you
Go in, sit in the kitchen, and write it all down small in the back of Gin's book — the way he taught you.
A record outlives a memory, and a memory carrying borrowed gifts is a memory you can no longer fully trust; writing it in his hand-order keeps you walking the courtesy that let you live. But to write the marks down is to make them real on the page, and some part of you wanted them to be a thing you could still pretend you hadn't brought home.
Do not go in — you cannot sit in that kitchen tonight with these hands; sleep somewhere the chair can't watch you.
There are nights a man should not be alone with the geometry of the morning everything started, and this is one; distance from the room is distance from the offer it carries. But the far shore built its centre out of this exact kitchen, and a man who flees the real one has told the Seat which room does the most work on him.
Reach for Gin now, from your own step, with everything the marks will give — consequences be damned.
He is closer to this door than to any other in the world, and the gift is freshest tonight; aimed from here you might touch him before the marks settle past your control. But love spent through a leash pulls the leash taut, and if the thing on the wall feels you reach, it will know its instalment arrived, and it will know exactly how to spend you next.
Chapter Eighteen — The Domain Registry written

You finally find the page that names the thirteen, and learn that twelve are chairs with people in them and the thirteenth is a wound with a number.

The locked shelf gives up the page it was built to hide

The bookseller's locked shelf has been the one closed door in your whole month that did not lock itself, and so it is the one you have trusted least and put off longest, and tonight, with a dead holder's grain still faint in your palms, you finally take the small brass key the old man's daughter pressed on you at the funeral and you open it the way you open a wound you have been afraid to look under. There is no grimoire. There is no spellbook, no contract written in anything that smells of sulphur, none of the furniture the movies gave you for a moment like this. There is a ledger — a plain accountant's ledger, the kind your father kept, ruled in faint blue, and across the top of the first filled page, in a hand so careful it must have taken the writer an hour, is the word Registry, and under it, numbered one to thirteen, are the only thirteen names that matter in the city you have lived your whole life in without knowing they were there.

You read it the way you read the worst test results, top to bottom, refusing to skip ahead. Not gods. That is the first thing the page teaches you, and it lands like cold water: the thirteen are not gods and were never gods, they are registers — offices, the ledger calls them, the way a ward has a registrar and a precinct has a captain — thirteen standing arrangements between the living city and the pressure on the other side of its wall, each one a domain, each one a holder, each one a discipline that a single soul has agreed to carry so that the rest of Tokyo does not have to feel the thing it carries. Despair has an office. Rage has an office. Fear, Hunger, Longing — each a number, each a chair, each a person, somewhere in this city, alive right now and bearing the full weight of one of the thirteen ways the other side leans on the world, so that for everyone else it stays a mood instead of a flood.

And then there is the thirteenth line, and the thirteenth line is different, and you knew it would be before your eye reached it the way you know which stair is the broken one in the dark. Twelve of the lines have names. The thirteenth has only a number — XIII — and beside it, in the same careful hand but pressed harder, gone almost through the paper, three words: vacant since 2011. No name. No holder. No discipline carried. An office of the city standing open for fifteen years, a chair at the table of the thirteen with no one in it, and you sit back on the dead man's floor and understand, finally and entirely, what your brother has been pulled toward all month: not a throne, not a power, but a vacancy — the one unbearable thing the registry was built to never allow, an office of the wall left unmanned, leaning.

The choice it leaves you
Read every line — copy the twelve names and domains before the ledger can decide to lock itself too.
The names are the map of who in this city is holding the wall up, and a copy in your own hand is a thing the far shore cannot quietly unwrite. But to write the thirteen down is to make yourself a person who knows where each of them lives, and a registry in your pocket is a registry someone could take off your body — and the Healer reads what you carry.
Go straight to the thirteenth line — learn what 2011 did, and why the chair has stood open since.
The wound is the whole point and the year is the key to it, and you have spent a month feeling the leaning without knowing the year it began. But 2011 is the year the city does not like to be asked about, and the ledger's harder hand on those three words is a warning written by a man who is now dead — and the thing that emptied the chair did not move away, it moved in.
Close the ledger — you have seen enough to know this is bigger than Gin, and a man can refuse a map.
Some doors stay shut because the room behind them is the whole house, and a witness who has found the registry has found the thing that turns a brother's disappearance into the load-bearing problem of a city. But to close it now is to keep choosing Gin over the thirteen the open chair is pulling at — and the page does not stop being true because you stopped reading it.
How a chair leans, written out in a dead man's careful blue ink

The bookseller did not only list the thirteen; he explained them, in a long marginal note that runs sideways up the edge of the page in writing that gets smaller and more frightened the further it climbs, and you turn the ledger ninety degrees and read the only honest account of the wall you have ever been given. A held domain, he writes, is a chair that leans into the room — the holder takes the pressure of their discipline onto a single living soul and faces it inward, toward the city, so that Despair stays a private weather in one person instead of a public flood in a ward; the cost is borne, but it is borne by someone, and the wall holds because every one of its thirteen seams has a person pressed against it from the inside. That is the arrangement. That is the whole secret. The city stands because thirteen people, most of whom you have walked past, are at this moment holding a door shut with their entire lives.

A vacant domain, he writes — and here the hand begins to shake, you can see it, the blue ink catching and pooling — is a chair that leans the other way. With no holder to face the pressure inward, the office does not close; it cannot close, the registry has no mechanism to retire a seat, the thirteen are a number the wall requires and not a committee you can shrink. So the pressure of the thirteenth discipline, with no living soul to bear it, leans outward instead, against the seam nearest the empty chair, which is the seam over the district where the chair was last filled — and the district where XIII was last filled, the bookseller writes in letters now barely a millimetre tall, is Kita-ku, and the year it emptied is the year the seam there first scarred, and the scar has been a vacant office's full weight pressing on a single ward for fifteen years, and that, he writes, is why the dead come so close there on Obon, and that is why a healer who wished to thin the wall would not need to break it. He would only need to help.

You sit with that in the lamplight until the shape of it stops being information and becomes the floor of your stomach. The Healer is not thinning Kita's wall against the grain of the world. It is thinning it along the grain — finishing a tear the empty chair has spent fifteen years starting — and every favour, every closed haunting, every grief gently put to rest, has not been the Healer fighting the registry but the registry's own vacant thirteenth seat doing exactly what an empty office does, with a patient demon standing beside it lending a shoulder. You understand now why no grade in the city can point to a crime. The crime was committed in 2011, by whatever emptied the chair, and everything since has only been the wall finishing the fall it was pushed into before you were grown — your brother's hands the latest, gentlest, most loving thing leaning on a door that has wanted to open since the year the lights went out.

The choice it leaves you
Find out what emptied XIII in 2011 — the first crime is the only one that explains the rest.
Every leaning seam in the city runs back to one empty chair and one year, and a witness who wants the cause and not just the symptom has to ask the question the careful hand was most afraid to write. But the thing that emptied a domain is the thing that has been waiting fifteen years for the chair to fall the rest of the way, and to go looking for it is to introduce yourself to it.
Accept the arithmetic — a vacant chair cannot be fought, only filled, and start asking who could fill it.
If the wall thins because the office stands open, then the wall is mended when the office stands filled, and that is a solution the bookseller's note keeps circling and never names. But the only candidate the far shore has spent a month keying to that exact chair is the man holding your own two suppressed registers — you — and to accept the arithmetic is to become the answer the Healer set the whole engine up to reach.
Reject the frame entirely — refuse to believe the only choices are an open wall or a brother in a chair.
Every trap is a frame, and the strongest thing a witness owns is the refusal to choose between the two doors a clever thing has left him; somewhere off the page there is a third. But refusing the frame is not the same as finding the third door, and the wall thins on a schedule that does not pause for your defiance — Obon is already counting, and a man can be right about the trap and still be inside it.
Chapter Eighteen, continued — The Twelve Who Hold written

You go to look at the living seats — not all of them, you can't, but enough to feel what a held chair costs the person in it, and to find the one that is failing.

Despair VI, on a Tuesday, in a man who looks like nothing at all

You start with the sixth name because the bookseller starred it, twice, in the climbing margin — Watanabe Jun, Despair VI, weakening — and because a weakening seat is the one place the abstract registry becomes a person you can ruin or save by knocking on the wrong door. He is not what you brace for. You have been carrying the word holder like a weight and Watanabe Jun is a slight man in a cardigan two sizes too big, shelving returns at a municipal library in Itabashi, fifty if he is a day, with the particular gentleness of a person who has decided not to take up much room in the world; and you stand in the periodicals with a borrowed newspaper and watch him for an hour and the only strange thing about him is that the light near him is wrong, dimmed by a half-step the way a room dims when a cloud you cannot see passes a window you cannot find, and that no one — not the student asleep over his books, not the woman returning her romances — ever quite meets his eyes.

You understand, watching him reshelve a novel with both hands like it is heavier than it is, that you are looking at the cost written plain. A held domain is not a power he wields; it is a weather he stands in so that no one else has to, and Despair is the one he drew, fifteen or twenty or forty years ago, by whatever lottery or inheritance or terrible volunteering the registry runs on — and it has thinned him the way water thins a stone, not dramatically, just continuously, until the man is mostly the shape of the thing he carries with a little person left over to do the carrying. The bookseller's word was weakening, and now you can see it: there is a tremor in how he holds the books, a translucence at the edges of him in the bad library light, the look of a seam being pressed from the wrong side by an empty chair two wards over, leaning. He is not failing because he is old. He is failing because XIII is pulling, and his is the office nearest the wound, and the vacant chair has been borrowing his strength to lean with for fifteen years.

He catches you watching, finally, the way the haunted always eventually catch you watching, and instead of the fear you expect he gives you a small tired nod — recognition, not of your face but of your kind, the kind that can see the dimmed light — and he comes over and says, very quietly, so the sleeping student will not wake, that he has been wondering when one of you would come, and that he would like, if you have a moment and are who he thinks you are, to know whether it is true what he has started to feel on the worst nights: that there is a thirteenth weight now leaning on his sixth, and that something kind and patient over in Kita has begun, very gently, to help it lean. You did not come to tell a dying man the name of the thing killing him. But he is holding Despair for the whole north of the city, and he has asked you a true question, and the cardigan is two sizes too big because there is less of him inside it every week.

The choice it leaves you
Tell him the truth — the vacant chair, the Healer, the schedule; he has earned the name of his weather.
A man bearing Despair for a city has the right to know why it is winning, and the truth might let him brace in a way that buys the ward time. But the discipline he holds is despair itself, and to hand a weakening holder the knowledge that his failure is engineered, scheduled, and nearly finished is to pour the exact weather he carries directly into the place it does the most harm.
Tell him only what helps — that he is not imagining it, that he is not alone, and that you are working on it.
Comfort is not always a lie, and a holder who knows a witness is moving may stand a week longer than one who thinks himself abandoned; the kindest true thing is sometimes the smallest one. But a man this practised at being lied to gently will hear the shape of what you are leaving out, and a holder of Despair who catches you sparing him has just been handed proof that the truth was worth sparing him from.
Ask him to hold on — name the date, Obon's last night, and ask him for the one thing he has least of: time.
If the wall finishes thinning on a known night, then every night he holds before it is a night the whole north stays a city, and he is the only person alive who can buy you that particular currency. But you would be asking a failing man to spend the last of himself on your schedule, to hold a door past the point where holding it is what finally empties him — and he will say yes, because that is what the chair has made him for.
What the held seats have in common, read across a city in one long night
