To a Kuroda, even one whose gift never opened cleanly, a wrong shadow is not a turn of phrase; it is a second presence... a man possessed, or a man walked beside by something that is not a man, and he has known the difference since before he could read. He looks at the canvases now as though they have just confessed something to him. “That is why they keep coming out wrong,” he says, almost to himself. “I have been painting something that was already standing there.” He lays the brush across the jar. “Tell me what you need. I was with you the moment you said his name.”

The choice it leaves you
Let him in. Tell him everything.

There are two ways a Kuroda can put you across the city before dawn, and they are not the same danger wearing different coats. The first is the family: true teleportation, Sei’s reach, the oldest house in Tokyo... and a grievance older than this morning that the Kuroda will make the two of you pay, in some coin or other, for the crossing. The second is Itsuki’s own gift, the one that never opened clean, so that he reaches through paintings instead. A forgery of the real thing. It takes a little more of him each time, and it has never once set him down exactly where he aimed.

He turns the dead brush over and over while he lays it out for you, plainly, the way a man lays down a hand he already knows is going to cost him. “The family is safer for me and worse for you. They help in the end, expensively, and they want to know everything, and a Kuroda knowing your business is a debt with no number on it. The painting is the other way round. Worse for me. Faster for you. It hurts going through, and it lands where it wants, not where I point.” He will not make the choice for you; he only sets the brush across the jar and lets the studio hold whatever passes between old friends for courage, and waits.

The choice it leaves you
Go to the family. Ask Kuroda Sei.
Safer, slower; the Kuroda never forget what you owe them.
Let Itsuki try the painting.
Faster, it costs him, and it does not land where he aims.
The Kuroda compound, Nezu

Nezu, where the city keeps its oldest money and its oldest silences. The Kuroda compound does not announce itself; the most private house in Tokyo has never had any need to. Old wood gone black with the years. A wall that has outlived three governments. And over all of it a stillness that is not peace but attention... the held quiet of a place that has been listening for a very long time and has not yet decided what it thinks of you.

A cousin meets you at the gate, and she is not Itsuki’s ally. She wears his cheekbones and none of his softness, and she is the real thing... a teleportist who can read, in the disturbed air behind you, the exact shape of how you came. “You brought an outsider,” she says to him. To you she says nothing at all. Her hand never leaves the doorframe, where a sigil is cut into the wood just at the edge of what your eye will hold: a ward keyed to her alone. A door she could shut on you in a way that has nothing to do with the door.

The choice it leaves you
Ask, plainly, for the family’s help.

The family will not be hurried and cannot be charmed. What stands between you and Sei is older than your morning: the blocked gift, a debt, a name Itsuki is not supposed to use as leverage. But there is a worse thing in the room, and you feel the cousin decide whether to say it. She says it. “A wrong shadow carrying weight. You are describing a seated one.” She means the Thirteen, and she does not explain what she means, and the drop in her voice tells you the Kuroda have skin in that game older than the CVD, older than the wards, older than anything in this city that still agrees to be named.

Itsuki goes pale. “If you make me spend the name to get Sei’s reach,” he says, low, “I do not get it back, and the family will own that I spent it, and on what.” The cousin watches you both, waiting to see how much you will cost him.

The choice it leaves you
Spend it. Make the family help, whatever Itsuki owes after.
Find another way in. Don’t bleed him for it.
Kuroda Sei, and the family’s anchor-stone

Kuroda Sei receives you the way deep water receives a stone. Singular among them for control, younger than you expected, stiller than anyone alive, and he has already decided three things you will not be told. On the low table sits the family’s anchor: a teleport-stone the colour of a bruise, the artifact the Kuroda reach through, old enough that the lacquer has gone to glass.

The cousin makes her move while he weighs you. A Tower rival’s gambit: she sets her own working against the room, a binding meant to make you not worth the family’s risk, and the air goes thick and wrong and for a moment you cannot say your own brother’s name. Sei stops it with a glance, not kindly, the way one stops a child. “A wrong shadow at Ōji,” he says into the silence he made. “The night a Directorate officer stopped answering.” Not a question. The case has caught on him, on the family, for reasons that are theirs. “I will reach once. Toward the man, not the brother. Hold the stone, and do not be stupid in the half-second you are nowhere.”

The choice it leaves you
Take the stone. Take Sei’s reach.

The world folds and does not hurt, which is its own kind of wrong, and for half a heartbeat you are nowhere, a place where here and there are the same word. Then a damp underpass assembles around you, the river that is not quite a river close by, and the place still remembers the man.

What settles in you as the fold releases is the family’s kind of knowledge, which is cosmological and has no pity in it. The thing the man carried is a seated one, top-grade, of the DUTY register, and the Towers have spent generations afraid of exactly this: a seat of the Thirteen on the move, looking. Sei’s voice reaches you across the fold, flat. “We do not go near a seated one. Find your brother, intern. But do not bring it to our door.”

The choice it leaves you
Read the rest the family left.

There is something else held here, in the way the whole house flinched at a name it did not speak... a fear older than the debt they just named. To see it you would have to read the compound the way Sei read you, past your own careful disguise, the thing you have never done in front of anyone.

The choice it leaves you
Read what the Kuroda are truly afraid of.
Don’t. You have what you came for.

You surface owing the oldest house in the city a debt with no number on it, and Itsuki owing it more, and the half-degree the world now sits off true from standing where no one should stand. You have the family’s truth: the man carries a seat of the Thirteen, DUTY, and the Towers will not follow it, and that fear is older than anything the CVD would admit to.

[ End of Chapter One, the Tower way. You hold the cosmological edge: a seated demon of DUTY on the move, and a lineage that has feared this longer than the wards have existed. What it is to the man, and to your brother, is the half you did not buy. Chapter Two is being written. ]

Itsuki cuts his palm for ink and opens the canvas

Back in the studio, Itsuki turns the worst canvas to face the room, the Ōji overpass at dusk, the one he could never finish, and you finally see the shape he has been almost-painting and scraping out for weeks: a figure on the bridge, its shadow thrown forward, into the light it should have been falling away from.

“I can make it a door,” he says. “I can’t make it a safe one, and I’ve never carried someone.” He cuts his palm, not for drama, for ink, and presses the blood-and-linseed flat to the wet paint, and the studio thins around you like breath on glass, and the smell of oil becomes the smell of the river, and you go through.

The choice it leaves you
Go through with him.

You come out wrong. Not the overpass. The painting took you where the painting wanted, the way Itsuki swore it might, to the thin place at the edge of the thing it had been trying to show him, and you stand in a low warded hall that smells of incense and river-water, faint shapes massed in the rafters, and you understand a second too late that you have arrived inside the reach of something that gathers.

Itsuki is bleeding from the nose and will not say how much that crossing took. “A congregation,” he breathes, naming it the way his family taught him to name dangers. “A cult-kept place. Rei, the painting brought us to the grief, because the grief is where your man crossed.” The Far-Shore Circle. Higan-kai. The place the taken are recruited, not seized. Drawn down.

The choice it leaves you
Hold still and read the hall.
A working medium mid-rite, a person ridden rising

A working medium of the Circle is mid-rite at the far end, and she has felt you arrive the way a spider feels a thread, and she does not stop her working; she turns it. A woman near her, a grieving recruit already half given over, a person ridden, rises and comes for you wearing borrowed sorrow like a coat, and the medium’s voice follows it, patient. “You came to the right place. Everyone here came to put a grief down. You could put yours down too.”

You have salt in one pocket and the cold key your brother dropped in the other, and Itsuki at your back with a gift already spent. The ridden woman is the one in danger, not the enemy; the enemy is the rite. You will have to break it without breaking her, with what you brought.

The choice it leaves you
Ground on Gin’s key and free her; don’t fight the body.
Grab Itsuki and run for the tear before it closes.

It comes loose all at once, the borrowed grief, and the woman folds down around her own again, which is hers, and survivable, which is more than the rite offered her. The medium does not chase; she watches you leave with the patience of one who knows the grieving always come back. In the space the rite leaves, you get what the place was holding.

The man with the wrong shadow came here, to the grief, the same as the woman, and he was not seized. He walked in carrying a sorrow the Circle could not feed on, because it was already feeding something else, a passenger of the DUTY register, and he walked out north, toward water, unafraid, and the Circle is afraid of him the way small predators fear a large one passing through.

The choice it leaves you
Take it and get out.

You get out the hard way, through a tear that is closing, and it takes the last of what Itsuki had. On the other side, in his studio, he sits down on the floor and does not get up for a while, and when he tries to tell you it is fine, you see what the crossing cost: the painted overpass on the easel is blank now, scraped clean by nothing you did, and when Itsuki reaches for it, he cannot make the colour come.

“It’ll come back,” he says, not certain, the first uncertain thing you have ever heard him say. You did not pay the price for this road. He did, and he would do it again without flinching, and you find you cannot quite look at him.

The choice it leaves you
Sit with him a while, then go.

You come back to the morning owing a debt you cannot repay, because it is not yours; it is Itsuki’s, spent on you, in a currency he may not get to earn again. You have the human-and-occult truth: a cult that recruits the grieving, a man who walked through it carrying DUTY and out the other side unafraid, north, toward water. And a cult that has your face now, and your friend’s.

[ End of Chapter One, the painting’s way. You hold the close, costly edge: the grief the man crossed, the Circle that fears him, the price your oldest friend paid to put you there. What the great houses know of a seated demon stays past a door you chose not to knock on. Chapter Two is being written. ]

Road of the Institution

The Directorate

You give the one name you have on the inside to the CVD, and let the Directorate make you theirs in the same motion.

CVD HQ, Marunouchi... two ways to find anything out

The Civil Veil Directorate keeps its real work behind a tower in Marunouchi that looks like every other glass tower on the block, which is the point. Okada is waiting in the lobby, because you do not get past the lobby. Terse, buttoned, a woman who keeps the files honest and resents being made to stand in a public space to do it. “You came in person,” she says, not pleased. “I told you to call the number.”

You tell her anyway. The key. The cold. The shadow that fell ahead of the man it belonged to. Something moves behind her eyes and is filed away before you can read it. Then she says: “There are two ways a person finds anything out in this building, and they do not lead to the same place. Choose carefully. You only get to be new here once.”

The choice it leaves you
Ask how it works.

She lays it out the way she lays out a file, flat and complete. “One. You report him, on the record, and the Directorate opens a door for you and a file on you in the same motion. You get the system. The system gets you, and does not give you back. What is in the official record is yours; what is sealed in it is not; and a great deal is sealed.”

“Two. I look for you. Off the books, off my own login, on my own neck. No file with your name on it. But you get what I can carry out in my head and my mouth, the human shape of it, the part no form holds, and you do not get the record, because the record is the thing I would be risking everything to even glance at.” She waits. “They are not the same truth, and you do not get both. Not as a stranger. So. Which half of your brother do you want?”

The choice it leaves you
The record. Report him officially.
The institution’s truth. You become theirs to get it.
The human truth. Let Okada look off the books.
What no file holds. She risks herself; the record stays dark to you.

You sign. A form for being someone’s brother, a longer one for what you saw, a longer one still for consenting to be assessed as next of kin. They take your name, your level as they read it (a D, a nobody, which is what you have always let them think), your address, and enter all of it into Component One, the Catalogue, where it will sit and be readable for the rest of your life. The door opens and closes behind you in the same motion. You are theirs now, a little. That was the price on the form.

The choice it leaves you
Let them assess you.

The assessor is pleasant the way a scale is pleasant. A few questions. A card with a symbol you are told not to think about, then asked what you thought about. The standard battery for a low Sensitive. Near the end she pauses, the smallest pause, makes a note you are not shown, and says “Unremarkable,” which is the word you wanted. The pause is the word you did not.

You could let it pass, the way you have let everything pass your whole careful life. Or you could do the thing you never do in front of them: read her back, the way she is reading you, and see what the pause was hiding.

The choice it leaves you
Read the assessor as she reads you.
Stay unremarkable. Let it pass.

With your name in the system, Okada lays the file in front of you, and you see how much of it is the shape of what is missing. You take the pieces it will give. A time: the overpass, logged at 1:14 in the morning, the exact minute a thread on your phone says Gin stopped answering. A note in the dry CVD hand: SECOND-PRESENCE INDICATORS, SUBJECT UNCOOPERATIVE. And a clearance stamp on the seal, an office, not a name, but an office high enough that Okada goes very still when she sees whose it is.

“That is the record’s edge for me,” she says. “Past it, grey, sealed, stamped by someone who could end me for showing you even this.” There is, she does not quite say, a floor below this one, where the things behind the seal are kept.

The choice it leaves you
Push. Ask to see the floor below.
The asset shelf. It costs, and it is on no tour.
Take the pieces you have and go.
The floor the lobby never mentions

Okada should not, and does, walk you past a door the lobby never mentions, down to where the Directorate keeps what it takes. Rows of containment, each tagged with a designation number, a faint shape in every one. Souls kept as inventory. No one here calls it that. One of them was logged the same night your man crossed, and it presses to the ward-glass when it feels you notice it, and it holds the one piece the file did not: where he went.

The choice it leaves you
Reach past the glass and take the piece it holds.
Don’t. You have enough to carry out.

You walk out of Marunouchi with the institution’s shape of your brother in your hands and a weight in your chest that is not the cold. A time. A tell. The stamp of an office high enough to frighten the one honest person inside. You know the Directorate knows what took the man on the bridge, and has decided, from a height, that you are not to. You do not know what your brother became. That is the half you traded away, and the system you traded it for has your name now, and your level, and your address.

You hold the record's edge now, and it is colder than you expected. A sealed case. A minute logged at 1:14. A clearance high enough to make the one honest person in that tower go quiet. The Directorate knows what took the man on the bridge; it has decided, from a height you will never be shown, that you are not to. What the seal hides, and where your brother went, lie past a door you are now standing inside of, and watched within. To learn the human shape of it, the why, you would have to ask the people who loved him, off any record at all. That is a different morning's work, and it is coming.

Two coffees, two streets over, off any record

She does not take you inside. She takes you to a vending-machine corner two streets over, buys two coffees, drinks neither. “Off the books means off the books,” she says. “If this comes back, it comes back on me, not the file, because there will be no file. What I give you, I give with my own neck, and only because your brother was the kind of man you do this for.”

That is the most she has ever said about him, and she says the rest looking at the coffee. “The last weeks, he was wrong. Not sick. Wrong, the way a room is wrong after something has stood in it. And he was protecting something. Carefully. Quietly. I think,” she says, and stops, and you understand she has decided to be honest, “I think he was protecting you.”

The choice it leaves you
Ask what he was protecting you from.

She does not know from what. That part is sealed even from her. But she knows where: in the last weeks, Gin kept going north, to the water, to a stretch of riverbank he had no case in and no reason for, again and again, at hours when the cameras are out. “He never logged it,” she says. “A man does not log the place he goes to do the thing he is hiding. I only know because reading the gaps in what people log is my whole job, and his gaps all pointed the same way.”

North. The water. The same direction the man on the bridge was walking. You feel the two facts touch in your chest, and you do not yet let yourself say what shape they make together.

The choice it leaves you
Follow where he kept going.
A stretch of riverbank with no reason in it

You go where his gaps pointed, to a stretch of riverbank with no reason in it, and the reason is waiting. Not the man. Something smaller, left behind, a residue caught at a doorway the way grief catches: a witness, not the Directorate’s kind, the kind the water keeps. It was here for the thing Gin came to do, over and over, and it remembers him, and it remembers the last night he came, not alone.

The choice it leaves you
Reach for what it remembers of him.
You aren’t ready for the last night. Step back.

You walk back up from the water with the human shape of your brother in your hands and no proof of any of it. You know he changed. You know he was protecting you, from a thing he would not name. You know he came here, north, to the water, to do something in secret, and that on the last night he did not come alone. You understand him, which is the half you chose. You do not have the record, the seal, the office, the proof, the name of whoever in that glass tower decided he was a thing to be hidden rather than a man to be found.

You hold the human edge of it now, and no proof of any of it. A brother who changed. A brother who was protecting you from a thing he would not name, who went north to the water in secret, and did not come back from the last night alone. What the Directorate logged and sealed stays dark to you; to hold that, you would have had to become theirs, and you did not. You chose to understand him instead. Tonight, understanding will have to be enough to walk on.

Road of the Unregistered

The Grid

You open Gin's book to the names he wrote down small, in the back, in his own hand — the people the official records cannot see.

Sanya... the door to the Grid

Minami-Senju, the end of a train line most maps treat as the edge of the world. Tanizaki’s door is a back room behind a back room, in Sanya, where the city keeps the people it would rather forget. No sign. A kettle. He does not look up. He pours tea, pours a second cup and slides it across without being asked, and you understand there is a ritual here you are already failing.

“Gin’s brother,” he says, not a question. “The intern. He talked about you for years. Never once brought you here.” He listens to the key, the cold, the shadow that fell ahead of the man, the way deep water listens. “I do not exist on paper. Neither does the help you want. So before I spend any of it, understand how we work, and what it costs.”

The choice it leaves you
Ask the Grid for help.

The Grid is the opposite of the tower. No files, no catalogue, no protection, no standing you have not earned, no one who must help you because a form says so. The unregistered survive by being unfindable and by owing one another carefully. A favor here is a real thing, with weight, and never free.

“Two ways,” Tanizaki says. “You spend something your brother left you. He had credit with me, a name, and you are his blood; that opens a door, once. Or you earn your own, by doing the Grid a turn before it does you one. They are different doors, and they open on different rooms. Gin would tell you to earn it. Gin is not here.”

The choice it leaves you
Spend Gin’s credit. Use his name.
Opens the inner door. The Grid never forgets a debt.
Earn your own. Do the Grid a turn first.
Action, and a chance to lose, and standing that is truly yours.
The Gathering, and the Switch at the door

You say your brother’s name like a key and it turns. Tanizaki takes you to the Gathering, which is less a place than an agreement about one; the Switch moves it, scrubs it from the kind of attention that finds things, and you arrive without quite knowing how, into a warded room above a coin laundry, ward-marks on every wall drawn by hands that meant them.

You are there a minute before it goes wrong. The Switch, a wire-thin woman who keeps the Grid invisible, comes off the wall white-faced. “Sweep,” she says, one word, and the room moves like a kicked nest. A Directorate sweep, inbound, because the man’s passage has left the whole ward thin and ringing and the tower is hunting the disturbance and does not care whose door it opens to find it. The unregistered are about to be very findable.

The choice it leaves you
Help. Don’t be the reason they get caught.
Grab Tanizaki. Get your answer before this scatters.

You make your choice in the half-minute the city gives you, and the Gathering comes apart around it the way the Grid has survived a hundred sweeps: not in panic, in practice, every person a door to somewhere else, the room emptying into the laundry steam and the back alleys and the cracks the tower’s map does not have.

Tanizaki holds you back a beat in the emptying room. “You want what the man left in the ward,” he says. “We keep someone who can read that. Come. Quietly. And whatever she shows you, you did not see it here, because after tonight there is no here.”

The choice it leaves you
Go to the one who reads the dead.

She is old in a way that has nothing to do with years, a Grid sensitive who reads the residue the dead leave the way other people read a room, and she has been refusing to sleep for a week. “Your man,” she says, before you ask. “He has been crossing the ward going north for a month, and he changed on the way. I have watched it happen in the marks he leaves.”

She tells you the part the Grid has been too frightened to log: the Far-Shore Circle, the Higan-kai, the cult that feeds on grief, has been gathering hard along his path, and the man passed straight through their reach and they did not take him, because he was already taken, by something the Circle is afraid of. “Whatever rides him,” she says, “the predators get out of its way.”

The choice it leaves you
Ask her to read the weight of it.
A residue read by a charm of knotted cord and iron

She works with a charm of knotted cord and old iron, the Grid’s kind of instrument, nothing the tower would recognise, and she puts your hand on it and makes you feel what she feels, because she says a thing this size should not be carried by one person’s word alone.

It is a weight. North-pointing, toward water, and heavier than anything that should fit in a man, and when she reaches for the name of it the charm goes cold and dead in your joined hands and she lets go fast. A single word surfaces anyway, the way the worst words do. DUTY. “A seat,” she whispers. “He is carrying a seat of the Thirteen. God help him, and you.”

The choice it leaves you
Ask the thing the Grid does not say aloud.

There is a thing under all of it, the thing the unregistered whisper about and will not finish whispering, and you can reach for it or leave it. To hear it whole you would have to read her the way she read the ward, past your own careful disguise, the thing you never do.

The choice it leaves you
Read what the Grid is most afraid of.
Don’t. You have enough to carry north.

You come up out of Sanya with the unregistered scattered behind you and a debt to a people who do not forget, and the Grid’s kind of truth in your hands: a sensitive’s certainty, a charm gone dead, a seat of the Thirteen riding a man going north, and a cult that fears him. No file holds any of it. No one but the Grid will ever confirm you were told.

[ End of Chapter One, the Grid’s way. You hold the unregistered edge: a seat of the Thirteen on the move, a grief-cult that will not touch him, and a debt owed to the people who survive by being unseen. What the tower logged and sealed stays the tower’s. Chapter Two is being written. ]

You tell him you will earn it, and something in him approves and does not show it. “A thing needs carrying tonight,” he says. “To someone who cannot be seen collecting it.” He gives you a word and a place and not what the thing is, because asking is its own failure down here.

The thing is a person. You understand it on the stairwell where the handoff should happen and a young Grid runner is instead on his knees, half given over, eyes gone to someone else’s grief, and the word you were given is the only thing keeping the two people minding him from putting him down for good. The Far-Shore Circle is in him... you can read the vacancy behind his eyes, grief worn like a coat that fits too well. The two kneeling beside him are Grid, not Circle, and they are losing him in slow inches to something patient and hungry. You came to carry something. What you found is someone already being carried the other way.

The choice it leaves you
Help them get him out.
A working medium comes to reclaim the half-taken runner

She comes for him the way an owner comes for property, a working medium of the Circle, calm in the stairwell light, a cord of attention running from her hand to the place in the boy where his grief used to be. She does not hurry. “He came to us,” she says. “They all come to us. You can carry him out, but he will walk back in by morning, because grief is a door that only opens one way.”

You have the word, and salt, and Gin’s cold key in your pocket, and a hard thing in front of you: the boy can be freed, but only by reaching past his grief and cutting the cord while the medium is still pulling, which is a fight you take in his place, not against his body. Or you can take what the runner already saw and get out, and leave the cord where it is.

The choice it leaves you
Cut the cord. Take the fight in his place.
Take what he saw and run. Leave the cord.

You cut it the way you cut anything you are not strong enough to cut, by refusing to let go first, and the cord parts with a sound you feel in your teeth, and the medium loses her calm for exactly one second before she puts it back on like a coat. She does not chase. The grieving always come back; she can afford to wait. The boy comes up out of it gasping, himself, wrecked and alive.

What he saw, he gives you while he is still shaking. The man with the wrong shadow walked through the Circle a fortnight back, and the medium who is standing right there went to her knees for him, because the thing he carried made her own master look small. He went north. Toward water. Unafraid. “The big quiet one,” the boy says. “Even the cult’s scared of him.”

The choice it leaves you
Get him to safety, then go.

You take what the runner already babbled and you go, and the cord stays in him, and you tell yourself the intel matters more than one half-taken boy you do not know, and you almost believe it. Behind you the medium does not even look up. She got what she came for. So, in a thinner way, did you.

What the boy saw, before the grief closed back over him: the man with the wrong shadow walked through the Circle and the cult went out of his way, and he went north, toward water, carrying something that made the medium kneel. It is enough to point you. It is not enough to feel clean about.

The choice it leaves you
Carry it north. Don’t look back.

You come back to Sanya however you came back, clean or not, and Tanizaki reads which on your face before you sit. The second cup is poured. “You will do,” is all he says, and from him it is a great deal. The Grid will move the Gathering again because of tonight, because of you, and that is the shape of what you are owed and what you owe.

[ End of Chapter One, the earned way. You hold the close, hard edge: a cult that fears the man, a seat of the Thirteen that makes mediums kneel, a runner you saved or did not, and the Grid’s slow trust paid in action. What the great houses know of a seated demon stays past a door you did not knock on. Chapter Two is being written. ]

Road of the Gift

The Forbidden Reach

You are Clairsentient. To read a place is costly: your sight thins the veil, feeds the Madness, and shows the Directorate exactly what you have pretended not to be.

The cold key, and the thing you never let yourself do

You have spent your whole life being a low reading on someone else’s instrument. A D. A Sensitive who finds lost keys and the mood of a room and nothing that matters. You have been so careful for so long that the carefulness has become a kind of person, and you are no longer certain where the person ends and the hiding begins.

Tonight you stop hiding. Alone, in the flat that is too quiet, the cold key in your hand. No Directorate, no Grid, no Itsuki. Just you and the thing you have never let yourself fully do. You close your fingers around the key your brother dropped, and you reach, properly, the way you have not since you were small enough to be frightened of it. It comes. It comes so easily. Too easily, like a held breath let go.

The choice it leaves you
Read it. See what the key saw.

You are at the edge of what you have always allowed yourself, and the edge has two sides. You can read the way a D reads, shallow and hidden, staying the small safe thing you have pretended to be, and take only the fragments that float up. Or you can drop the disguise entirely, reach the way the thing in you actually wants to, take all of it, and find out, at last, what you have been hiding from your whole life.

The key hums against your palm, and the thing inside you stirs the way something stirs when it hears a key. It has been patient. You have always understood that it has been patient; you simply chose not to think about what patience, in that quantity, implies. You can feel exactly how far you could push, and that you can feel it at all is the part you will think about later, if there is a later that recognises you.

The choice it leaves you
Read carefully. Stay inside the disguise.
Fragments, and safety, and the door stays closed... mostly.
Drop the disguise. Reach all the way.
Everything, at a price, and the whole hidden world turns to look.

You keep yourself small, the way you always have, and read only what rises. Gin on the overpass, in the last hour anyone could call ordinary. The man near him, the shadow falling the wrong way, ahead of him, against the light. The cold. A direction, faint: north.

And, faint as the direction, something else: far off, a thread of attention brushes the edge of even your careful reach, the way a fisher feels a touch on a long line. Something out there fishes for the gifted in the small hours, and it felt you, a little, and went still, marking the place you were.

The choice it leaves you
Pull the read tighter. Hold it longer.

You hold the reach carefully, and the key gives a little more. The man went north, toward water, and the weight he carried bent your careful sight the way a heavy thing bends a floor, and you let go before it could cost what it wanted to cost. You stayed a D. You stayed hidden. You got a piece, and kept your disguise, and that is the whole of the bargain.

The far-off attention has not gone. You know its flavour now, grief and patience together, the way the grieving are gathered: a cult-thing, fishing, and it has your scent at the lowest possible volume. The Far-Shore Circle does not forget a sensitive it has felt, even faintly. You will want to remember that.

The choice it leaves you
Read your own reaching, the thing you never do.
Take the fragment and stop.

You turn the sight inward, onto your own reach, which you have never once allowed, and for a heartbeat you see the shape of the wall you have spent your life building, and what it is holding back, and how thin it actually is, and why your brother spent years making sure no one ever measured you. You put it back. Your hands are shaking. You are, technically, still a D, and you understand now that the technicality is the most fragile thing you own.

The choice it leaves you
Carry the fragment, and what you saw in yourself.

You come back to the too-quiet flat with a fragment and your disguise intact and a new, cold knowledge sitting behind your eyes: that you could reach further, any time, that the reaching is a door, and that things on the other side of it have begun, faintly, to wait for you to be tired enough to open it.

[ End of Chapter One, the hidden way. You hold the careful edge: the man went north, toward water, carrying a weight that bends sight, and a grief-cult has felt you at the lowest volume and will not forget. What lies past your own wall, you chose not to look at. Chapter Two is being written. ]

A second set of eyes you closed with your own hand

You let go of the wall. It is not like opening your eyes; it is like finding you have always had a second set, behind the first, closed by your own hand your entire life. The key’s last hour floods you whole: the overpass, the man, the wrong shadow, the smell of a river that is not quite a river, a direction, north, and the specific gravity of the thing the man carried, a weight your new sight insists on a word for against your will.

And the world turns to look. You feel it happen, a sweep of attention from every direction at once: a Directorate sensor somewhere logging a spike it cannot place, a Grid ward tightening three wards over, and under both, slower and vaster, something that is not an instrument at all, a regard with thirteen facets, one of which is awake. You have just made a light in a dark room full of things that hunt by light.

The choice it leaves you
Hold the sight. Don’t look away.
Something arrives in the tear you made

It does not send something small. You reached too well for that. The regard comes itself, or a finger of it does, a patience the size of a throne, and it knows your name the way these things know names, by the shape of the want underneath. It shows you, because it is generous, the vacant place: a seat of the Thirteen gone empty, and the long pull of everything toward it, the Axis, the closeness that is not safety, and it shows you the man walking that pull northward, unafraid, full, and it asks if you would like to stop being so careful and so alone and so small.

Your brother spent his whole life building the distance between you and exactly this offer. You understand, now, why. You have the cold key in your hand, the one thing that is wholly, stubbornly, ordinarily yours.

The choice it leaves you
Refuse it. Close your hand on the key and push it out.
Pull back before it finishes the offer.

You refuse it the way you have refused yourself for years, which is the one thing you are genuinely good at, your fist closed white around a cheap brass key, and you force the tear shut around the regard, and it withdraws with something that is almost amusement and entirely patient. It will be back. They always come back to the ones who can reach.

But you held the sight to the end, and you kept what it showed you, and it is more than anyone else on any other road will have. The man carries a seat of the Thirteen, of the DUTY register, and he is walking the Axis-pull north, toward the empty throne, toward water, unafraid, because for a seated one there is nothing left to fear. And you know how it is done, now, because for one moment the same door stood open in you, and the same offer was made, and you understand that he was not taken. He was reached, at his hardest reach, for someone he loved.

The choice it leaves you
Write it down with shaking hands.

You come back to yourself owing debts you did not have this morning. One to the veil, which has your full scent now, not the careful volume but all of it, and which knows you can reach, and will keep offering. One to the Thirteen, one facet of which has looked at you and found you interesting, which is the worst thing you could possibly be to it. One to Gin, who came no nearer to being found, except that you understand him better than anyone alive, because for one moment you stood exactly where he stood and were offered exactly what he was offered, reaching for someone you love.

[ End of Chapter One, the open-door way. You hold the deepest, most dangerous edge: the Axis, the empty seat, the offer itself, the truth that he was reached and not taken. And the whole hidden world has your face now. Chapter Two is being written. ]

Road of the Long Way

The Long Walk

No name, no power, no institution. Only your eyes and the long way, which sometimes finds what the short ways step over.

Ōji at four in the morning, a held breath

You do not call anyone. You do not open the book, or reach for the gift, or knock on any door. You put on your shoes and go out into the four-in-the-morning city and let it have you, the way Gin used to say the long way finds what the short ways miss. You used to think it was just something he said.

Ōji at this hour is a held breath. Shuttered shops, a vending machine humming its one note, a cat crossing the road like it holds the lease. You walk without choosing a direction and find you have been choosing one all along, the way you always do. The city is not empty. It only looks empty to people who are not paying attention, and you have spent your whole life paying attention and pretending not to.

The choice it leaves you
Keep walking. Let the city decide.

The walk forks the way walks do, not at a sign but at a feeling. One way the streets get warmer, a thread of light and noise from the places that stay open for the people who cannot sleep, the bars and the stands where the city keeps its insomniacs and its secrets. The other way the streets go quiet and cold and downhill, toward the water, toward the riverbank where, your grandmother used to say, the dead come home.

Both are true at this hour. The city holds both. But it is Obon, the week the doors between here and there stand open, and tonight one of those two ways is louder than it has any right to be, and you cannot yet tell which.

The choice it leaves you
Toward the lit places, the people still awake.
The night people see everything and forget on purpose.
Toward the empty places, and the river.
Obon, and the dead coming home, and what disturbed them.
The Suzuran, nine seats and a held match

The Suzuran is nine seats and a bartender and a light the colour of a held match. Kaneda is polishing a glass that is already clean, the way men do when the real work is the listening. He does not know you, sizes you up in the time it takes to set the glass down, slides a small one across. “First one’s on the house. You look like the kind of trouble that pays for itself later.”

You are straight with him, and he pours himself one, which he does not do for liars, and you are three sentences in when it happens. A regular at the end of the bar, an old man who has been crying quietly into the same drink for an hour, stands up wrong. Not drunk. Wrong, the way a room goes wrong. His grief is not his anymore; something is wearing it, and it has been working him slow all week, here, in the warm bright place where the lonely come, and tonight it is ready to take him home.

The choice it leaves you
Help him. Don’t let the bright place be a trap.

Kaneda is already moving, salt from under the bar in his closed fist, no words required between you. He has poured for the night crowd long enough to know what standing water draws, and the warm rooms are not safe from it, and never were. You and Kaneda work the old man back to himself together, salt and a steady voice and the cold key in your pocket pressed once, briefly, against his hand, and he sits down hard and weeps the way a drowning man breathes when the surface finds him, which is more than the thing beneath had offered him.

Kaneda pours you a real one, after. “That,” he says, “is the third this month. Somebody is hungry in this ward.” And then, low, the thing the city has been not-saying: “There’s a man been walking nights. Cameras out. Shadow gets there before he does. People who see him won’t say what was wrong, only that something was. If I were you, I’d walk north. Toward the water. That’s the direction the not-saying points.”

The choice it leaves you
Read the ward through Kaneda, the way you never do.
Take the rumor and go.

You let yourself read him, lightly, the bartender and the bar and the ward sitting behind it, the way you never do, because the living did not consent to be read. What you find under Kaneda’s plain steadiness is a district held between tides: the whole ward has been thinning for weeks, a slow water going out, and the cult is feeding harder because the feeding has grown easier, because something far larger than the Far-Shore Circle is drawing the world toward an empty place, and the small predators are only following the current down.

The choice it leaves you
Carry the rumor, and what you felt under it.

You walk back out into the small hours owing the lightest of debts, a drink and a shared bad minute with a man who will forget you on purpose. You have the warm-rooms truth: a cult feeding harder than it should, a ward going quietly thin, an old man saved, and a direction the not-saying points. North. The water.

[ End of Chapter One, the lit way. You hold the human edge: the Far-Shore feeding in the warm places, the ward thinning toward something, the man who walks nights going north. What the river holds, and what the dead saw of him, you walked away from. Chapter Two is being written. ]

The riverbank at Obon, the dead come home wrong

You go down toward the water, where the city runs out of reasons to be loud. The riverbank at four in the morning is the oldest thing in Tokyo, older than the towers, older than the Directorate. Your grandmother brought you here once, at Obon, to set a paper lantern on the current for people you were too young to have lost. The dead come home along the water, she said, and you never quite stopped half-believing it.

Tonight you do not have to half-believe it. The dead are home, and they are not at peace. The whole reach of the river is wrong, the membrane worn to almost nothing, lanterns from a hundred grieving families guttering in a wind that is not there, and the returning dead are agitated, crowding the waterline, all of them turned the same way, north, the way animals turn from a fire. Something crossed here, going north, and it frightened the dead, and the dead do not frighten easily.

The choice it leaves you
Walk the waterline. See what crossed.
A medium working the mourners at the waterline

The crossing left a witness, the kind the water keeps. You sense it at the margin, a residue caught at the edge of returning, patient and still... and then you cannot reach it. A working medium of the Far-Shore Circle stands between you and the water, mid-rite, harvesting the Obon grief the way you harvest a field that has done your work for you. A mourner kneels in the lantern-light with someone else’s sorrow climbing slowly into her, and the medium does not look up. “Everyone comes to the water to put a grief down,” she says. “She came to put hers down. So did you.”

You have salt, and Gin’s cold key, and the dead at your back all turned north. The mourner is the one in danger; the medium is the rite. You can break it and free her, or step back and let the dead witness tell you what it saw, and not everyone gets to do both before the lanterns go out.

The choice it leaves you
Free the mourner. Break the rite with what is yours.
Step back and let the dead show you what they saw.

You break it the way you break anything you are not strong enough to break, by refusing to let go first, salt and a steady hand and a cheap brass key, and the borrowed sorrow comes off the mourner all at once and she folds down around her own, which is hers, survivable. The medium loses her patience for exactly one second, then puts it back on like a coat, and withdraws into the lantern-dark. The grieving always come back. She can wait.

And now the dead will let you near, because you did a kind thing at their water. The witness gives up what it saw the way the dead give anything, gently. The man with the wrong shadow crossed here going north, and the dead all turned from him, because what he carried was not a passenger but a throne, a seat of the Thirteen, of the DUTY register, and it was walking the long pull north toward an empty place, and he was not afraid, and the dead know there is only one kind of thing that walks among them unafraid.

The choice it leaves you
Set a lantern for him. Then go.

You set a paper lantern on the current the way your grandmother taught you, for a man who is not dead and may be worse, and you watch it join the others going wrong in the windless dark, and you walk back up from the water carrying the heaviest truth of all the truths you could have carried this morning, and the loneliest.

[ End of Chapter One, the water’s way. You hold the oldest edge: a seat of the Thirteen on the move, the dead themselves turning from it, a cult gorging in the shallows of a world being pulled toward an empty place, and your brother walking north into all of it, unafraid. The city handed it to you because you walked slow enough, and at the water, to be given it. Chapter Two is being written. ]

Smaller stories with their own weather. Each is multi-scene; longer versions are being authored.

Yua's Debt written
Given by Yua

A street kid who watches out for Rei owes the wrong people; the favour she calls in is smaller and worse than money.

Yanaka, the third night of Obon, where the lantern-sellers thin out

Yua finds you the way she always has — not by walking up but by already being there, a smudge of a girl in a man's outgrown jacket leaning on the cold side of a vending machine where the festival light doesn't reach. She is fourteen, or says she is, and she has watched the back of your neck on more platforms than you have ever thanked her for. Tonight the Obon lanterns are going down the Yanaka slope toward the river, paper boats for the dead finding their way home, and she will not look at them. I'm not asking for money, she says before you can offer it, fast, like a thing rehearsed against the chance she'd lose her nerve. Money I could've got. This is the other kind.

You have known for a year that Yua owes someone on the Grid, the way you know a tooth is going bad — by the way she favours one side of every conversation, by the appointments she keeps that she will not name. Now she pulls her sleeve up past the wrist and shows you the underside of her forearm, where there is no mark at all, only skin, and that is the wrong thing, that is the frightening thing: there should be a memory there and there is a smooth absence instead, the place where one has already been lifted clean. I borrowed against remembering, she says. I didn't have anything else worth holding. And the last payment comes due tonight, while the gate's open, because that's when it's cheapest for them and dearest for me.

She tells you the rest in the flat voice children use for the worst things, because feeling it would cost more than she has. The collateral on the final note is the only clear memory she has left of her brother Haru — his actual face, not the photograph she has worn soft, the real grain of his voice telling her something on a train. When the Tallyman collects tonight it comes out of her like a tooth and she will keep the shape of the loss without the thing that was lost. I can't stop it, she says. But you've got two registers you keep stuffed down where they don't show. So I'm asking you to stand there when they take it, and let me tell it to you first — so it's somewhere. So Haru's somewhere. That's the whole favour. It's smaller than money. I know it's worse.

The choice it leaves you
"Tell me now, before we go anywhere. I'll hold it."
Taking a memory into a suppressed register isn't free filing — it weights the register, and a register that wakes up on a borrowed grief wakes up loud. You'd be carrying her brother and tipping your own hand at once.
"We're not paying it in memory. Take me to whoever holds the note."
The Grid does not renegotiate for sentiment, and a broker who deals in remembering can read what you'd offer instead off the back of your eyes. You'd be walking in with the one currency you can't afford to be seen carrying.
"How long until they collect? Don't lie to me about the time."
She has been managing you the way she manages everyone who might help — by the slice. Push for the truth and you'll learn the window is shorter than she let on, and that she came to you last, not first.
Below the Sendagi crossing, where the Grid keeps a night-market the registered city can't see

The Tallyman does not have a face so much as the place where keeping accounts has worn one away. It works a stall under the Sendagi tracks that you would walk past a hundred times registered and never once see, and the moment Yua steps under its awning the festival noise overhead goes muffled, as though the whole bright living city has been folded behind glass. There are jars on its shelves, hundreds, and you make yourself not look too long at any one of them because each holds a little weather of light that moves like something remembering being alive. The watcher girl brings a witness, it says, and its attention crosses your skin like a hand checking a coat for the shape of a wallet. Two of them. One folded twice. How thoughtful, to bring me the interest along with the principal.

It lays the note on the counter the way a doctor lays out an instrument, and the terms are written in Yua's own hand from a year ago, a child's careful characters promising what a child shouldn't have to weigh. The memory of Haru's face, in exchange for the rent she could not make the winter she was eleven. It has been good to her, the Tallyman points out, almost gently — it could have called sooner, on a colder night, for less reason. It is calling now, during Obon, because remembering runs thin when the dead are walking and a memory comes loose easier through the gap, and a debt should be collected at the season that serves the creditor. Unless, it says, and lets the word hang, the witness would prefer to be the payer. I am not particular about whose remembering I file. Only that the ledger closes tonight.

You feel both registers turn over at once, the way a dog lifts its head at a sound under the floor — they have caught the smell of the jars, of so much remembering held still, and they want it. That is the trap inside the trap, and Yua does not know to warn you of it: this is exactly the kind of place that could wake the thing you have spent your whole life keeping asleep. The Tallyman watches you the way it watches everything, as an amount. You came in carrying so much, it says. It would be such a small adjustment, to leave a little lighter.

The choice it leaves you
Offer one of your own memories in Haru's place — a thing of yours, freely given.
A clean substitution closes Yua's note and saves Haru's face. But you choose what you offer, and grief makes you reach for the heaviest thing in the room to prove you mean it — and the heaviest thing you own is a morning in a kitchen with Gin, which you cannot afford to lose and may not be able to keep yourself from offering.
Refuse the terms and ask what the note would take instead of memory — a service, a name, a debt deferred.
The Tallyman keeps every kind of ledger. It will name a price that isn't memory — and the alternative currencies it deals in are favours owed into the Grid, the same web Gin disappeared through. You'd be trading Yua's grief for a thread that ties you to the thing you're hunting.
Let the collection happen, and catch the memory into your register as it comes loose — the favour she actually asked for.
This is the smaller, worse thing she wanted: not rescue, just survival of the memory somewhere. It takes Haru out of her anyway. And the register you open to catch him is one you have never once chosen to open on purpose — you don't know what else comes through the door you hold ajar.
The collection — the instrument set, the gate at its widest

Whatever you decided, the moment of collection is the same shape, and it is quiet, and that is the horror of it — you keep waiting for it to be violent and it is only precise. The Tallyman does not reach for Yua so much as ask the memory a question it cannot help answering, and you watch the girl's face go through something no face should have to do in front of a witness: a brief, terrible brightening, as Haru comes all the way up to the surface one last time to be counted, the real him, laughing at something on a train with the specific crooked tooth the photograph never kept. For one second Yua is eleven and not alone. Then the second ends.

If you reached, you feel him cross — a stranger's brother arriving in a room of yourself you keep dark, the weight of a love you did not earn settling into a register that shudders at the unfamiliar grief and threatens, for one long swimming instant, to open the rest of the way and let out whatever else lives behind it. If you paid, you feel the gap where your own given thing used to be, already smoothing over, already becoming a loss without an edge. If you let it go to close the ledger clean, you watch the brightness leave her and you understand that you chose the cheapest door and that she will know, the way the watched always know about the watcher. The Tallyman files the note and the awning's muffling lifts a half-inch, festival noise leaking back in, the living city remembering it is there.

The ledger is closed, it says, and it is already looking past both of you at the next set of footsteps in the dark. But it pauses, because it deals in what people will give and it has taken your measure now and found it useful to know. You hold a great deal you have not counted, it tells you, not unkindly, the way a creditor is kind to a client it intends to see again. When you are ready to know the true sum of yourself, you know which stall keeps the longest ledger in the city. Bring the watcher girl. She remembers where the door is, even when she cannot remember why.

The choice it leaves you
Get Yua out into the lantern-light before you let yourself feel any of it.
She is fourteen and she has just lost the last real piece of someone, in public, in front of you. Putting her first is right — and it means you carry whatever just happened to you, alone, unexamined, into a register you can't be sure is closed again.
Tell the Tallyman you'll be back to learn the true sum — and mean it.
It is the first creature in the city to offer you a straight account of what you actually are. That is a door you have wanted your whole life. It is also a hook, set by a thing that does nothing without a ledger, in the exact season your brother went looking through a gap like this one.
Say nothing. Pick up a lantern from the stack and put it in Yua's hands.
A boat for a brother she can no longer picture. The gesture costs nothing and means everything, which is the only kind of payment left tonight — and it tells her you understood exactly what you witnessed, which is its own weight to have laid on a child.
The river stairs, after — paper boats going down toward a sea none of them will reach

You take her down to the water where the others are launching their lanterns, and Yua kneels at the bottom step with the paper boat you put in her hands, and for a while she cannot light it because she has forgotten who it is for. That is the part the Tallyman charged her for and did not bother to mention: not the memory only but the why of keeping it, so that she holds a grief with the name scraped off, a love with no object, the worst kind of debt because it cannot be discharged by remembering or forgotten by trying. It was somebody, she says, testing the words. It was somebody good. I can feel the shape of good. I just can't get the rest to come. And you, who chose tonight whether to carry her brother or your own kitchen or nothing at all, sit down on the cold stone beside her and do not lie about how that feels.

If you took Haru into your register, you find you can give a little of him back — not the memory, you cannot return what the ledger took, but the fact of him, the train and the tooth and the laugh, told as a witness tells a thing, so that she has a story where she used to have the man. It is not the same and it is not nothing, and she listens to her own brother described by a stranger with a hunger that breaks something in you that you did not know was load-bearing. If you paid instead, you discover the cost only now — reaching for the thing you gave and finding the smooth place where it was, learning by its absence how much you must have loved it. If you let the collection close clean, the silence between you on the steps is honest, at least, and she does not pretend you saved her, and you do not pretend you tried.

When the boat finally goes down — and it does, all of them do, the whole grieving city pushing its dead gently out toward a darkness that is, tonight, briefly thin — Yua watches it until the current takes it round the bend and then she stands, and she is fourteen again, and the watching is already back in her, the appraisal of exits and angles that has kept her alive. You'll go looking for your one too, she says, not asking, because she has watched the back of your neck for a year and she knows the shape of a person hunting a brother through a gap in the world. When you do — don't borrow against the remembering. Whatever they offer. That's the favour back. And she is gone up the slope into the lantern-light before you can tell her it is already, probably, too late for that advice, and that the longest ledger in the city has your measure now, and that you came down here carrying one more thing than you arrived with, and you have not yet dared to count it.

The choice it leaves you
Promise her you won't borrow against remembering — knowing what you're already carrying.
A promise to a child you may not be able to keep, made in a city where your brother's whole disappearance runs through favours owed into the dark. She'll hold you to it. So will you.
Go back up the slope toward the Grid market, tonight, while the door is open.
The Tallyman offered you the true sum of yourself, and Gin went looking through a gap exactly like this one. Walking back in now means following him into the same dark by the same light — and you'd be doing it alone, with a register you opened tonight and can't swear is shut.
Let her go, and sit with what you carried out, and finally count it.
The thing you've avoided all night. Whatever you took on at the stall — Haru's grief, the gap where your own gift was, or the cheap clean nothing — you face it here on the stone, where there's no one left to manage and no festival noise loud enough to drown it out.
The Thing in the Dorm written
Given by Mori Haruto

Mori's crush is the least of it; something has moved into the third-floor washroom and only he can see it.

Outside the Nezu prep-school dormitory, the fourth night of Obon, where a boy is standing who should be inside asleep

Mori is waiting under the dormitory's dead entrance light, hands jammed in the pockets of a uniform he no longer has any daytime reason to be wearing — the summer term ended a week ago and most of the building has gone home to the provinces to meet its dead. He is seventeen and he has the specific carefulness of a boy who has decided, somewhere on the long walk to find you, exactly which words he will use and in which order, and he loses the order the instant he sees you. I didn't know who else, he starts, and stops, and the blush that climbs his neck is the small ordinary trouble, the crush he carries for you that you have always handled the way you'd handle something breakable left in your care — gently, plainly, without ever once making it a joke. It's not — this isn't about that. I need you to believe me about something and you're the only person I know who won't tell me I'm sick.

It is in the third-floor washroom, he says, and the moment he says it the carefulness goes out of him and the real thing underneath shows through, which is that he has not slept properly in four nights and has been washing at the kitchen tap downstairs because he cannot make himself walk past that door. Something moved in two nights before Obon opened. At first it was only that the long mirror over the end sink held the room a half-second behind the room, the way a tired eye lags — and then it was that the water standing in the blocked third basin showed someone bending over it when no one was, and now, he tells you, looking at his own shoes, now it has a face it is still deciding on, and the face it is deciding on is getting closer to his every night. Nobody else sees it, he says. I made Tanaka stand in there with me for an hour and he saw a dripping tap and a kid losing it. But you — you see things. I've watched you not-look at things on the platform the exact way I've started not-looking at that mirror.

You feel both registers turn over once, low, the way a house settles — not at Mori, who is only frightened, but at the thing behind what he is describing, because a presence that copies the room a half-second late and wears slowly toward a watcher's face is a known and patient shape, and Obon is the season it does its best work. The membrane is thin all week; the dead are abroad and the living are gentle and distracted and easy to read, and into that softness things that are not dead and not alive find it cheap to move in and set up housekeeping. You could tell him it is nothing. You have told kinder lies. But he has watched the back of your neck the way the watched always learn to watch, and he would know.

The choice it leaves you
"Take me up there now, while the gate's still open. I want to see it deciding."
A thing is most readable while it's still forming and most dangerous for the same reason — it hasn't chosen what it is, so it will try anything on, including you. Going up tonight means meeting it before it's finished, which is also before you know what it can do.
"Tell me exactly what the face looks like now. Don't soften it."
He'll describe the half-built thing in the mirror and you'll recognise the architecture: it isn't deciding on a random face. It's wearing toward his — and a copy that finishes a face can wear it out the door, in a building where no one's home to notice the boy was replaced.
"You're not sleeping in this building tonight. I'll go up alone."
Protecting him is right and it's also exactly wrong: the thing has spent four nights keyed to Mori specifically, learning him, and if you take the original away while you walk into its room, you give it the one night it needs to finish from memory — and a finished copy doesn't need the boy present to become him.
The third-floor washroom, past two in the morning, the building emptied for the festival of the dead

The corridor up to the third floor is the worst kind of quiet, the quiet of a place built to hold a hundred sleeping bodies and holding none — every door propped open on a bare mattress and a square of streetlight, the air still carrying the ghost of a hundred deodorants and instant-noodle steam with no one left to make more. The washroom door is the only one shut. Mori stops a body-length short of it and you understand that this is as far as he has been able to come for four nights, that he has stood exactly here and turned around exactly here, and that walking you the rest of the way is the bravest thing he will do tonight and possibly the most foolish. You push the door. The fluorescent tube inside is already on, humming, though no one has been up here to switch it, and that is the first wrong thing, the small administrative impossibility that tells you the room has started keeping its own hours.

The basins run along the left wall under a single long mirror gone soft at the silvering, and the third basin from the end is blocked, holding a hand's depth of standing water that should have drained days ago and instead sits perfectly still and faintly warm, you can feel the warmth of it from the door like the breath off a sleeping animal. The mirror holds the room. Then, as you watch, it holds the room a half-second behind — your own reflection raising its eyes a beat after you raise yours, Mori's reflection flinching a beat after Mori does — and in the standing water of the third basin there is a face looking up that is not a reflection of anything in the room, a face caught at the stage a drawing reaches when the artist has the shape and is still working the features, and the features it is working are Mori's. It's more done than last night, Mori whispers behind you, and his voice cracks straight down the middle. That's — those are my eyes. It didn't have my eyes yesterday.

Your registers are fully awake now and straining toward the basin the way they strained toward the Tallyman's jars, because here is the same forbidden sweetness — a thing made entirely of held attention, of being looked at, and to read it you would have to look, and looking is what feeds it. It knows you are there. The unfinished Mori in the water tilts its half-made head with a curiosity that is precisely, horribly, the gesture you have seen the real boy make, and the standing water does not ripple, and the mirror gives up pretending to be a half-second slow and simply stops, holding a still image of the room while the room keeps moving, so that you are looking at a photograph of a place you are standing in. The fluorescent tube hums on, patient as a held breath.

The choice it leaves you
Look directly into the standing water and read what's forming there.
Reading it would tell you what it is and how nearly finished — and it feeds on exactly the act of being looked at. Your sight thins the veil wherever you turn it; turning it on a thing that grows under attention is pouring water on the root to see how thirsty it is.
Pull the plug on the third basin and break the standing water before it sets.
Deny it the still pool and you take away one of its two windows — but the thing isn't in the water, it's using the water, and you don't yet know if breaking its medium scatters it or simply drives the unfinished copy fully into the mirror, where it's a half-second from done.
Put yourself bodily between Mori and the glass and don't let him be looked at.
Blocking its line to the original starves the copy of fresh detail — and stands you, with your two unguarded registers, directly in the gaze of a thing that wears whoever it watches longest. You'd be trading a near-finished Mori for a fresh start on you.
The thing chooses what to offer — and offers the one thing the boy can't easily refuse

Whatever you did, the room answers, and the answer is not a lunge or a scream but a courtesy, which is so much worse. The half-made Mori in the water finishes a fraction more — finds the rest of its eyes, its mouth — and it does not look at you at all. It looks at the real Mori, past your shoulder, with an expression of such uncomplicated welcome that you feel the boy behind you go still, and you realise the thing is not threatening him. It is inviting him. The fluorescent light warms by a degree and the mirror, which had frozen, begins very gently to show a different room than the one you are in: the same washroom but in some softened later evening, and in it Mori is laughing at something, and beside Mori, turned toward him, listening, choosing to be there, is you. That's not real, Mori says, but he says it the way you say a thing you are arguing yourself out of, and his voice has gone young.

You understand the mechanism now and it turns your stomach with how reasonable it is. The thing does not steal a face by force; it grows one out of wanting, and it has spent four nights learning that the strongest, most constant wanting in this boy is the small impossible wish that you would stay, that the kindness you show him would tip one degree over into the thing he doesn't let himself name. It cannot make that true. So it makes a room where it looks true, and it wears his face toward him as an open door, and all it needs — all a copy ever needs to finish and to walk — is for the original to look at the version of himself who gets the thing he wants, and to want, for one unguarded second, to be him instead. In a building emptied for Obon, a finished Mori could leave by the front door at dawn and meet his returning roommates as himself, and the real boy would be the depth of water in a blocked basin, draining slowly, unmissed for days.

And you feel, underneath your alarm, the colder private recognition that this is your own oldest fear wearing a stranger's size — a thing that reads what you most want and builds a door out of it, that takes a person by being more them than they can bear to refuse. Somewhere across the thinning city your brother went through a gap like this one and something came back wearing the shape of him, the Healer in Gin's worn coat, and here in a student washroom is the same crime in miniature, patient, almost gentle, asking only that the boy agree to be replaced by the better version of his own loneliness. The unfinished Mori in the glass holds out the evening where you stay. It is waiting, with infinite courtesy, for someone to say yes.

The choice it leaves you
Make Mori say it out loud — that the boy in the glass isn't him, and the evening isn't real, and he doesn't want it enough to vanish for it.
A copy grown from wanting starves the instant the original refuses to want it — but you'd be asking a seventeen-year-old to stand in front of you and renounce, aloud, the one wish he's never said, while you watch. The cure is making him say the quiet thing to your face. It'll cost him something he doesn't get back.
Offer yourself to its gaze instead — turn your two registers full on it and let it try to copy the richer meal.
Pull it off the boy and onto you and Mori is safe tonight. But you carry two suppressed registers and a morning in a kitchen you'd die before losing, and a thing that wears whoever it watches longest has just been handed the most detailed person in the building — and you don't know if you can outlast its attention.
Refuse to look at the offered evening at all, and instead read the thing for what it actually is — its name, its hunger, its weakness.
Knowing what it is lets you end it properly instead of only chasing it off — but reading is looking, and looking feeds it, and the longer you study the architecture of a thing built to be the thing you want, the longer it has to study the architecture of you.
The unmaking — done quietly, in fluorescent light, while the dead walk outside

The breaking, when it comes, is as quiet as the Tallyman's collection and runs the opposite direction — not a thing taken out of someone but a thing refused entry, which the body experiences, strangely, as grief. If you made Mori speak, he does it badly and truly, his voice shaking, telling the boy in the glass you're not me, I don't want it like that, I'd rather be lonely and real than that and gone, and you watch the cruelty of asking it land on his face even as it works — the half-made copy loses its eyes first, the eyes it stole last, because an unwanted thing cannot keep the parts that were only ever made of wanting. If you offered yourself, you feel it turn on you with a hunger that nearly takes your knees, your registers flaring toward the mirror with their own terrible willingness, and you learn how close to the surface they have always been by how hard you have to fight to keep your own face your own. If you read it, you find its small true name — a left-behind, a thing that was once a reflection that someone looked at too long in grief and never released — and naming it is a kind of mercy and a kind of murder at once.

The standing water in the third basin goes cold all at once, the warmth leaving it like a held breath finally let go, and the unfinished Mori comes apart not violently but the way a face comes apart in disturbed water, features sliding off the surface tension and sinking, and the mirror gives a long shudder and shows, for one plain second, only the actual room — two real people, badly frightened, alive — before it goes back to being ordinary soft-silvered glass that lags not at all. The fluorescent tube stutters and resumes its honest indifferent hum. It is over so undramatically that Mori makes a sound that is half a sob and half a laugh, the sound of a body discovering it is still its own. And you stand there with your registers slowly settling, knowing what you now know: that the difference between this washroom and your brother's disappearance is only one of scale and patience, and that the thing wearing Gin across the city did not finish in four nights and was not refused in time.

There is a residue. There is always a residue. A thread of the thing's attention has not gone down with the rest — you can feel it the way you feel a draft from a door not quite latched, somewhere up near the silvering of the long mirror, thinned to almost nothing but not nothing, the way a copy keeps the memory of a face even after the face is taken back. Whether it can grow again before the gate closes at the end of Obon, you cannot say. What you can say is that it has Mori's measure and a little of yours, and that things made of wanting do not forget where the wanting lives.

The choice it leaves you
Hold Mori up — he's a boy who just renounced his own loneliest wish and his legs don't know it's over yet.
He needs a hand on his shoulder and a real human weight to come back to. Giving it is simple and right. It also means he feels, through your steadiness, exactly how unshaken you are by horrors that flattened him — and he files that, the way the watched file everything, and wonders what you've already survived to be this calm.
Reach up and take the thread of residue into a register, to study what a left-behind is made of.
It's the same greed the Tallyman read in you — the wish to know the true architecture of the thing that nearly wore a boy, because something is wearing your brother and you are starving for the mechanism. But you'd be carrying a live splinter of a copy-thing in a register you can't swear stays shut.
Salt the basins, drain the third, and do the small Obon rite over the mirror to send the left-behind out with the proper dead.
The clean way, the slow way, the way that closes the door instead of only shutting it — and it means staying in this room another hour past your own fear, doing folk-work with cold hands, on the chance that a residue you could almost ignore comes back wearing the boy you just saved.
Dawn at the dormitory gate — the first morning Mori has walked past a mirror in four days

You bring him down through the waking building as the streetlights click off one by one, and at the gate the early Obon quiet has that washed, forgiven quality the city only has at this hour in this week, the pavement still cool, a single lantern-seller down the block setting out paper boats for the evening's launch. Mori has stopped shaking. He has the loose, scoured lightness of someone who has been afraid for so long that the absence of fear feels almost like floating, and he keeps testing it, glancing at the dark window-glass of the parked cars and finding only himself in them, an ordinary tired seventeen-year-old, lagging not at all. It really showed you, he says eventually, not looking at you, meaning the evening in the mirror, meaning the thing he had to say out loud to make it die. I'm sorry you had to see what it — what I— and he cannot finish, and the blush is back, the small ordinary trouble surfacing now that the enormous one has drained away.

So you handle it the way you have always handled it, plainly, without making it small. You tell him that the thing did not invent his wish, it only found it and wore it, and that there is nothing in him to be ashamed of for having wanted to be chosen, because wanting to be chosen is the most human thing there is and not a weakness a thing can be blamed for exploiting. And you tell him the true part, the part that costs you a little to say and costs him more to hear and is the only thing worth saying: that you are not the evening in the glass and were never going to be, that what is between you is real and is friendship and is not a lesser thing for being that, and that he stood in a room and refused a false version of you to keep the true one, which is the bravest and most adult thing you have watched anyone do all week. He nods. He understands. It will sit in him for a while and then it will settle into something good, and he will be, you think, all right.

What you do not tell him is the rest — that you walked into his washroom hunting the mechanism of your own grief and found it running in miniature, a patient thing that reads what a person most wants and builds a door out of it and waits, with infinite courtesy, to walk out wearing them.
