That something across the city did exactly this to your brother and was not made to say the true thing out loud in time. That you felt your own two registers strain toward the glass with a willingness that frightened you more than the copy did, because it means you, too, are a thing that could be worn, a door someone is already standing patiently outside of. Down the block the lantern-seller lights the first of the day's paper boats, and Mori watches it the way you both watched the basin — carefully, from a body-length back — and asks, in a small voice, whether the thing is really gone. You tell him the truth, which is that you closed the door, and that doors in this season do not always stay closed, and that you will check the washroom every night until the gate shuts and the dead go home. It is the most honest comfort you have. He takes it. The morning comes up over the dormitory roof, indifferent and clean, and for now there is only one of each of you, which is, this week, a great deal to be able to say.

The choice it leaves you
Tell him the truth about why you came up there — that you know exactly what it is to be hunted for your face.
It would mean something to him to know he wasn't a charity case, that you needed that room as much as he did. It would also hand a frightened seventeen-year-old the knowledge that the person he trusts most is being stalked by a bigger version of what nearly ate him — and you can't take that back once he's carrying it.
Promise to check the washroom every night until the gate closes — and keep the promise.
The residue up in the silvering isn't nothing, and a left-behind grows where the wanting lives, and Mori's wanting hasn't gone anywhere just because he said the words once. Keeping the promise ties you to this dormitory for the rest of Obon, the same nights your brother's trail is thinning fastest across the city.
Walk him to the river and put a lantern in his hands for the self he refused to become.
A boat for the boy in the glass who got the evening — grieving, properly, the false future he gave up so the true one could stay real. It's the right rite and it costs nothing but the admission, made beside a child, that you have your own such boat to launch and have not yet found the nerve to light it.
Tanizaki's Drowned Client written
Given by Tanizaki

A Grid fixer wants a body found before the river gives it up on its own terms.

Tanizaki's back room above the shuttered tea shop, the second afternoon of Obon, rain not yet arrived but promised

Tanizaki pours before he speaks, which is how you know the matter is serious — the old man has spent forty years in Kita learning that nothing true gets said over an empty cup, and he has never once wasted the gesture on small business. The room smells of roasted barley and the river two streets over, that brown August smell of low tide and lantern-wax that the whole district wears this week, and through the rice-paper the light has gone the colour of weak tea itself, flat and waiting. He sets your cup down with both hands and folds his own around his and says, without preamble, that a man went into the Sumida three nights ago and has not come up, and that this is not a police matter and is not going to become one, and that he needs the man found before the river gives him up on its own terms.

You let him talk. The drowned man's name was Kase — a fixer, Grid-side, the kind of careful unregistered go-between who arranges that a thing change hands without either hand being seen, and Tanizaki had used him for thirty years the way you use a tool you trust enough to stop noticing. Three nights ago Kase sent word he had something that had frightened him, something he wanted off his hands and into Tanizaki's before Obon's gate opened all the way, and then he sent nothing, and then a Grid courier found his flat aired and empty with the kettle still warm and his shoes by the door. A man does not walk to the river barefoot in this season by accident, Tanizaki says, and turns his cup a quarter-turn on the table, and does not drink. And the Sumida in Obon does not keep what it takes the ordinary way. It launches the dead all week. I would rather find Kase before the river decides he is one of them.

Both registers move at that, low, the way they move when something true is being undersold — because Tanizaki is not asking you to recover a body, not really, he is asking you to read one, and he knows what reading a drowned man this week will pull through you and is asking anyway, which is itself a measure of his fear. You have known the old man as the one person in Kita who never spends you carelessly, who shaped your brother's whole field practice with exactly this restraint, and to watch him push a cup toward you with a thing he wants kept off the official ledger is to understand that whatever Kase was carrying, it reaches further than one fixer's bad night by the water. The rain has not started. Outside, somewhere down toward the embankment, the first of the evening's lantern-sellers begins setting out paper boats no one has yet died recently enough to need.

The choice it leaves you
"What was he carrying that frightened him? Tell me before I go near the water."
Tanizaki will tell you as much as he knows, which is little and pointed — and knowing what Kase held means you'll be reading the river for it too, not just for a body. The more precisely you know what you're hunting, the more precisely it can be hunting back through the same thinned water.
"Why keep it off the police book? A drowning in Obon is the most ordinary thing in Tokyo."
His answer will tell you who else is interested in Kase's last errand and why a clean municipal recovery would be the wrong kind of clean. But pressing the old man on why he wants it quiet invites him to tell you exactly how far up the obligation runs — and once you know, you're inside it.
Say nothing; drink the tea, take the address of Kase's flat, and go to the water cold.
Going in without the frame keeps your reading uncontaminated by what Tanizaki expects you to find — you'll see only what the river shows. It also means meeting whatever held a careful man under, with no idea what it wanted from him or what it might recognise in you.
The Sumida embankment after dark, downstream of Kase's flat, the first lanterns going onto the water

The river at this hour is a black road carrying small fires. Toro nagashi has begun upstream and the lanterns come down in loose constellations, paper boxes each with a candle and a name brushed on the side, the families' grief made buoyant and sent out to float the dead home across the dark — and you stand on the concrete embankment where the city's romance about the river ends and its plumbing begins, smelling diesel and silt, watching the little lights come on past you toward the bay. This is the worst place to use your sight and you came here to use it. The membrane is at its thinnest all week and nowhere thinner than over running water at the festival of the dead, and the moment you let the first register open a hand's width the whole surface of the Sumida tilts in your perception from a river with lanterns on it to a procession, a long patient column of the departing moving with the current, and every paper flame is suddenly attended.

You walk the embankment downstream, reading for the wrong note in a song of the dead — and a drowned man three nights gone is a wrong note, because the properly dead this week are leaving and Kase is not leaving, Kase is held. You feel him before you see anything, the way you feel a snag in cloth, somewhere down past the third pier where the current goes slack behind a concrete footing and the lanterns that drift in there turn slow circles and do not come out. The held place has a temperature your sight reads as cold and your skin reads as nothing at all. Down there, in the slack water behind the pier, something keeps a man the way the third basin kept a half-made boy — not eating him, not yet, just holding, the way you hold a thing you have not decided whether to use.

And here is the part Tanizaki could not have prepared you for, the part both registers strain toward with their old forbidden appetite: the held place is not silent. It is leaking. A drowned man read this fresh, this snagged, still has his last quarter-hour in him the way a struck bell holds its note, and you can feel Kase's final minutes pressing out against the river's grip — the cold, the weight of his own coat, a decision, a hand that was or was not on the back of his neck — all of it down there waiting to be looked at, and looking is exactly what thins the wall, and the wall over this slack water is already thin enough to put your fingers through. The lanterns turn their slow circles behind the pier. One of them, you notice, has no name brushed on its side at all.

The choice it leaves you
Open the register fully and read the slack water from the bank, before you go down to it.
Reading from a distance tells you what's holding Kase and how, so you go down knowing — but opening fully over running water during Obon is putting your hand through the thinnest membrane in the city, and a thing that holds the drowned will feel the warm living attention arrive and turn, unhurried, to see what's looking.
Climb down to the slack water and find the body with your hands, registers shut, sight ordinary.
Going in blind keeps your sight off the membrane and denies the held thing the meal of your attention — but it means lowering yourself into cold slack water in the dark to feel for a three-day drowning by touch, with whatever kept him circling somewhere you can't see, and trusting your hands to know a held man from a held thing.
Pull the nameless lantern out of the slack water first and read it instead of the man.
A lantern with no name that won't leave the held place isn't a stranger's grief gone astray — it's bait, or a marker, or Kase's own, and reading it might tell you who put him there without making you look at the body at all. But taking the thing the held place is keeping is the same as knocking, and it will answer.
Behind the third pier, knee-deep in slack water, the moment the river gives Kase up

He is where you knew he was, hung in the slack behind the footing with one sleeve snagged on rebar and the rest of him turning slow in the eddy, face down, coat heavy as a drowned animal — and the river, when you take his wrist, does not fight you for him the way you braced for. It gives him up. That is the first wrong thing and the worst: a body three nights held in slack water should be claimed, should be the river's, and instead the current eases its grip the instant you commit to taking him, the way a thing hands you something it wanted you to have all along. You turn him over in the dark and the lantern-light from upstream slides across a face that drowning has been gentle with, slack and almost untroubled, a careful man who at the very end stopped being careful — and the last quarter-hour, freed by your touch, comes off him into your open register like cold off a stone.

You read it because you cannot not, and it is not what Tanizaki sold you. Kase did not slip. Kase walked here barefoot in the dark with the thing that frightened him buttoned into his coat, because the thing that frightened him was a name — a list, a small waxed-paper ledger of who in Kita had taken a particular favour in the last two years and what each favour had quietly cost the membrane to grant — and Kase had understood, three nights too late, that to carry such a list was to be a loose thread in something very large and very patient, and that the only place in the city thin enough to lose it where no register could read it again was the bottom of the Sumida in Obon. He did not drown himself out of fear. He drowned himself to hide the ledger somewhere even the dead could not be made to give it up, and he walked in barefoot and unhurried, and the river, this week, was glad to keep them both.

And the held thing is here, of course it is here, you have felt it the whole time the way you feel a watcher behind glass — not a demon and not a ghost but the slack place itself gone aware, a stretch of Obon river that has held a hidden man and a hidden name for three nights and learned, the way standing water learns, the taste of being a secret-keeper. It does not want Kase. It wants the going-on of it, the warm thing now kneeling in its water with two registers open and a brother across the city it can feel you ache toward — and it offers, in the wordless slack-water way, to hold for you too, to take whatever you most need hidden and keep it at the bottom where no Healer and no Directorate and no patient brother-shaped thing could ever read it off you. The ledger is buttoned cold against the dead man's chest. The nameless lantern turns its slow circle by your knee. The current waits to see what you do with all three.

The choice it leaves you
Take the ledger off the body — Kishin would kill for who-owes-what in Kita, and so would the people on it.
The waxed list is the single most valuable object you've touched all week: a map of every quiet favour and its membrane-cost, which is a map straight toward what Yushiro is doing to your brother. Carrying it out of the river makes you the loose thread Kase died to cut — and the slack place will remember exactly who took the secret it was keeping.
Leave the ledger where the dead man drowned to hide it, and bring up only Kase.
Honouring a man's last terrible decision means giving Tanizaki a body and no answers, and leaving the one document that might explain your brother at the bottom of a river you'd have to read again to recover. It's the clean grief and the coward's mercy at once, and you'll never be sure which.
Let the slack water make its offer real — give it one thing of yours to hold while you decide.
The river is offering the safest hiding place in Tokyo for the one truth you can't afford anyone to read off you, and the relief of it is enormous and immediate. It is also exactly how it took Kase: one careful man's reasonable wish to hide a single thing, and a patient water glad to keep the man along with it.
On the embankment again, soaked, the body laid out under a tarp, deciding what the river is allowed to keep

You get him out — that much you do not let yourself negotiate — and lay Kase on the concrete above the tide-line under a courier's tarp Tanizaki's network leaves cached for exactly such nights, and you kneel there dripping while the lanterns keep coming down past the pier, indifferent, name after name floating out toward a sea that does not read. Whatever you carried up the bank with you sits heavy and cold against your own chest or sits at the bottom where Kase put it, and either way you have made the choice that the rest of this week will be lived inside. The slack water behind the footing has gone ordinary again, just river, the awareness in it lapsed back into mere current the moment it got — or did not get — what it offered to hold, and you understand that you will not be able to tell anyone the difference between the version where you outwitted it and the version where you simply weren't worth keeping.

There is the matter of the body, which is also the matter of the truth. A barefoot drowning in Obon is the most ordinary death in Tokyo and the most explicable, and you could hand Kase to the morning and the municipal book and let him be a sad seasonal statistic, the river's romance intact, no one the wiser about waxed ledgers or favours that cost the wall. Or you could give him the rite he chose for himself — slide him back to the proper dead with a lantern and a name, let the festival carry out a man who walked into it on purpose — and keep his death off every book, which is what Tanizaki asked and what Kase died to arrange. The rain has finally started, fine and warm, dimpling the black water and guttering the nearer candles, and the dead go on leaving regardless, and you have to decide whose terms Kase departs on: the river's, the city's, or his own.

Both registers are still half-open and slow to close, the way they always are after running water, and through them you feel the shape of what you've actually been doing down here, which is not a favour for an old man at all. You came to the Sumida to practise the unbearable thing — to read a held person whom a patient power did not want to release, to take or refuse a hidden truth, to sit knee-deep in a thinned place and not be kept — because somewhere across the city your brother is held exactly so, in slack water of a different kind, by a Healer glad to keep him, and you do not yet have the nerve or the method to go in after him. Kase was rehearsal. The knowledge sits in you cold as the river: that you could find Gin, perhaps, the way you found this man, and that finding is the easy half, and that the water does not always give up what you commit to taking.

The choice it leaves you
Launch Kase yourself — a lantern, his name, the proper rite, his death off every book.
Giving him the departure he chose honours a brave and terrible decision and keeps Tanizaki's promise of silence. It also means you, personally, perform the rite to send a man out with the dead this week — and standing over the water saying the words for someone else's chosen drowning teaches your hands how it would be done for one you love.
Call it in clean — let Kase be an ordinary Obon drowning on the municipal book, truth buried with the routine.
The official version protects you and everyone on the ledger by making the death boring, which is the safest thing a death can be. But it spends Tanizaki's trust, brands you to the Grid as someone who hands their dead to the authorities, and lets the city's lie about the river stand over a man who died telling the truth about it.
Bring Kase and what he carried straight to Tanizaki and put the whole choice in the old man's hands.
Tanizaki has forty years of judgement you don't, and he loved Kase in his restrained way, and the decision is arguably his to make. Handing it up also hands him the ledger — and you'll have learned nothing about whether you can carry such a weight alone, the night before the week demands you carry a heavier one.
Dawn, back in the room above the tea shop, the cup poured for a man who isn't coming

Tanizaki has set out two cups and filled three, which is the old form, the third for the one who has gone, and he does not explain it and you do not ask. You come in soaked to the river-line with the night still on you and tell him what you found, all of it or the careful part of it, and he listens the way he listens to everything, without moving, turning his cup the slow quarter-turn that means he is rearranging forty years of a district to fit a new and terrible fact into it. When you finish he is quiet for a long time, and then he says the thing you did not expect, which is that Kase had carried for Gin too, years back, the same kind of waxed quiet errands, and that your brother had been the one to teach the fixer the trick of hiding a thing in running water where no register could reach — and that if Kase chose the Sumida, he chose it because Gin once told him it was the one safe place, and so even this, even a fixer's chosen drowning, has your brother's careful handwriting in the margin.

He pours the third cup out on the floor, the smallest libation, and tells you what the old give the young when they have nothing else: that the river takes everyone on its own terms in the end and that the only mercy available is to make the terms a person's own choosing, which Kase did, and which is more than most of the dead this week managed. He does not thank you in words. He slides the safe-house key across the table, and the ward methodology written in his own hand, and the beginning of Gin's thirty-year field profile — the things he gives only at full trust — and you understand that the drowned client was, among everything else, the assessment, and that you have passed it the hard way, by being trusted with a death that had to stay off the book. The rain has stopped. The barley smell is back. Outside the dead are mostly gone home and the lanterns have all reached the sea.

What stays with you, walking out into the washed grey morning, is not the ledger or the key or even Kase's almost-peaceful face, but the small unbearable lesson the river handed up with the body: that hiding is a kind of drowning, that a held thing can be loved into staying held, and that your brother — who taught a fixer the safe place, who is himself now hidden in slack water of his own — knew this trick from the inside long before the Healer ever wore his coat. You have rehearsed, tonight, the exact shape of the thing you will have to do for Gin, and you have learned its exact cost, and you have learned that the water does not always give up what you commit to taking, and you have decided to go in after him anyway. The cup Tanizaki poured for you has gone cold. You drink it. It would be an insult, this morning, to leave anything the river could keep.

The choice it leaves you
Take the key, the methodology, and Gin's profile — accept the safe house and everything that owing Tanizaki now means.
A master's forty years, a base no one can read, and the truest outside portrait of your brother — the strongest foundation you can build on in Kita. It also makes Tanizaki's enemies yours and binds you to a man who just proved he'll spend a beloved fixer's death to test you, and will, when it matters, spend you the same careful way.
Ask Tanizaki, plainly, to tell you everything Gin ever hid in running water — and whether Gin could be found the same way.
The old man knows your brother's methods better than anyone alive, and this is the question the whole night was really asking. But naming aloud, to Gin's own mentor, that you mean to go into the slack water after him makes the plan real and makes Tanizaki complicit, and he may decide the kindest thing is to stop you.
Say nothing about Gin; pocket the lesson, keep the river's offer to yourself, and let the old man think this was only ever about Kase.
Keeping your own counsel protects Tanizaki from knowing what you intend and keeps your one possible method secret from everyone, which is the careful Grid way — Kase's way. It's also exactly how a careful person ends up walking barefoot to the water alone, with the thing that frightens them buttoned cold against their chest and no one told in time.
The Broker's Vacant Mediums written
Given by Suzuran-dō

Already-trained animal-mediums, waiting for hands steady enough — and one that will not be re-homed.

Suzuran-dō, a shopfront the width of two arms in a Kita backstreet, the fourth evening of Obon, the cages quiet in a way that is not sleep

The shop has no light of its own; it borrows what the festival lanterns let down the alley, so that the cages along the back wall surface and submerge as people pass the mouth of the street outside. Komori-san does not turn on a lamp for you. She is the kind of old woman who has decided light is a thing you spend, and she has spent a lifetime of it on animals that other people could not keep and could not bear to drown, and she watches you cross her threshold with the flat patience of someone who has already guessed why you came and is only waiting to see how badly you'll ask. The place smells of straw and warm fur and, under it, the particular mineral cold that clings to anything that has carried a passenger — that smell you have learned to read off your own brother's empty room.

She keeps animal-mediums here, she says, the way another shop keeps umbrellas: trained, waiting, sized to the hand. A myna that can hold a single sentence of the dead and give it back clean. A pair of carp that go still over thinned water the way a compass goes still over true north. A cat with a stripe of white at the throat that will sit on the chest of the recently-grieving and take the worst of the weight off them for an hour, which is a mercy and a theft in the same motion. They are good instruments, she tells you, broken in gently, and any one of them would steady a sensitive who works alone — and she lets the word alone sit in the air between you long enough that you understand she has already read your solitude off you like a price tag.

Both registers move at the cages, low and unhappy, the way they move near anything that has been hollowed to make room — because that is what a trained medium is, an animal taught to leave a little of itself vacant so something else can sit there, and your sight cannot help reading the small repeated emptiness in each of them as a kind of wound that has been kept open on purpose. Komori watches you not-flinch and almost approves. You feel it, she says. Most don't. The ones who feel it are the ones I'll sell to. And then, before you can answer, her eyes go to the one cage she has not offered you, low in the corner, where something old and brown lies with its nose to the bars and has not once looked away from the door you came in through.

The choice it leaves you
"Show me the steady ones. I work alone — I could use an instrument I can trust."
Komori will sell you a good medium, and it will help, and you will spend the rest of the case feeling the small kept-open wound of it against your own register every time you reach. An instrument that works because someone hollowed it gentle — and you would be the someone now.
"The one in the corner. The one you didn't offer me. What is it waiting for?"
She didn't offer it because it can't be sold, and asking means asking about a grief she keeps in a cage by the door — and once you know what it's waiting for, the not-helping becomes a thing you chose.
Read the cages first — let your sight tell you what each animal is carrying before money is mentioned.
Reading hollowed things this close, this many at once, opens both registers wider than a backstreet can hold, and the festival has already thinned the air outside. You'll know exactly what you're buying — and everything in the room will know it's being read.
The corner cage, the brown dog lifting its head only enough to be sure you are not the one it is waiting for

Her name is Tsuyu, Komori says, which means the dew, the kind that is gone by the time anyone wakes to see it, and she is older than she looks and was never meant to be a stock animal at all. She belonged to a man named Asano, an unregistered sensitive who worked the Kita line and trained her himself, slow and kind, to hold one thread at a time and give it back unbroken — a courier's dog for messages the dead leave half-spoken. Eleven nights ago Asano walked north and did not come back, and a neighbour brought Tsuyu here because the shop is where the district's hollow animals come to die quietly, and she has not eaten properly since and will not be re-homed, because she is not finished with the job he gave her: she is still holding his thread, the last one, waiting to give it to the only hands she was trained to give it to.

You crouch to the bars and she does not warm to you, which is correct — she is a professional, and you are not her handler — but she lets you close enough to feel it, and it is unmistakable. There is a passenger in her still, faint, frayed at the edge the way a thread frays when the other end has been pulled too far away too fast, and it is unmistakably a man, and unmistakably going. Komori has tried to ease it out of her the way she eases the others, so the cage might be useful again, and Tsuyu has refused every time, clamping down on the fading thing inside her like a dog refusing to drop a stick, because dropping it is the one thing Asano never taught her and the one thing she will not do without him. She'll die holding it, Komori says, without cruelty, only accounting. That's not loyalty. That's a job that never got a stop word.

And here is the shape of it, cold and exact: the thread Tsuyu is holding is Asano, or the last of him she could keep, and Asano went north in Obon, alone and unregistered and uncounted — the exact profile of the tidying you have been reading the edges of for days, the half-touched and the unguarded swept gentle up the Kita line toward the thinning wall by a familiar warmth that makes the leaving feel like being led home. You did not come here for the spine of your own case. You came for an instrument. But the corner cage has handed you a witness instead — a witness with no words, no file, no admissibility, who watched a man be invited north and has been holding the proof against her ribs ever since, waiting for hands steady enough to take it without breaking it.

The choice it leaves you
Open your registers and take the thread from her — read Asano's last walk the way she's been holding it.
She was trained to give it to her handler, not to you; taking it means convincing a grieving animal you are close enough to count, and reading a man's frayed last hour will pull it through you the way the river pulled Kase. You'd get the clearest map yet of how the tidying is done — paid for in a register held open over a dying dog.
Don't take it. Sit with her until she chooses — let the dog decide whether you're the steady hands.
Patience is the honest way and the slow way, and the thread is fraying while you wait; she may finish dying before she trusts you, and then the proof and the dog go out together. The kindest road may cost you the witness entirely.
Ask Komori to ease it out the way she eases the others, cleanly, and let Tsuyu rest.
It would end her suffering and free the cage — and it would scrape the last of Asano off the one surface he survived on, close the only window onto his walk north, and teach you that mercy and erasure can be the same gesture done at the right angle.
The thread, given or taken or refused — and the cold clear sense of where it leads

If you reached for it, Tsuyu does not fight you the way she fought Komori; she has been waiting eleven nights for hands that feel like the work, and yours, opened wide and unafraid of the hollow in her, feel close enough. The thread comes across into you whole and frayed, and for the length of one held breath you are Asano on his last walk — not his memories, his going, the particular footstep-by-footstep ease of a man who never noticed he'd decided to leave, drawn north past the embankment by a warmth he took for a friend's, the lanterns kind, the streets obliging, the whole night arranged so that walking away from his own life felt exactly like walking home. You feel where the obliging stopped. You feel the half-second of late air. You feel him think, once, of the dog — and then the thread ends, not cut, just arrived somewhere you cannot follow on foot, and you come back to the cage with the map of the tidying drawn in a dead man's gait and a dog going slack with eleven nights of finished waiting under your hands.

If you sat and waited, the choice was hers, and dogs are not generous with strangers — but Tsuyu is a professional, and somewhere in the long quiet she weighs you against the only handler she ever had and decides you are not him but you are the work, and she gives it the only way she has left, by laying her head against your sternum and simply letting go, the thread passing the way warmth passes between two bodies in the cold. You get less of it this way, no walk, no map, only the shape of the leaving — gentle, invited, northward — but you get it clean, unforced, a witness's testimony freely given, and you get the weight of having been chosen by something that had every reason to die distrustful and didn't.

If you asked Komori to ease her, the old woman does it with hands that have done it a hundred times, and it is quiet and it is kind and it is the wrong kind, and you watch the last of Asano go out of the dog like breath off a mirror — not delivered, not witnessed, just tidied, the same verb the thing up north uses, done now by your own request in a borrowed back room. The cage will be useful again. Tsuyu sleeps and does not wake afraid. And you understand, standing in the borrowed dark, that you have just learned the precise feeling of choosing the clean erasure over the costly witness, and that the feeling is exactly as comfortable as Komori warned you the easy mediums would be, and that you will recognise it now every time the fast road offers it to you again.

The choice it leaves you
Follow the map north as far as a living person can — verify where Asano's walk ended.
The thread points at the thinning wall, the same edge your brother is being walked toward; following it means lighting yourself up on the Kita line again, brighter and closer, exactly where the Healer would most like a searching sensitive to be.
Take what you have to Tanizaki and the others — make Asano count as evidence, uncounted as he was.
A dog's thread is admissible nowhere, but it corroborates the tidying, and pooling it with Kase and the broker's census builds the one case no single faction can dismiss — at the cost of telling people you trust exactly how bright you let yourself burn to get it.
Keep it to yourself for now — carry the dog, or her absence, and say nothing.
Holding the only witness alone keeps it from being spent by a faction's agenda, and it also means the weight of Asano's leaving sits entirely in you, one more uncounted soul you took responsibility for that no one asked you to save.
Suzuran-dō, the leaving — Komori counting what the night actually cost

Komori does not thank you, because thanks are a thing you spend and she has spent enough on you already, but she walks you to the mouth of the shop, which she does not do for everyone, and she looks at you in the borrowed lantern-light the way she looked at the cages, reading the small new emptiness in you that was not there an hour ago. You took it like a handler, she says, if you reached; or she gave it to you, which she's never done, if you waited; or you chose the kind way, and you'll choose it again, if you asked her to ease it — and in each version it is not quite praise and not quite warning, only the flat accounting of a woman who has watched a great many people learn what they are by what they were willing to do to a dog in a corner cage.

She will not sell you one of the steady ones now. You have shown her the only thing she sells to, she says, which is the difference between someone who feels the kept-open wound and someone who would keep it open for their own convenience, and she has decided which you are, and the deciding is the whole transaction — the mediums were never really for sale, the way the best things in Kita are never really for sale, only offered to see what you'll do. If you ever come back, she tells you, come back for an animal you mean to be the handler of, not an instrument you mean to use, and shut the door on your way out, because the festival is leaning wrong tonight and she would rather the cold stayed in the street where it belongs.

Out in the alley the lanterns are doing their patient work of making a thinned city look like somewhere it is safe to gather, and you carry the night's weight north or homeward or nowhere, depending. Whatever you did with Tsuyu, you came for an instrument and left with a witness, or with the knowledge of having silenced one, and both registers have gone quiet in the particular way they go quiet after they have been made to understand something the easy road would have let you skip. Eleven nights ago a man walked north and felt like he was going home. A dog held the proof against her ribs until hands steady enough came. The only question Obon leaves you, turning out of the backstreet, is whether you will let what she held make the case — or let it be one more gentle, uncounted erasure in a season that is full of them.

The choice it leaves you
North — let the thread point you back toward the spine, toward Gin.
It feeds the main search, and it feeds it with you brighter and surer of the route than ever, which is precisely the condition under which the Healer's whole plan works best.
Home, for now — set the weight down before it sets the terms.
Resting is the sane choice and the slow one; the thinning does not rest, and the part of you that took a dying dog's last thread will be awake at two in the morning whether you are or not.
Back to the others — make Asano count, and let them see what counting him cost you.
The case grows; so does the number of people who now know exactly how far you'll open yourself to find your brother, and knowing it, will worry about it, and may try to stop you.
The Shrine That Stopped Answering written
Given by Miyamoto Kazuo

The wards have held for a century; this week the basin's water stopped reflecting the lanterns.

The Hozumi shrine in upper Kita, third morning of Obon — a fox-shrine the size of a shed, wedged between a parking tower and a shuttered noodle counter, and Miyamoto Kazuo waiting at the gate before you have finished climbing the steps

The old man is sweeping when you arrive, which is the first wrong thing, because the stones are already clean and he is sweeping them anyway — the slow, going-nowhere stroke of a man keeping his hands busy so they will not shake. Miyamoto Kazuo has tended this shrine for fifty-one years and his father tended it before him and the wards that ring the precinct were renewed by a hand in his line every Obon for a hundred summers, and he tells you all of this without your asking, the way a man recites the deed to a house he is afraid is no longer his. The shrine is nothing — two fox statues with the lichen grown into their grins, a vermilion gate gone the colour of dried blood, a basin of standing water under a little roof where you are meant to rinse your hands before you bother the god. It is the kind of place the city forgets so thoroughly that forgetting it becomes its own kind of protection.

He will not let you near the basin until he has said the thing he called you to say, and he says it badly, in pieces, looking at the broom and not at you. For a hundred years the water in that basin has held the lanterns — every Obon the neighbourhood strings paper lights along the lane, and every Obon the basin catches them and holds them doubled and trembling on its skin, and that doubling, Miyamoto says, is how you know the boundary is keeping. A shrine that reflects is a shrine that is still listening. This week the lanterns are lit and the lane is strung and the water lies in the basin flat and black and holds nothing at all — no lights, no roof-beam, not even your face when you lean, he says, when you lean over it your own face is not there.

Both registers come up at that, slow and certain, the low one and the searching one both, and they do not disagree. You have spent enough of this week learning the particular wrongness of a thing that has stopped answering — Gin's flat, the address in no file, the cases closed too clean — to recognise the shape of it in an old man's basin. Miyamoto sets the broom against the gate at last and folds his hands and tells you he is too old to read what has gone wrong and too frightened to leave it unread, that he sent word to the Tower and the Tower sent a form, that he sent word to the temple and the temple sent prayers, and that he is asking you because you are the only one who came up the steps instead of mailing something down them. Behind him the basin sits under its little roof, holding the morning the way a closed eye holds a room.

The choice it leaves you
"Tell me what the wards were keeping out — a hundred years of renewing them, your line knows the name of the thing."
Miyamoto will tell you what his father told him, which is old and half-liturgy and possibly true — and knowing the name of what the boundary held back means you will be looking for it now, in the water and everywhere else, the way you cannot unsee a face once it has been pointed out. Some wards are kept strong precisely by the keeper never quite knowing.
"Who renewed the wards this Obon? You said your line does it — did you do it yourself this year?"
The answer is the whole mission and the old man knows it, which is why his hands are shaking; press him and he will have to say aloud whatever help he accepted this year, from whatever reasonable stranger offered it. A favour done to a hundred-year ward is a favour you cannot easily take back.
Say nothing yet; go to the basin and read the water before he frames it for you.
Reading the basin cold means seeing only what the boundary itself shows, uncoloured by an old man's fear or his father's stories — the truest read you can get. It also means putting your own face over water that has stopped giving faces back, with no idea yet what is on the other side of the skin, waiting to be looked at in return.
The basin under its little roof, the lane's paper lanterns burning unreflected three steps away, the water holding nothing

Up close the water is worse than Miyamoto described, because he described an absence and an absence is at least a clean thing, and this is not clean. The basin is full to the lip and dead still, no wind reaching it under the roof, and when you lean the lanterns behind you do not appear and neither do you, but the surface is not blank — it is busy, in the way a held breath is busy, a faint slow churn under the flatness like something turning over far down in water that is only a hand deep. You put two fingers to the stone rim, not the water, and let the read come, and the read comes wrong. A shrine basin should taste of a hundred Obons of cupped hands and rinsed mouths and small honest fears asked into the dark; this one tastes of all that and then, underneath, of a single clean cut — a place where something woven was unwoven, neatly, by someone who knew exactly which thread to lift.

You crouch and look longer than is wise, and the longer you look the more certain both registers become that the water is not failing to reflect you — it is choosing not to, the way a person who has been told something about you declines to meet your eye. Somewhere in the basin's hundred years a boundary was strung between the lane and whatever the lane was built to forget, and the boundary was kept taut by reflection, by the simple daily proof that the world on this side was solid enough to throw an image back. Pull the proof and the boundary goes slack. The lanterns burn three steps behind you, gold and doubled along the lane, and stop dead at the rim of this stone as though the basin has been excused from the evening, lifted quietly out of the week, set apart so that something can use it without the neighbourhood noticing the seam.

When you take your fingers off the rim the churn under the surface settles, slowly, the way water settles after a fish has turned — and for one held instant before it goes flat again the black skin shows you a face after all, not yours, a calm and pleasant and entirely reasonable face that you have been learning the shape of all week without yet being allowed to name it. Then the water lies still and gives nothing, and behind you Miyamoto asks, in the small voice of a man who already knows, whether you saw anything. The honest answer is that you saw the Healer's bedside manner wearing a hundred-year-old shrine like a clean white coat. The lane keeps burning. The basin keeps its peace, which is the wrong kind of peace, the kind that has been arranged.

The choice it leaves you
Read deeper — push past the flatness and follow the single clean cut to the hand that made it.
Following the cut means reading toward the one who unwove the ward, and you already half-know whose calm face the water just showed you — to read further is to let it read back along the same thread, to announce to the thing on the other side that someone on this side has finally looked. Your sight thins what is already thinning.
Pull back and tell Miyamoto the truth: the ward wasn't worn down, it was opened, this year, by a hand that knew the work.
The old man deserves the truth and will break a little under it, because it means the help he accepted was the wound, and he will want to know who — and naming Yushiro to a frightened keeper sends the name out into the neighbourhood ahead of you, where it cannot be called back. Some truths are doors you cannot reach back through.
Empty the basin — tip the dead water out onto the stones and refill it from the lane's tap, and see whether fresh water reflects.
Emptying the basin is the obvious test and possibly a mercy, restoring the daily proof that keeps the boundary taut — or it is exactly the disturbance the cut was made to invite, the keeper's own hand breaking the last still surface and letting the slack boundary swing. The thing about a trap built from a kindness is that undoing the kindness springs it.
Inside the shrine office, a one-mat room behind the gate, Miyamoto's ledger of renewals open on the low table under a bare bulb

The ledger goes back further than the bulb, further than the table, a stack of bound books in a cloth-wrapped pile each one a generation's worth of Obons recorded in the cramped hand of whoever held the broom that century. Miyamoto turns to this year's page with the reluctance of a man opening a wound to show the doctor where it hurts, and the entry is there in an unfamiliar hand — not his, not his line's, a clean modern script that has written, in the place where the keeper records who renewed the wards, the single word that means *a friend assisted*. He came in the spring, Miyamoto says, looking at the foreign handwriting in his family's book. A soft-spoken man, a healer he said, who had heard the old keeper's hands were failing and offered, for nothing, for the simple rightness of it, to help renew the work his father could no longer manage. He was so kind, Miyamoto says, and his voice does the thing voices do when kindness turns out to have been the knife. He was so very kind, and he asked only that I let him tie the last knot himself, because the last knot, he said, is the one that matters.

You read the foreign entry and both registers go cold and quiet, because you have seen this hand before — or its work, the same neat economy, the same gift offered into exactly the gap a person is most ashamed of — in Gin's ledger of favours, in every place this week where Yushiro the Healer has reached in through someone's worst small fear and adjusted one thread while they wept with gratitude. The wards were not breached. They were *re-tied*, expertly, by a guest invited in through the front gate by the keeper's own grief at his failing hands, and the last knot — the one that holds the reflection, the one that keeps the boundary listening — was tied to give way on a schedule. This week. Obon, when the membrane over all of Kita is thinnest and every shrine in the district is straining to hold its own small portion of the wall. Pull one keeper's stone loose now and you do not get a flood. You get a draught, a single clean current through one forgotten shed of a shrine, feeding the larger thinning Yushiro has been working toward all along.

Miyamoto wants to know what you will do, and under that, what he did, whether his failing hands made this or whether it would have come anyway — and there is no answer you can give that returns the spring to him, the morning he was so grateful to be helped. You can re-tie the last knot yourself and close the draught, which costs the neighbourhood nothing and costs you the one open window you currently have onto what Yushiro is building, a window the Healer does not yet know you have found. Or you can leave it open and watched, let the shrine keep failing on its arranged schedule, and learn from this small clean current exactly where the larger thinning runs and what it is for — at the price of an old man's basin, and the boundary his line kept for a hundred years, and whatever uses a draught while you stand at it taking notes.

The choice it leaves you
Re-tie the last knot now — close the draught, restore the reflection, and give the old man his shrine back.
Closing it is the clean kindness and the right one by Miyamoto, and it shuts a wound in the membrane before whatever wants through gets through. It also blinds you: the one place you can currently watch Yushiro's plan operate goes dark, and the Healer learns nothing was missed — which means the next stone he loosens, you will not be standing over when it gives.
Leave it open but ward the keeper — seal Miyamoto out of the precinct, let the draught run watched, and trace where the current pulls.
A watched draught is intelligence you cannot get any other way, a live thread back into the thinning while the Healer still believes it unobserved — but you are choosing to let a hundred-year boundary stay open, with an old man exiled from the shrine his family kept, and you do not fully know what a single clean current into Kita this week is meant to feed, or whether watching it counts, to the thing on the other side, as an introduction.
Re-tie the knot but tie it wrong on purpose — close it to all eyes but yours, a knot only you can read, so the draught looks shut and isn't.
The clever middle path: the shrine reflects again, Miyamoto sleeps, the Healer sees his work apparently undone by ordinary decay — and you keep a private window the size of a keyhole. But a knot tied by your two suppressed registers is a knot with your name woven into it, hung in the one place Yushiro is certain to come back and check, and a healer reads handwriting better than anyone alive.
The lane at dusk, lanterns lit, the basin's verdict settling — and Miyamoto at the gate waiting to learn what his hundred years come to

Whatever you chose, the evening insists on happening: the neighbourhood comes out as it always does on the third night, old women in cotton yukata and children with their fox-masks pushed up on their foreheads, and they file past the little shrine and clap their twice and bow and do not feel the seam you have spent the day standing inside. That is the mercy and the horror of a forgotten shrine — its failing is invisible to exactly the people it fails, and the boundary your day's work either closed or kept open holds back a thing none of them will ever be asked to believe in. The lanterns are doubled along the lane in their gold strings, and now, depending on the knot, the basin either catches them and holds them trembling on its skin the way it has for a hundred summers, or it lies flat and black and apart, excused from the evening, holding its arranged peace.

Miyamoto finds you at the gate and reads your face before you speak, the way old keepers read water, and what passes between you is not gratitude exactly — you have not given him back the spring, the morning he was glad to be helped, and both of you know the kind stranger will come again to some other failing pair of hands with the same soft offer and the same patient knot. But he takes your hand in both of his, dry and light as paper, and tells you that his father used to say the shrine does not protect the neighbourhood from the thing on the other side; it protects the thing on the other side from the neighbourhood's forgetting, keeps it small and named and bounded, because a forgotten thing grows and a watched thing waits. You have either restored the watching or volunteered to be the watcher. The Healer, somewhere across the thinning city, has either lost one quiet draught or gained, without yet knowing it, one observer at the seam — and either way he is one stone further along a wall he means to bring down by Obon's end.

You go down the steps as the drums start somewhere toward the river, the slow Obon rhythm that calls the dead home for their three nights and sends them off again, and the sound finds the loose thing in you that has been pulled tight all day. A shrine that stopped answering has been made to answer again, or made to answer only to you, or let alone to teach you the shape of the larger silence — and any of those is a hand laid on the same wall your brother's keeper Gin once helped tend, and that Gin, worn thin and wearing a healer's calm, is now helping to thin. The lane burns gold behind you. Down at the embankment, the first paper boats go onto the black water, holding their small lights out for the dead, and the dead this week are very close, and getting closer, by exactly as many stones as the Healer can talk a grieving keeper into loosening.

The choice it leaves you
Take the foreign ledger-page to Tanizaki — the Healer's handwriting is evidence, and the old fixer can read a hand across a city.
Carrying the page to Tanizaki turns one shrine's wound into a thread you can pull on the spine itself — proof, in Yushiro's own script, of how the thinning is being worked. But it also means the shrine's quiet trouble becomes part of the loud thing you are chasing, and a healer who learns his handwriting is being collected starts writing in other hands.
Tell no one; fold the shrine into the small private map of loosened stones you are building, and keep watching for the next one.
Keeping it private keeps it yours and keeps the Healer unalarmed, and lets you build, stone by stone, the actual shape of the wall he means to drop — the most valuable thing you could carry into the Vacant Seat. But a map you tell no one about is a weight you carry alone toward a two-demon trap, and the last keeper who tried to hold the whole wall in his own two failing hands is sweeping clean stones at a basin that only just started reflecting again.
Kaneda's Last Bottle written
Given by Kaneda Tōru (the honest table)

A bar owner's final bottle of his late wife's plum wine keeps refilling itself a finger overnight. Grief, an anchored spirit, and a man who would rather be haunted than healed.

The honest table, an hour before open

Kaneda's place is six stools and a counter the colour of long use, down a Jūjō side-street where the vending machines hum the only light. He lets you in before open because Gin's name still works like a key in this part of the city, and he sets a cup of barley tea in front of you without asking, the way a man does when he has decided to talk and needs his hands busy first. You read the room before he says anything — you cannot help it, the way you cannot help hearing your own pulse — and the room reads back grief, but the clean kind, the kind that has been folded and put away and taken out only on the right nights. It is not the room that is wrong. It is the bottle behind him, on the high shelf, where the light does not reach and something nonetheless keeps a small, patient account.

He pours you nothing, because the bottle is not for pouring. Sachiko made it, he says, and the name comes out worn smooth. Plum wine, the last batch she put up the summer before the stroke, eleven jars that became ten that became one, drunk down over six years of anniversaries to the width of two fingers in the bottom of the glass. Two fingers, he says again, and looks at it, and you watch him not reach for it. I measured it. I marked the glass. I am not a superstitious man, Uedera-kun. I marked the glass with a grease pencil and I went to bed and in the morning there were three fingers, and I have not slept right since.

The choice it leaves you
Read the bottle directly — throw your sight at it.
The fast knowledge. Objects hold contact-history; this one held her hands for a whole summer. Reading it will tell you what is anchored here — and announce, to anything listening on the other side, that a strong unmanaged aperture just opened in the room.
Ask him what he wants to happen.
The slow, decent question, and the one that complicates everything. A man who marks a glass and keeps it is not always a man who wants the measuring to stop.
What the glass remembers

Whichever way you come to it, the bottle resolves under your attention like a held breath. There is no demon here and no suffering-being weight, none of the deposited dread that makes a Nerima stairwell unbearable; there is a person, thin and intact and gentle, anchored not to the bar and not to Kaneda but to the wine itself — to the one object in the world that still holds the work of her hands. Register I, grief, the most common and the most misread: a spirit that does not haunt so much as wait, deepening the sorrow already in a place without ever intending harm, the way a low note under a song makes the whole song heavier. She is topping the bottle up. You feel it the moment you understand it, and the understanding is unbearably tender: she is not draining anything, not feeding, not asking. She is filling the glass back toward full because for forty years that is what she did when his cup got low, and death changed the medium and not the habit.

It is the kindest haunting you have ever read and it is killing him by inches, which is the part the official framework gets wrong about grief-register spirits and the part you understand in your own chest before you can put it in words. Every morning he wakes to proof that she is still tending him. Every morning he cannot drink the proof, because to drink it would be to lower the level she keeps raising, and so the one bottle that should have run out and let him grieve cleanly will now, gently, lovingly, never empty, and he will stand behind that counter being cared for by his dead wife until it hollows him out completely. The cruelty is that nothing here is cruel.

The choice it leaves you
Tell Kaneda exactly what you read — all of it.
The truth he is owed and may not survive whole. To name her is to make her real to him in a way the grease-pencil mark let him keep at arm's length.
Tell him only that it is harmless, and buy yourself time to think.
A mercy and a small lie of omission. You would be deciding, for a grown man, how much of his own haunting he gets to know — the exact thing the bureaus do, that you have always hated them for.
Say nothing yet; find out if she can be spoken to.
Spirits with intact selves can sometimes be reached. But reaching her means opening further, here, in a thin district during Obon — and a grief-anchor that learns it has been noticed does not always stay gentle.
The thing he will not say he wants

You get it out of him eventually, the way you get the true thing out of anyone, by being quiet longer than is comfortable. He does not want her gone. Of course he does not. He has spent six years drinking a bottle down so that the drinking would be a thing he did with her, one anniversary at a time, and the prospect of an empty bottle was never relief — it was the second funeral, the real one, the one where the last work of her hands finally passed through him and out of the world. The refilling terrifies him and he would not trade it for anything. If you make it stop, he says, not as a threat, just as a man laying his cards face up on his own honest table, then I have to bury her again, and I am not — I don't think I am — and he does not finish it, and the unfinished part is the whole job.

This is the place where your two registers pull opposite ways and you feel both of them, the searching one and the suppressed one, the part of you that wants to fix and the part of you that knows fixing is sometimes just tidying away another person's love because it makes you uncomfortable to watch it cost them. Gin would have known what to do here. Gin always knew the difference between a haunting that harms and a haunting that merely hurts, and you are starting to understand that the difference is not in the spirit at all. It is in whether the living one has finished needing it. Kaneda has not finished. The bottle is the only place his hands and hers still meet.

The choice it leaves you
Help her finish her own habit — gently, with him in the room.
Not an exorcism. A leave-taking. You would be guiding a gentle spirit to set down a task she no longer needs to do, and asking a man to let his wife stop tending him — which may free them both or may be the cruelty he fears.
Leave the anchor exactly as it is and teach Kaneda to live beside it.
Cohabitation is a real, documented outcome. But a grief-anchor in a thinning district during Obon is not a fixed thing; what is gentle this week can deepen, and you would be betting his slow hollowing against the membrane holding.
Move the anchor — decant the wine into vessels he can actually drink.
A trick more than a rite: split the contact-history across many small cups so the level he must lower is shared, not singular. Clever, and it may simply teach her to fill them all.
Obon, and the door that is open anyway

You do not get to choose the night. It is the thirteenth of August and the lanterns are out along the river and every shrine in Kita has its gate propped for the dead who are, this week and only this week, expected. Whatever you decide to do with Sachiko's bottle, you do it inside that — inside a city that has, for three days, agreed to leave the door open, which is exactly the condition under which a gentle anchor can become something with more reach than it ever wanted. You light the small fire Kaneda's mother would have lit, in the alley, in a chipped dish, because the form matters even to people who say it does not, and Kaneda stands in his own doorway with his hands at his sides like a man at a graveside, which is what this is.

If you choose to help her finish, it does not look like the bureau footage. There is no cold blast, no figure, no voice. There is a bottle that, while you hold the register steady and Kaneda finally says the thing out loud — you can stop now, I've got it, I've got the bar, go on — settles, at last, to two fingers, and does not rise in the morning. If you choose to leave her, the bar simply stays what it has been, a haunted, gentle, slowly emptying man behind a counter, and you carry the knowledge that you let a kindness keep costing him because it was his to spend. Either way you are the one who has to watch his face do the thing faces do when a long argument with the dead is finally, quietly, conceded.

The choice it leaves you
Stay until morning, and check the glass with him.
Be present for the result you caused, whatever it is. Witnessing the cost is the least the work asks of you, and the most it can promise.
Leave him to the morning alone.
Some leave-takings are not yours to attend. Walking out means trusting a grieving man with his own grief — and never knowing, for certain, which way the level went.
What the honest table pays back

Kaneda is a contact, not a holder and not a node, and the thing he gives you in return is not power or a heading — it is a table you can come back to, which on the roads ahead turns out to be worth more than either. A man who has watched you handle his dead wife with your hands open and your verdict unhidden will, after this, pour you the barley tea and tell you the true thing instead of the safe one, and in a city run on managed information that is a rarer asset than a Tower favour. If you helped Sachiko set down her task, he tells you, the next time you are in, that he slept the whole night through for the first time in six years and hated you for an hour at dawn and then did not. If you left the anchor, he tells you nothing, and pours the tea anyway, and the not-telling is its own kind of trust.

You also carry out a smaller, sharper thing, filed wherever you file the lessons this morning keeps handing you: that the Directorate's neat taxonomy of harm has no column for a love that injures by persisting, and that the gentlest entities in the catalogue are the ones most likely to teach you what you actually believe about letting go. Gin knew this. It is, you are increasingly sure, the exact knowledge the thing wearing him is using against him right now — a duty that injures by persisting, a tending that will not stop because stopping would mean the task is finally over. You leave Kaneda's with the taste of barley tea and the cold understanding that you have just rehearsed, on a bottle of plum wine, the argument you are walking north to have over your brother.

The choice it leaves you
Carry the lesson north, toward Gin.
Let the small mercy sharpen the large one. What you learned about a love that injures by persisting is the shape of the thing in Gin — and you go knowing it now, which is worse and better.
Mark the table as a place to return.
Bank the trust. An honest counter in Jūjō is a fixed point on roads designed to keep you moving — somewhere the verdict is true and the tea is hot, for after.
The Bookseller's Locked Shelf written
Given by Sōma the bookseller, Jūjō

A shelf behind the counter that no key opens and no fire has ever touched. The books on it have contact-history, and one of them remembers Gin.

A shop that smells of foxed paper and held breath

Sōma's secondhand shop is the kind of narrow that makes you turn sideways between the stacks, three storeys of other people's libraries pressed into a frontage barely wider than its door. The old man has run it since before Gin was born and he does not look up when you come in; he finishes the page he is on, the way booksellers do, asserting that the book outranks the customer. When he does look up he goes very still, and you understand that he has placed you by the face — Gin's jaw, Gin's way of standing in a doorway as if apologising for the space he takes — and that the placing has cost him something. You're the brother, he says, and it is not a question, and behind him, on the high shelf with the small brass lock, something shifts its weight the way a sleeper does when a familiar voice enters the room.

You feel the shelf before you properly see it. It is not loud. It is the opposite of loud — a pocket of held quiet in a shop already quiet, a dozen volumes that have stopped radiating the soft contact-history of handled books and started radiating something denser, more deliberate, the way a word repeated too many times stops being a word. Sōma watches you feel it. Sixty years I've had this shop, he says, and forty of them that shelf has been locked, and I did not lock it. The key turned itself the week your brother stopped coming in.

The choice it leaves you
Ask why Gin came in — and what he came in for.
The human thread first. Gin had a reason to frequent a Jūjō secondhand shop, and the reason is on that shelf, and Sōma has been waiting four years for someone with the right face to ask.
Read the shelf before you let the old man frame it for you.
Trust your own sight over his telling. Objects with deep contact-history can hold more than memory; reading a locked shelf in a thin district means deciding to know what is on it before you know whether you can put it down.
What objects keep when no one is looking

Sōma makes tea on a hotplate that should have been condemned a decade ago and tells you the part the bureaus would file as folklore and you now know to be field-accurate. Objects are not alive. They do not think. What they have, the old man says — and he says it the way a man says a thing he worked out alone over years and never had anyone to tell — is contact-history: decades of being present when things happened, of being held during specific moments, of sitting in the same room as entities that left a residue the way a fire leaves smoke in cloth. Most books carry a little. A book that was held by the same grieving hands every night for thirty years carries a lot. And a book that was in the room, open, during a crossing — during the exact moment a self came apart at the membrane — carries something that is not memory at all, but a kind of standing wound, a place where the page remembers being a threshold.

That, he tells you, is what the shelf is. Not cursed, which is a word for people who need the comfort of a villain. Accumulated. He bought a dead exorcist's library forty years ago without knowing what some of the volumes had sat through, and the worst of them found their own level on the high shelf the way water finds the low place, and there they have stayed, quietly thickening, a small private membrane of their own behind the counter of a shop in Jūjō. And four years ago your brother came in, white to the lips, and asked to be left alone with the shelf, and the lock — Sōma swears this on his dead wife — turned itself when Gin put his hand flat against the wood.

The choice it leaves you
Ask which book Gin held.
Narrow it from a shelf to a single spine. The one Gin reached for is the one that remembers him — and a book that remembers a man can sometimes be made to give him back, in pieces, which is exactly as dangerous as it sounds.
Ask what the dead exorcist was, and how he died.
Provenance. A library is its owner's afterlife; knowing what kind of practitioner assembled these volumes tells you what kind of residue you are about to disturb during the one week the door stands open.
The spine that remembers your brother

The lock does for you what it did for Gin: it turns under your hand, the small brass tongue sliding back with a click you feel in your teeth, and the shelf exhales four years of held quiet into the shop. You do not reach blindly. You let your sight walk the spines until one of them looks back — a slim casebound notebook, no title, the boards swollen with damp that was never water — and the moment your attention lands on it you are not in the shop. You are in a low place where the water remembers, and it is night, and a man with your brother's careful hands is writing by lamp-light an argument with himself about whether a debt that was never his to pay might nonetheless be the only honest thing left to do. The book held the room when Gin first found the low place. It kept the threshold. And it has been keeping it, faithfully, on the high shelf, for four years, the way Sachiko's bottle kept filling — an object continuing a function past every reason except the function itself.

This is the find and the danger in one motion. The notebook is a fixed copy of a night you have been trying to reconstruct from the outside — Gin's Saturday, the address in no file, the seam where the entries stop being entries and start being arguments — and to read it is to stand where he stood, in his own dread, at the exact hour the thing wearing him made its reasonable case. It will tell you more than any node ever could. It will also open you, here, in a shop pressed against a private membrane, during the one week the dead are expected, while your unmanaged aperture floods the room with the omnidirectional noise that buried the warning the first time. The book remembers being a threshold. Held by the wrong hands on the wrong night, a threshold is a door, and a door is a thing that opens both ways.

The choice it leaves you
Read it here, now, with the shelf open and the city's door propped.
The most Gin you will ever get back, at the highest price. You would be standing in the recorded dread of the night he was turned, in a thinning room, broadcasting — and learning, perhaps, the one thing the nodes could not give you. It will cost your Stability and may cost more.
Take it out of the shop sealed, to read somewhere warded.
Patience over hunger. Tanizaki's wards, or the clinic's blue curtain, could let you open the book without opening the room. Slower, safer, and it means carrying a kept threshold through the Obon streets in your own bag.
Lock the shelf again and leave the notebook where Gin left it.
The hardest restraint. Gin put his hand flat on this wood and walked away from what it held; there may be a reason he chose to leave the record of his turning sealed, and honouring that means giving up the clearest window you have found.
What it costs to read a kept threshold

Say you read it — most do; you came across a city for exactly this. The notebook does not narrate. It deposits. You get the low place in fragments that arrive in the wrong order, the way Itsuki's sight came back scrambled: the smell of river-mud at slack tide, the particular cold of a place where a great many crossings have happened unmourned, a voice that is not a voice making its case in the register of pure reasonableness — they are owed, and no one will pay them, and you are the only one who can, and is that not the definition of a duty? — and your brother's hand on the page getting tighter and truer with every line until the line between what Gin wanted and what it wanted stops being a line and becomes a rhyme. You learn the address. You learn the shape of the argument. You learn, worst of all, that he was not deceived. He was persuaded, by something that only ever asked him to do what was right, and the persuasion is the most frightening thing you have ever stood inside because you can feel, reading it, how nearly it would work on you.

And the room answers, because you opened the book in it. Sōma's shop goes the particular degree colder that means a residue has noticed a strong unmanaged aperture and oriented toward it; the other spines on the high shelf lean their quiet weight your way; somewhere under the floorboards the building does the listening thing buildings do in this city this week. You do not get out of this clean. Either you spend Stability you cannot spare holding the room shut while you finish reading, or you stop short of the end to keep the membrane from lurching, and never learn the last line your brother wrote before the entries became arguments. Sōma stands by the hotplate with the cold tea going colder and does not interfere, because he is sixty years a bookseller and he has always understood that some books are only honestly read at a price.

The choice it leaves you
Finish it — pay the Stability, take the whole night.
The complete record of Gin's turning, and a dent in you that does not buff out. You walk away knowing the address and the argument entire — and a little nearer, yourself, to the omnidirectional noise that is part of the campaign's hidden clock.
Stop at the lurch — protect the room, lose the last page.
Choose the district over the data. You keep the membrane from tipping and the shelf from waking the rest of itself, and you carry out an unfinished knowledge that will gnaw at every later road.
The lock, and who keeps it after you

However far you read, the shelf is your problem now in a way it was Sōma's only by accident, and the old man knows it and is, in his dry way, relieved. He has carried a private membrane behind his counter for forty years out of nothing but the bookseller's refusal to burn a book, and he is too old to carry it much longer, and you are the first person to walk in with the right face and the wrong, useful gift. You can re-lock the shelf and leave him the keeping, which means a thinning district has one more quiet wound in it that no bureau will ever survey. You can take the worst of the volumes to Tanizaki, who knows what to do with kept thresholds and will not thank you for the work. Or you can do the thing Gin did not do, and end the notebook — not read it, end it, let the recorded night finally cross the way the man in it never got to — which closes your clearest window forever and may be the only mercy on the shelf.

Whatever you choose, Sōma gives you the one thing a bookseller has to give, which is provenance: the name of the dead exorcist whose library this was, and the fact — offered last, the way the true thing always is — that the man had a student, decades back, who came to nothing the bureaus recorded and may not have come to nothing at all. A thread for later. You leave the shop turning sideways between the stacks the way you came in, the brass lock warm now where a dozen hands have never warmed it, and the street outside doing its Obon business of small fires and propped gates, and you understand that you have added a place to the map your brother drew — a shelf in Jūjō where, if the worst ever comes, a door stands already half-open behind a sixty-year-old counter.

The choice it leaves you
Re-lock it and leave Sōma the keeping.
Preserve the window and the wound together. You may need the notebook again; the cost is a thinning district carrying one more sealed threshold and an old man carrying it for you.
Take the worst volumes to Tanizaki to be properly warded.
Hand the danger to the one man in Kita who can hold it. It puts you further in Tanizaki's debt and the Grid's orbit — and admits you cannot keep what you found.
End the notebook — let Gin's recorded night finally cross.
The mercy that costs you everything the book could still tell. You close your clearest window forever, on purpose, the way Gin closed the lock — and you will never be sure whether you freed something or buried the last of him you could reach.
The Grid Courier Gone Dark written
Given by 'Switch' (Grid quartermaster)

A courier who carries between safehouses has missed three drops. The Grid does not call the police; it calls you. Finding her means walking the routes the records cannot see.

A back room that is on no floor plan

Switch runs the Grid's quartermaster post out of a karaoke building in Ōkubo that the registry insists has four floors when it has five, and you reach the fifth by an elevator button that only exists if you already know to press the place where it would be. The room is all charging cables and burner phones laid out in rows like a surgeon's instruments, and Switch herself does not turn from the wall of cheap monitors when you come in, because turning would mean admitting she has run out of people to send. Mika's missed three drops, she says, and the flatness of it is the tell — Grid people do not panic out loud, they go level, they file the fear somewhere it won't slow their hands. Three. A courier misses one, she's sick. Misses two, she's burned. Misses three and goes dark on the check-in app, she's either dead or she's been turned, and I need to know which before I move forty people who trusted her with their addresses.

She slides a phone across the table and it is Mika's backup, recovered from a dead-drop magnet under a vending machine in Higashi-Nakano, and the screen still glows with the last route she walked: a string of safehouses linked by streets that are real and one street that is not. You have walked thin places before but you have never seen one queued into a delivery app like a stop on a milk run, a pin dropped on a lane that the map software renders as a grey nothing between two buildings that, on the satellite layer, share a wall. She started taking the short way, Switch says, and you hear what she is not saying, which is that the short way is short because it cuts through somewhere the city does not keep. The routes the records can't see. I told her never. The Grid stays in the parts that are real, that's the whole point of us.

Obon is three days in and the streets outside are loud with returning dead in the harmless, festival sense — paper lanterns, families, the smell of grilled corn — and you understand that this is exactly the week a thin lane would be at its hungriest, the membrane worn soft by the season's traffic of grief. Switch finally turns, and her face is younger than her voice, and she says the thing that makes it your problem: You read places. You read your brother into half the trouble in this city. Find my courier, and I'll owe you a debt the Grid honours, which is the only kind worth having. But you go in knowing — if she took the short way and the short way kept her, you might not be bringing back the same Mika who left.

The choice it leaves you
Take the recovered phone and walk Mika's last route forward, drop by drop.
The methodical way. You retrace the human path while it's still warm — but every step toward the grey lane is a step she already took and didn't come back from, and the route was built to be walked in one direction only.
