Ask Switch what Mika was carrying on the run she vanished on.
Cargo first. A courier's worth is what's in the bag, and a turned courier's danger is what she gave up. Switch will tell you — but knowing what's loose in the city makes you responsible for it before you've even found the body.
Refuse the grey lane outright and search only the real streets between the drops.
The safe discipline Switch herself preaches. You stay in the parts of Tokyo that keep records — but if Mika is in the place the records can't see, searching only the real streets means searching everywhere she isn't.
The seam between two buildings that share a wall

You find the mouth of the short way at dusk, in Higashi-Nakano, exactly where the app's grey nothing sits between a shuttered eel restaurant and a coin laundry that hums all night. To the registry it is a maintenance gap, eighty centimetres of dead air no one owns; to your sight it is a held breath, a lane that goes back further than the two buildings could possibly allow, lined with the soft afterimages of everyone who has used it as a shortcut and the harder, brighter residue of the one who used it too often. Mika's trace is all over the entrance like a smell that hasn't faded — confident at first, the loose stride of someone who has done a thing a hundred times and stopped being afraid of it, and then, deeper in, the stride changing, the steps closing up, the residue of a person who realised mid-walk that the lane was longer tonight than it had ever been.

You go in because the alternative is leaving her wherever it took her, and the lane closes around you the way water closes over a dropped coin, the festival noise behind you thinning to nothing in three paces and then to less than nothing, to the specific Tokyo silence that means a place has stopped being part of the city. The walls are still the eel restaurant and the laundry — you can read the tile, the rust, the laundry's vibration in your teeth — but they go on too far, repeating like a song with a skip in it, and you understand the lane's trick: it is not a door to somewhere else, it is the same eighty centimetres folded over and over, a corridor made of one place insisting on itself until walking it costs you the certainty that you will ever reach the end. Somewhere ahead, faint, a phone is playing the Grid check-in chime on a loop, the sound a courier's app makes when it cannot reach the network.

Mika is forty metres in and four years thin, sitting against the laundry-wall with her bag still clipped across her chest, and she is alive in the way a held note is alive — present, sustained, not going anywhere. Her eyes track you. Her mouth works. I kept walking, she says, and her voice has the lane's skip in it now, the same syllable trying to land twice. I kept walking and the end kept being further and I worked out — if I stop, it stops. If I stop counting steps it lets me sit. So I stopped. She has been sitting for, by Switch's count, eleven days. The check-in chime loops. The bag on her chest is still full, and that is the detail that turns your stomach, because a turned courier empties her bag and Mika never did — whatever took the short way from her, it was not her cargo, it was her forward motion, the simple human ability to arrive.

The choice it leaves you
Get her up and walk her out the way you came in — backward, against the fold.
The direct rescue. The lane is built to be walked one way; reversing it might unspool it — or it might cost you the same forward motion it took from her, and then there are two of you sitting against the wall.
Open the bag first — find out what she was carrying that made this lane worth the risk.
You came for the courier, but the cargo is right there. Knowing might explain everything; it also delays the only thing that matters, which is getting a thinning woman out of a place that eats arrival.
Sit down beside her and let her tell you where the end of the lane actually is.
Meet her where she is. Mika has had eleven days to learn the lane's rules — but to learn them she had to stop, and sitting down beside someone the lane has stilled is how the lane gets the next one to stop too.
What the lane takes instead of a toll

You get your hands under Mika's arms and the moment you lift, the lane notices, the repeating walls stuttering faster like a film catching in the gate, the check-in chime climbing a half-step into something that is no longer a notification and is almost a voice. She comes up light — too light, the lightness of someone the place has already been drawing down for eleven days — and the instant her feet take her weight the corridor's length changes again, the entrance behind you sliding away, the fold deepening, the lane responding to the one thing it cannot abide, which is a body intent on leaving. It does this, Mika breathes against your shoulder. It makes the way out longer the more you want it. That's why I stopped wanting.

And there is the trap laid bare, the cruelty of the short way's design: it is not a predator, it is a bargain, and the bargain is that it will let you sit forever in exchange for your wanting to leave, and the wanting is the only currency it accepts. You feel it start to take yours — the entrance is so far now, the walls so insistent, and a part of you that sounds reasonable and tired suggests that you could simply set her down, that the floor is right there, that arrival was always a kind of arrogance. You have read enough thin places to know that this voice is the lane wearing your own register, the searching part of you that has spent the whole campaign wanting to reach Gin turned into a tool against you, your own longing for an end used to keep you from one.

So you do the thing the lane cannot model: you stop trying to reach the entrance, and you start narrating the city instead — out loud, into Mika's ear, the eel restaurant on the left with the rust at knee height, the laundry that hums in B-flat, Ōkubo Street two turns up with the corn-sellers and the lanterns and Switch on a fifth floor that exists, the whole loud real grief-soaked Obon machinery of a Tokyo that keeps its records. You do not walk toward the exit. You walk toward the description, naming the real until the lane's single insisted-upon place can no longer hold against the sheer specificity of everywhere else, and the fold, starved of your wanting and drowned in your noticing, gives — not dramatically, just a loosening, the eighty centimetres remembering it is only eighty centimetres, the festival noise arriving all at once like a held breath let go.

The choice it leaves you
Carry her straight to Switch and let the Grid quartermaster decide what's owed.
The clean handoff. You did the job; the debt is real; the Grid honours it. But Mika walked the short way because someone taught her it existed, and handing her over closes the case before you've asked who.
Before the network reconnects, ask Mika who showed her the short way.
The thread you're afraid of. A courier doesn't invent a folded lane; someone with reasons gave it to her as a kindness. In this city, reasonable favours that thin people out have one author, and you already know his handwriting.
The favour that came dressed as a shortcut

Out on Ōkubo Street the lanterns are obscene with ordinary life and Mika sits on the curb breathing the corn-smell like it is the first food she has had in eleven days, which in a sense it is, and when you ask her she does not pretend not to understand. A doctor, she says. Not Grid. He never wanted anything. He patched a courier up after a bad drop, no charge, and then a few weeks later he mentioned — just mentioned — that there were faster ways between the houses, if you had the eyes for them, and he drew one on a napkin. The word doctor lands in you like a stone down a well, because you have spent this whole August learning the shape of a man who helps people for free and then, a few weeks later, mentions a faster way, and the faster way always costs the one thing the helped person was using to stay whole.

You ask to see the napkin and of course she still has it, folded in the bag she never emptied, and the hand that drew the lane is careful and kind and unmistakably the hand that has been signing reasonable favours across the city all summer — Yushiro's work, wearing your brother's borrowed patience, seeding the Grid's own couriers with shortcuts that thin them at exactly the rate a membrane-plan would want. This is not a coincidence you stumbled into; it is a tributary of the thing you have been chasing, the great quiet plan to wear Kita's wall soft, and a courier who walks folded lanes is a courier slowly converting her own forward motion into thinness, a battery charging a circuit she will never see. Mika watches your face change and understands that the kind doctor was never kind, and that understanding is its own small wound, the loss of a debt she thought she owed to mercy.

What you do with the napkin is the consequence the mission turns on. Burned, it protects Mika and the next courier and tells Yushiro nothing about what you know. Carried to Switch, it arms the Grid — and a Grid that knows its shortcuts are poisoned will close them, which saves bodies and also tips your hand to a demon who reads, very carefully, who has started refusing his favours. Kept for yourself, it is evidence in Gin's own borrowed handwriting, a thread you can pull later in a room where it will cost more — and every day you keep it, the lane stays open, and somewhere a courier you have not met is being shown a faster way on a fresh napkin by a doctor who never wants anything at all.

The choice it leaves you
Burn the napkin on the curb and tell Mika the short way is dead.
Mercy, and silence. You protect the couriers and give Yushiro nothing — but you also destroy the one piece of his handwriting you could have used, and the lanes he hasn't drawn yet stay undrawn only by luck.
Give it to Switch so the Grid can close every folded lane in the network.
The systemic save. Dozens of couriers stop walking poison — but a Grid that suddenly shuns shortcuts is a signal, and the Healer notices signals, and he will know someone with the right face is reading his favours backward.
Keep the napkin — evidence in Gin's hand, for a room where it will count.
The long game, and the cold one. You hold proof you can use against Yushiro when it matters most. The price is the open lane: while you wait for that room, the short way stays drawable, and another courier learns it the kind way.
The Weakening Holder written
Given by Watanabe Jun (Despair VI)

A domain holder near the thin Kita district is failing. Help him, learn what a seat costs from the inside, or stand in the room when a register lets go and feel the membrane lurch.

The library that dims by a half-step

You go back to the municipal library in Itabashi because the bookseller starred his name twice and because a starred name in a climbing margin is the only kind of warning you have learned to obey. Watanabe Jun is at the returns cart in the same cardigan two sizes too big, and from the periodicals you watch him work for the better part of an hour, and the wrongness around him is worse than the first time — not dramatic, never dramatic, just a deepening of the half-step dimming, the air near him gone the colour of a photograph left too long in a drawer. He shelves a stack of children's picture books and his hand shakes on the last one and he stands very still until it stops, the way a man stands still when standing is the only competence he has left, and you understand that you are not watching a holder hold. You are watching a holder lose his grip one finger at a time and decide, each time, not to mention it to anyone.

When the library empties toward closing you do the thing you came to do, which is let him see you seeing him. He does not startle. He sets the last book down and looks at you with the tired recognition of a man who has been waiting years for someone to finally notice, and the relief in his face is so naked it is almost unbearable, because relief on a face that gentle is a confession that he has been alone with this since before you could read. You can tell, he says — not a question — and the words come out dimmed too, soft at the edges, a voice that has been spending itself on something invisible for so long it has forgotten how to be loud. Most people just feel cold near me and move their chair. You're the first one in a long time who looked at where the cold was coming from.

He makes you tea in the staff room out of a kettle that takes too long, and he tells you, in the flat unhurried way of someone describing weather, that he is the sixth of the Thirteen, that he carries Despair the way a retaining wall carries a hillside, and that lately the hillside has been getting heavier. It used to be that I held it and it held still, he says. Now I hold it and it leans. North. Always north now. Toward the empty place. He wraps both hands around his cup for the warmth he can no longer quite generate himself, and he asks you the question that turns a starred name into a person you can ruin or save: I don't suppose you came here to help. People don't, usually. But I find I'd very much like it if you had.

The choice it leaves you
Tell him the truth — that the empty place is the vacant Thirteenth Seat, and that something is widening it on purpose.
He has carried the leaning for years without ever being told why it's worsening. The truth would end his loneliness — and it would also hand a man at the end of his strength the knowledge that his suffering is engineered, which is a weight a dying holder may not have the room left to set down.
Offer to help without explaining — just sit with the weight and learn its shape first.
Kinder, and slower. You spare him the architecture of his own ruin and buy time to understand what holding actually requires — but a man this tired can read evasion, and helping someone while keeping the reason from him is the exact thing the Healer does, in the exact tone.
Ask him to teach you how a seat is held, before you commit to anything.
The witness's instinct: understand the mechanism. If you may someday face the thirteenth chair yourself, the only teacher in the city is a man holding the nearest one — but to learn how a chair is held from a man it is killing is to study the shape of your own possible ending.
What Despair costs before breakfast

He lets you shadow a day, and the day is the horror, because there is no scene in it. Watanabe Jun wakes at five in a one-room apartment in Itabashi and the first thing he does, before the light, before tea, is what he calls the settling — he sits on the edge of the futon and he goes and finds the despair of his district, all of it, the man on the night shift who has stopped opening his mail, the widow three floors down who has begun giving her things away, the boy who studies the train timetables not to ride them, and he gathers it the way you would gather spilled rice off a floor, grain by grain, into himself, so that the people do not have to carry the killing weight of it alone. That's the seat, he says, watching your face. Not a throne. A drain. The discipline runs through the district and a holder is the place it runs to so it doesn't pool somewhere a person can drown in it.

You spend the morning learning that a holder's mercy is invisible by design — that the reason no one in his ward walks into the river in August is a slight man in a cardigan who got up before dawn and took the weight of their worst hour into a body that was not built to store it, and that he has done this every single morning for longer than he will tell you, and that no one knows, and that this is the point. If they knew, they'd thank me, he says, and then they'd feel they owed me, and the owing is its own despair, so I make sure they never find out. A seat held right is a seat no one can see. He says it without bitterness, which is the part that gets into you, because a man with no bitterness about a thankless lifelong sacrifice is a man who has spent his bitterness too, along with everything else.

And then, mid-sentence, the leaning takes him. You see it happen — the dimming around him deepens fast, north-ward, a pull you can feel in your own teeth like a held note dropping a half-step, and Watanabe's hands go to the edge of the table and grip until the knuckles whiten and his breath comes in the careful counted way of a man keeping a wall up by force of attention alone. It passes. He lets go of the table. That's new, he says, and tries to smile. This year. The empty chair pulling on the full ones. Some mornings the settling takes everything I have and there's nothing left over for, you know. Lunch. Standing up. Wanting to. He looks at the window that faces north, toward Kita, toward the wound, and for one second the gentleness slips and you see what is underneath it, which is a man being slowly poured out through a hole he did not make and cannot plug.

The choice it leaves you
Take some of the morning settling onto yourself — share the drain, even once.
You have two suppressed registers; you might be able to hold a little of the load and give him a morning of rest. But to take Despair into yourself even once is to learn how it feels to be a place a discipline runs to — and a body that has held it once is a body the seat remembers as a candidate.
Refuse to touch the weight and instead find out who else feels the northward pull.
The mapper's caution: you don't add yourself to a failing circuit, you trace the circuit. If all twelve are leaning north, the pattern is the evidence — but mapping the choir while Watanabe pours himself out means choosing the diagram over the dying man in front of you, this morning, with his hands on the table.
Tell him to stop the settling for one day and let the district feel its own weight.
Brutal arithmetic. One morning's rest might be the difference between a holder who lasts the month and one who doesn't — but a morning he doesn't drain is a morning his ward carries its own despair undiluted, and a holder who has never once let go does not know which of his people would not survive the experiment.
The room when a register lets go

It is not Watanabe who lets go first. Word reaches him through whatever channel the Thirteen use to feel each other — he goes still over the returns cart and says, very quietly, Oh, the way you say it when the phone rings at the wrong hour, and he tells you that the fourth, the night nurse who carries Fear, has gone into the place a holder goes when the weight finally exceeds the will, and that he is going to her, and that you may come if you can keep your feet. You go north with him, into the part of the city that reads thin, to a hospital staff dormitory in the hour before dawn, and you stand in a small room with a single bed and a woman in it who is not dying — that would be ordinary — but releasing, her seat coming open in real time, fifteen years of held Fear coming ungathered all at once.

You have stood in thin places. You have walked folded lanes and read your brother into the membrane and felt the far shore breathing through a torn seam. None of it prepared you for the sound a register makes when it lets go, which is not a sound — it is a lurch, the whole membrane over Kita giving a single sickening shudder like a ferry hitting the wake of a ship you cannot see, and for three full seconds every frightened thing in the northern wards that the night nurse had been holding off all those years arrives in its people at once. You feel it as a wave of pure groundless terror that has nothing to do with you and passes through you anyway, and you watch Watanabe brace against the doorframe and take what he can of it — not all, he hasn't the strength for all, but a portion, instinctively, the way a man throws an arm across the passenger seat in a crash — and you understand that this is what the Thirteen are: not rulers, catchers, twelve sets of arms thrown across a city in the moment it brakes too hard.

When it settles the night nurse is asleep, finally, ordinarily, a tired woman who held something terrible for a decade and a half and has set it down, and her seat is empty now, the fourth chair pulled out, and you can feel the table tilt further toward the gap because of it. Watanabe slides down the doorframe to the floor and sits there grey and emptied, and he says the thing you came here not wanting to hear: Two empty chairs now. Hers and the thirteenth. The leaning's going to get worse for the rest of us — for me — because every chair that opens pulls harder on the ones still full. He looks at you from the floor. I'm next, you understand. Nearest the wound, weakest of the lot. I've about a season in me. Less, if another goes. That's not me being sad. I stopped being sad years ago. That's just the arithmetic, and I thought you'd want it straight.

The choice it leaves you
Promise him you'll close the thirteenth seat so the leaning stops — whatever it takes.
It is the only thing that would actually save him, and it is the promise the entire campaign has been steering you toward making. But to swear you'll close the chair is to swear you'll put something in it — and the only two things keyed to fit are your brother's worn body and your own.
Make no promise — tell him the truth, that you don't yet know if the chair can be closed without spending someone.
Honesty over comfort. You refuse to hand a dying man a hope you can't guarantee — but a holder counting his last season may have needed the hope more than the honesty, and a witness who won't promise is a witness the dying remember as the one who stood there and did the math out loud.
Sit on the floor beside him and ask, plainly, how he wants his own release to go when it comes.
Meeting him where he is, all the way. You stop trying to save the seat and start honouring the man — but to plan a holder's release with him is to accept that he will fail, and to accept it is to let go, a little, of the part of you still fighting to keep every chair full.
The settling you take instead of him

You make a choice in the grey week that follows, and the choice is to give Watanabe Jun one morning. You go to Itabashi before five and you sit on the edge of his futon in the dark and you tell him to stay lying down, and when the hour comes you do the settling yourself — you go out into the district with the two registers you have spent your whole life suppressing and you find the despair of strangers, grain by grain, the man who has stopped opening his mail and the widow giving her things away and the boy with the timetables, and you gather it. It is the worst thing you have ever willingly done and you do not stop, because Watanabe is lying ten feet away breathing the slow even breath of a man resting for the first time in a year, and the resting is the whole point, and you came here to be a place the weight could run to so that he would not have to be, this once.

It comes into you and it does not feel like sadness, which is the lie the word despair tells. It feels like gravity — a steady downward true thing, the simple physical fact that down exists and everything tends toward it, and holding it means standing inside that fact and choosing, with your whole body, to remain upright in it on behalf of people who will never know there was ever a choice. You feel, for one morning, what Watanabe feels every morning, what the night nurse felt for fifteen years before her arms gave out, what the seat actually costs the person in it: not a wound that is done to you, but an agreement you renew at dawn, over and over, to be broken slightly for the sake of a ward that thinks it simply woke up able to face the day. By seven you are on your knees on a stranger's stairwell and you understand the registry from the inside, and you understand why the Healer's offer is mercy, and you understand exactly how a thing that is mercy can also be the trap.

When you come back Watanabe is up and has made the slow tea, and he takes one look at your face and knows what you did and what it told you, and for the first time since you met him his eyes are wet, not for himself. Now you know, he says. Now you know it isn't done to us. We say yes. Every morning, we say yes, and the saying is the seat, and that's why a thing that wants the chair can't just take it — it has to get a living register to say yes in a body keyed to fit. That's what it's doing to your brother, isn't it. Teaching a hand to say yes. He sets the tea in front of you and folds your hands around it for the warmth, the way he warms his own. You shouldn't have learned this. But God help me, I'm glad someone finally did. I've been the only one who knew what it felt like for a very long time.

The choice it leaves you
Tell Watanabe what you now know about Gin and the Healer keying a hand to the chair.
He has just handed you the mechanism of the entire plot — that the seat is taken by a yes, not a force. Telling him completes the picture for both of you. But arming a dying holder with the knowledge that your brother is being trained into the chair makes him an ally with a season to live and nothing left to lose, which is a dangerous kind of friend to make.
Keep what you learned about the 'yes' to yourself — it is the key to the whole trap, and keys are safest unspoken.
The cold discipline. You now know the chair can only be filled by consent in a keyed body — the single fact the Healer's whole engine depends on. Holding it close protects it. But holding it close also means leaving Watanabe, who taught it to you, to die without knowing his lesson was the most important thing said to you all summer.
A cardigan two sizes too big

However the mission ends, it ends with Watanabe Jun still in his cardigan at the returns cart, holding, because that is what the seat is and he has not been given a way out that does not cost a brother or a city. You visit when you can through the back half of August and each visit he is dimmer, the half-step deepening toward a full one, the leaning north a little worse every time another chair so much as creaks, and each visit he greets you with the same naked relief and the same slow tea and the same refusal to complain, and you carry away from each one the thing he gave you on the morning you took his settling: the knowledge that the wall is held by consent, that a register says yes, that the Healer's whole patient engine is a campaign to manufacture one yes in one borrowed hand.

What you do with that knowledge is the consequence the mission turns on, and it follows you out of the library and into every room left in the campaign. You can carry Watanabe's lesson to the river as a weapon — the chair takes a yes, so a yes withheld is a chair unfilled, and a brother taught not to consent is a plot starved at its root. You can carry it as a temptation — because you, too, are keyed now, you took the settling and the seat remembers, and the cleanest way to save the twelve and spare Gin is to be the one who says yes yourself, in a body that fits, and Watanabe taught you exactly how. Or you can carry it as grief and nothing else — visit the gentle dimming man until the season he counted runs out, sit with him the morning his own arms give, feel the membrane lurch one more time for the sixth chair, and let the lesson be only what it was: the truest thing anyone told you about what the wall costs the people who are it.

He asks you, the last good visit, not to be there when it happens. You've felt one go, he says. You don't need to feel mine. Go do the thing only you can do — you're the one with two of yourself and a hand the chair wants, you're the only piece on this board that isn't already spent. He shelves the last picture book of the day and his hand does not shake this time, by an act of will you can see costing him, a gentleness performed for your benefit so that the image you keep is steady. Close the chair if you can do it without becoming me. And if you can't — he stops, and starts again, dimmer. If you can't, then become me knowing what it is, with your eyes open, the way I never got to choose it. That's the only mercy I've got to give you, and it's a poor one, and I'm sorry it's the truth.

The choice it leaves you
Promise to close the chair without becoming him — and carry the 'yes' to the river as a weapon.
You leave Watanabe his last seasons and take his lesson into the endgame: starve the plot by ensuring no yes is ever given. It is the hopeful door — and it forecloses the one where you spare your brother by sitting in the chair yourself, because a witness who has sworn never to consent cannot then consent.
Promise nothing, and stay — be in the room when the sixth register lets go.
You refuse to leave a man to fail alone, and you choose to feel the membrane lurch one more time at close range. It is the door of grief and witness — and it costs you the days you might have spent at the river, and it teaches your body, again, the exact shape of a seat coming open.
Take his last mercy: tell him that if the chair must be filled, you'll do it with your eyes open.
You let him give you the only gift he has — permission to become what he is, knowingly. It eases his last weeks immeasurably. But to accept that permission is to half-agree to the chair already, and the Healer has spent all August teaching a hand that a half-agreement is just a yes that hasn't finished arriving.
The Rage That Won't Cool written
Given by Mori Takao (Rage II)

The Rage seat is hard to find on purpose — a holder you can locate is a holder someone can provoke. A man who has spent forty years keeping a furnace banked needs one night's relief, and you have to weigh what giving it will crack.

The man you are not supposed to be able to find

The bookseller would not write this name down. He told you it aloud, once, in the back room with the door shut and the radio turned up, and then he made you repeat it back to him with your hand over the margin so the ink could not hear it: Mori Takao, the second of the Thirteen, who carries Rage, and who has spent the better part of forty years making himself unfindable on purpose. The others hide because hiding is restful, the bookseller said. He hides because a Rage holder you can find is a Rage holder someone can stand in front of and insult until the wall cracks. Despair you have to coax out. Rage you only have to provoke. So the second seat learned to be nobody, and he is very, very good at it. It takes you nine days and three wrong men to find the right one, and you find him at last in the boiler room of a public bath in Kita-ku — the wrong ward, the thin ward, a man tending a furnace forty metres from the wound in the world, which you do not think is an accident either.

He is sixty-something and built like a retaining post, and the first thing you notice is how still he holds his hands, the way a man holds his hands when he has learned that his hands are the part of him most likely to do something he cannot take back. He is shovelling coke into the bathhouse boiler with an economy of motion that is almost beautiful, no wasted gesture, no slammed door, every movement banked down to the minimum, and the heat off the firebox is enormous and he stands in it without flinching, a man entirely at home inside something that would burn anyone else. He does not turn around when you come down the iron stairs. You walked careful, he says, to the fire. Most people come down those stairs like the building owes them money. You came down like you didn't want to wake anything. So either you're afraid of me, which is sensible, or somebody taught you what I am. Which is dangerous. For you. Sit where I can see your hands.

You sit, and you let him look at your hands, and he reads them the way the others read the air around you — you watch him register the two suppressed registers, the searching, the keyed thing in you that the chair wants, and his jaw does something that is not quite anger but is the muscle anger lives in, tightening and then deliberately, visibly, releasing. Two of you, he says. And neither one's lit. You've spent your whole life sitting on your own boiler same as me. I can always tell. He shovels another scoop and bangs the firebox door shut with a control that makes the small sound smaller than it should be. So I'll ask you the only question that matters down here. Did you come to provoke me, or did you come because you finally understood that somebody's been trying to, and you wanted to see the man they're aiming at?

The choice it leaves you
Tell him the truth — someone is widening the vacant Thirteenth Seat, and you think the Rage chair is being provoked on purpose to crack the membrane faster.
He has spent forty years assuming the pressure is just the job. Naming the cause hands a man whose entire discipline is not-reacting a brand-new and entirely justified reason to react — and a Rage holder who learns his suffering is engineered is a furnace someone just told exactly who lit the match.
Say only that you came to see the man they're aiming at — keep the architecture to yourself for now.
Gentler, and it keeps the furnace banked. But a man this practised at reading approaches will feel the withheld half, and a holder who senses you are managing him is a holder who starts wondering what you think he can't be trusted to hold.
Ask him to teach you how a furnace is banked before you tell him anything — you may need to bank one yourself someday.
The witness's instinct: learn the mechanism from the only man in the city who runs one. But to study how Rage is held from a holder forty metres from the wound is to study the discipline you would need if you ever sat in a chair yourself — and he will know that is why you asked.
Banked, never out

He lets you stay a shift, and the shift teaches you that Rage is the opposite of Despair in every way that matters. Watanabe's seat was a drain, a low place the district's despair ran down into so no single person drowned in their own. Mori's seat is a furnace, and the difference is that despair wants to pool and rage wants to spread, to jump, to find the next dry thing and take it, and so the second holder's whole art is not gathering but containment — he stands in the middle of the ward's anger the way this boiler stands in the middle of the bathhouse, taking all of it in, burning it down to a heat he can stand inside, and letting out only as much as keeps the water warm and never one degree more. You can't put rage out, he tells you, feeding the fire by hand. People who think the job is putting it out burn their own house down within a year. You can't even let it go cold, because a cold furnace with this much fuel in it doesn't stay cold, it goes off. The job is banked. Hot enough that it never builds a head of pressure, low enough that it never throws a spark. Forty years I've kept this ward's temper at a simmer and not one of them knows there's a reason the fights downstairs never quite start.

You spend the night watching him do it, and the discipline is the most exhausting thing you have ever seen performed by a man standing nearly still. Every time something in the ward flares — a drunk three streets over winding himself up, a married couple two floors up finding the old wound again, a kid somewhere deciding tonight is the night he finally hits back — you feel it arrive in Mori as a small hard surge, a flush up the back of his neck, his hands closing and his breath going slow and counted, and then you feel him take it down into the firebox of himself and burn it to something the ward can stand, and let out, through the boiler, only warmth. That one was a knife, he says quietly, after a surge worse than the others. Kid two wards over had his hand on a knife and his sister's boyfriend in front of him. He doesn't know why he set it down. He'll tell himself he chickened out and hate himself a little for it. That's the job. They never get to know it was held for them. They just get to keep their hands clean and think less of themselves for it. You learn to be all right with that or you don't last.

And then, near three in the morning, the pull comes, and it is exactly the northward lean Watanabe described but it lands wrong in a furnace, it lands as a draught — you feel the vacant seat pull and you feel Mori's banked fire want to rise toward it, the way a fire leans toward an opened door, and his control goes from difficult to brutal in the space of a breath. He grips the shovel two-handed and plants his feet and you watch a sixty-year-old man hold a furnace down by main strength while something off to the north tries to open the flue. It passes. He sets the shovel against the wall with a care that is itself a held thing. That's new this year, he says, and for the first time the flatness in his voice has a hairline crack in it. The empty chair's started pulling on mine. And a furnace that gets pulled on wants to flare. You understand what I'm telling you. Despair leaning north just leans. Rage leaning north wants to go off. If the second seat ever flares — really flares, all forty years at once — it doesn't drain anywhere. It throws a spark into the thinnest part of the membrane in the whole city, which happens to be right over our heads.

The choice it leaves you
Take some of the banking onto yourself for an hour — stand in the furnace and let him step back from it once.
Your two registers might hold a portion of the heat and give a man at the limit one hour off the shovel. But to take Rage into yourself even briefly is to learn how it feels to be a furnace forty metres from the wound — and a body that has banked the second seat once is a body the second seat remembers as a fit.
Refuse to touch the fire and instead work out who is leaning on it from the north — find the provocateur.
The mapper's caution: you don't climb into a furnace, you find the hand on the flue. If someone is deliberately drawing the Rage seat toward the wound, the draught has a source — but tracing it means leaving Mori on the shovel alone for however long the hunt takes, on a night his control already cracked once.
Tell him to let it rise, just a little, in a controlled way — to find out how close the flare really is before it comes by surprise.
Dangerous arithmetic. Knowing the margin might save the membrane later — but asking a man to deliberately unbank a furnace forty years banked, forty metres from the thinnest membrane in Tokyo, is asking him to take the one risk his entire discipline exists to never take.
The hand on the flue

You find the provocation because Mori, once he lets you, knows exactly what to look for — a Rage holder spends his life feeling for the things that try to light him, and now that someone has named the pattern out loud he can point at it. Three times in the last month, he tells you, the same kind of night: a man comes into the bathhouse who does not want a bath, sits in the changing room saying small ugly things just loud enough to start something, and leaves the moment a fight nearly begins, as though the fight was the errand and bathing was the cover. Testing the boiler, Mori says. Walking in here and tapping the gauge to see how high the needle jumps. I thought he was just a sad bastard with a grudge. But he comes on the nights the pull's worst, and he leaves the second the room tips, and he's never the one who throws the first punch. He's the one who makes sure somebody else nearly does. You ask who sent him, and Mori shakes his head, and then his face does the thing you have learned to dread, the gentleness on a holder's face that means they have just understood their own ruin. He talks soft, Mori says. Soothing, almost. Calls it a shame, all this anger, says wouldn't it be a mercy if a man like me could finally set the weight down. Talks like a doctor.

You know the voice before he finishes describing it, because you have been chasing that exact bedside tone all summer — the reasonable favour, the kindness that is a hook, the Healer's borrowed warmth wearing whatever face the errand needs. The cult is not trying to break the Rage seat by attacking it. They are trying to break it by being kind to it, by walking a tired old furnace-keeper toward the idea that flaring would be a relief, that letting go would be mercy, that forty years is enough and no one would blame him — and every provoked near-fight is a tap on the gauge to measure how close that idea has brought the needle. They want me to want it, Mori says, very quietly, into the firebox. Same as the others, I'd bet. They can't make a holder flare. They can only make a holder tired enough to choose it, and then tell him choosing it is kindness. And the hell of it is some nights — some nights, listening to that soft voice — they're not entirely wrong, and that's the part that frightens me, because a furnace that half-agrees to go off is already leaking.

It is the same engine you have watched run on your brother, the same engine Watanabe described from inside Despair, the same single fact under all of it: the seat cannot be forced, only consented to, and the cult's whole patient art is the manufacture of consent in the holders worn thin enough to give it. Mori Takao is being courted toward a yes the way Gin is being courted toward a yes, by a voice that calls the worst possible choice a mercy, and he has just told you that on the bad nights the voice is starting to make sense. He bangs the firebox shut. So now you know why I made myself nobody for forty years, he says. It was never the strangers I was hiding from. It was the one who'd come talking soft. I always knew he'd come eventually. I just didn't think I'd be this tired when he did.

The choice it leaves you
Confront the soft-voiced man the next time he comes — let him see that the Rage seat is no longer unwatched.
Putting yourself between the provocateur and the furnace might end the gauge-tapping for good. But announcing to the Healer's errand-runner that you are guarding the second seat tells the Healer exactly where you are and exactly which chair you have decided to defend — and the Healer has never yet needed to be told a thing twice.
Don't confront him — teach Mori to recognise the soft voice as the hook, so the next mercy offered lands as the threat it is.
Arming the holder against his own temptation is the more durable defence; a furnace that knows the kindness is a trap is a furnace that stops half-agreeing. But it means leaving the provocateur free to keep walking in, and it puts the whole weight of resistance back on a tired old man on the nights the voice makes sense.
Use the provocateur as a thread — let him keep coming, and follow him back toward whoever holds his leash.
The mapper's gambit: a courted holder is bait you didn't have to set, and the errand-runner's route may lead to the Healer's actual room. But using Mori as live bait means leaving the gauge to keep being tapped, night after night, on a seat that has already admitted it is starting to leak.
One night's relief

You give him the night because he asks for it, finally, plainly, the way a man asks who has not asked for anything in forty years and has used up his pride doing the asking: one night off the shovel, one night where the furnace is not his sole responsibility, one night to sleep without one ear on the gauge. So you do the thing you did for Watanabe, except that Rage does not let you do it gently. You stand in the boiler room in the small hours and you take the banking onto your two unlit registers, and the ward's anger comes into you not as gravity but as fire, every flaring temper in Kita-ku arriving at once and wanting to spread, and you learn in about ninety seconds why Mori holds his hands so still — because the rage does not make you sad and it does not make you tired, it makes you right, it hands you a hundred perfectly good reasons to do something unforgivable and dresses every one of them as justice, and the discipline is not strength, it is refusal, the moment-by-moment refusal of a hundred true grievances that all deserve an answer and cannot be given one without burning the ward down.

You bank it. You stand in the middle of the city's temper and you take it in and burn it down to a heat you can stand inside and let out, through yourself, only warmth, and across the room Mori Takao lies on the changing-room bench with his arm over his eyes and sleeps, actually sleeps, for the first time in longer than he will say, and the sleeping is the whole point, and you keep the furnace banked over a sleeping man for the worst four hours of your life. And in the middle of it the soft voice nearly gets you — not a man this time, just the idea arriving the way it must arrive in Mori every bad night, the perfectly reasonable thought that you are holding a hundred just grievances unanswered, that the people upstairs and three wards over have done real wrong to real people and deserve the fire you are denying them, that letting it out would not be rage at all but justice, mercy, a setting-right — and you feel the needle jump in your own chest and understand, all the way down, how a tired holder gets walked to a yes. It is not weakness that opens the flue. It is the furnace telling you, in your own voice, that going off would be the moral thing to do.

You hold. By dawn you are emptied out and shaking and you have not flared, and Mori wakes and takes one look at your face and knows everything — knows you stood in his fire, knows the soft voice came for you too, knows you held. Now you know, he says, the same words Watanabe used, the words the Thirteen must say to the rare outsider who finally feels it from inside. It doesn't come at you like a monster. It comes at you like you're finally being honest. That's why they can't just take the seat — they have to get you to agree the fire's only fair, and a register that agrees the fire's fair has already opened the flue a crack. You held it. He says it like a man conferring something. Forty years I've wondered if anyone else could, and you did it one night and it nearly had you, and now you understand what you'd be saying yes to if it's ever your chair. Most people learn that the easy way. By being told. You'll never be able to unfeel it.

The choice it leaves you
Tell Mori what the night taught you about the 'yes' — and about your brother, who is being walked toward one in a borrowed body.
He has just confirmed the mechanism of the entire plot from inside the second seat. Telling him completes the picture and makes him an ally who knows the furnace better than anyone alive. But a Rage holder who learns your brother is being courted toward the chair is a furnace you have just handed a target the soft voice can no longer talk him out of hating.
Keep the 'yes' to yourself — you now know, in your own chest, exactly how the flue opens, and that knowledge is too dangerous to spend.
The cold discipline: you have felt the consent-mechanism fire from inside Rage as well as Despair now, and holding it close keeps the Healer from knowing how much you understand. But it means leaving the man who let you stand in his fire to keep facing the soft voice without the one piece of context that would let him name it every time.
A furnace forty metres from the wound

However the mission ends, it ends with Mori Takao back on the shovel, because no one has handed him a way out of the boiler room that does not crack the membrane over his head, and the second seat cannot be left untended any more than the first. You visit when you can through the back half of August, down the careful iron stairs, and each time the northward draught is a little worse, the flue a little harder to hold shut, the soft voice having come once or twice more in your absence and found him, each time, just tired enough to listen and just warned enough not to agree. You bought me time, he tells you on one visit, feeding the fire. Not a cure. There isn't one that doesn't end with somebody in a chair. But a man who knows the kindness is the hook can hold a furnace a good while longer than a man who thinks the kindness is real, and you gave me that. I'll burn it slow.

What you carry out of the boiler room is the same lesson Watanabe gave you in a different register, and the two of them together are the spine of everything left in the campaign: the seat is held by consent, the wall is a yes renewed daily, and the Healer's entire patient engine is the manufacture of one yes in one worn body keyed to fit. But Rage taught you the part Despair could not, which is how the yes feels from the inside when it comes — not as surrender, but as righteousness, the furnace handing you a hundred just reasons and calling the worst of them mercy. You will hear that voice again. You will hear it at the river, and you will hear it in your brother's recorded entries after the fourteenth, and now, because of one terrible night on a sleeping man's behalf, you will recognise it as the flue opening rather than the truth arriving.

Mori asks you, the last good visit, the thing the second seat would ask. If it comes to a chair, he says, not looking up from the fire, don't sit in mine. Despair you can carry tired. Rage you carry angry, and a holder who sits down angry stays angry, and the ward gets forty years of a furnace that resents them. Whatever you do with that keyed thing in you — and you'll do something, I can see it on you, you're the only piece on this board that isn't spent — don't let it be the second chair. Close the empty one if you can. Bank your own boiler if you can't. But the day you feel the fire start to make sense, the day going off feels like the honest thing — he bangs the firebox shut, gently, all that strength spent on making the sound small, — that's the day you walk careful down some other man's stairs and ask him to take the shovel, the way you walked careful down mine. Nobody banks a furnace alone forever. That's the whole secret. That's the only one I've got.

The choice it leaves you
Promise to close the vacant chair so the second seat stops being pulled — and carry the 'righteous yes' to the river as a thing you can now recognise and refuse.
You take Mori's lesson into the endgame: the flue opens when the fire feels fair, so a yes recognised is a yes you can withhold. It is the hopeful door — and a witness who has sworn to recognise and refuse the righteous yes is a witness who has half-foreclosed ever sitting in a chair to spare his brother, because he will feel that yes coming too, and he has promised to know it for what it is.
Promise nothing about the chair, but promise the smaller true thing — that if your own boiler ever starts to make sense, you'll walk careful down someone's stairs and ask for the shovel.
You take the only secret he has — that no furnace is banked alone forever — and make it a vow about your own keyed registers. It is humbler and more survivable than any promise about the vacant seat. But it is also an admission that you expect, someday, to need banking, which is its own quiet way of agreeing the chair is in your future.
Use everything the second seat taught you to go after the soft-voiced man directly — end the provocation at its source before it walks any holder to a yes.
You turn the lesson into a hunt: if the engine runs on manufactured consent, the engineer is the thing to break. It is the most active door. But hunting the Healer's errand-runner means stepping out of the boiler room for good, leaving Mori to bank alone again, and putting a register that has now felt the righteous yes from inside on a collision course with the voice that knows exactly how to offer it.
The Fear on the Stairwell written
Given by Nakashima Yuna (Fear IV)

A stairwell in a Kita danchi where the fear in the concrete is not yours and cannot be talked down. The fourth of the Thirteen is too frightened to enter her own domain; she does not need you to be unafraid — she needs you to be afraid in the right place, on purpose, so she can climb back into the chair while something holds the worst of it for her.

The flight of stairs that nobody uses

The address the bookseller gave you is a danchi in Kita-ku — one of the long pale public-housing blocks the post-war city threw up by the thousand and then forgot, a four-storey rectangle of identical doors going grey at the seams, forty metres on the wrong side of where the membrane runs thin. It is the second week of Obon and the lanterns are out on the ground-floor balconies, paper boxes of orange light hung for the dead to find their way home by, and every single one of them is on the south face of the building. Not one hangs on the north stairwell. You notice this the way you notice a held breath: not as a fact but as a pressure, the sense of a whole block of living people quietly agreeing, without ever once saying it aloud, that there is a part of their own home they do not go into after dark.

You go into it. The north stairwell is open-air, concrete treads switchbacking up the outside of the block behind a waist-high wall, and the moment your foot leaves the landing for the first riser the temperature of the thing changes — not the air, which is the same wet August heat as everywhere else in the city, but the temperature of you, some inner thermostat dropping all at once as though you had stepped into water you could not see. It is not a thought. There is no image, no figure, no sound that you could point to and call a haunting. There is only the body's oldest verdict arriving ahead of any reason for it: do not go up these stairs. Your heart is going hard before you have decided anything. Your knees have an opinion. And the worst of it, the part that tells you what you are actually standing in, is that the fear is not about anything. It has no object. It is just present in the concrete the way damp is present, a thing the structure holds, and it is reaching into you and using your own nervous system to make itself felt.

Halfway up the first flight, on the half-landing where the stairs turn back on themselves, a young woman is sitting on the bottom step of the next flight with her back against the wall and her knees drawn up, and she is shaking. She is perhaps twenty-three, in a cardigan buttoned wrong and canvas shoes with the laces gone, and she has the specific stillness of someone who has been making themselves small for a very long time and has gotten frighteningly good at it. She does not look at you. She looks at the step above her own as though it were the edge of a cliff. You can feel it, she says, not a question, her voice thin and even in a way that costs her visible effort to maintain. Most people just feel sick and leave. You came up anyway, and you can feel it, which means somebody sent you, which means it's worse than I thought. Her hands are pressed flat to the concrete on either side of her. Please don't make me explain who I am while I'm sitting in it. Just — sit down where you are. Don't come higher. And tell me whether the two of you in there are lit, because I can't hold and read you at the same time anymore.

The choice it leaves you
Sit where you are and tell her the truth — two registers, neither lit, both searching — and let her read you while you're both inside the fear.
Honesty hands a terrified woman the one fact that will let her trust you, but it also confirms what she's afraid of: that the chair recognises you, that the keyed thing in you is exactly the kind of thing the fear in this stairwell would love to get a grip on. You'd be telling the worst place in the building precisely what you are.
Don't answer yet — climb one more step toward her first, so she isn't alone in it, and let her see you choose to come closer to the thing.
Closing the distance is the kind thing and the brave thing, and it might be the thing that breaks her — she has spent who knows how long keeping people on the lower steps for a reason, and a stranger walking up into her fear without being asked may be exactly the loss of control that pulls the worst of it down on both of you.
Ask her, quietly, what is at the top of the stairs.
You want the shape of the danger before you commit to anything — but naming the top of the stairs out loud, while sitting inside a domain made of fear, is how you find out whether the fear has been waiting for someone to ask. Some questions are how the thing learns you're listening.
What the fourth seat costs to hold

Her name is Nakashima Yuna and she is the fourth of the Thirteen, and she holds Fear, and she has not been able to climb past the half-landing of her own domain for eleven days. She tells you this in pieces, in the flat careful voice, never raising it, because she has learned that her own emotion is the one thing in this stairwell she absolutely cannot afford. A holder doesn't fight the register, she says. You carry it. It runs through you like current through a wire, and the wire doesn't get to have feelings about the current, the wire just has to be a wire. The day I started being afraid of my own seat is the day the seat started leaking. She tips her head a fraction toward the flights above, where the dimming light makes the upper landings into stacked rectangles of grey. It used to live at the top. In the plant room. I'd go up once a week and sit with it and it stayed put. Now it comes down to meet me. It's on the third landing tonight. Yesterday it was on the fourth. It's learning that I'm scared, and every time it learns, it gets a step closer, and a fear holder who's running out of stairs is a domain about to come off its hinges.

You understand, sitting there with your back against the cold wall and your own pulse hammering at a threat that does not exist, that this is what the inside of a seat feels like — not the grand thing the bookseller's diagrams make it look like, twelve dignified pillars holding up the world, but a young woman who cannot go upstairs, doing the unglamorous arithmetic of how many landings she has left. The fear is not hers. She keeps saying this, gently, the way you'd correct a child who had it backward: the fear in the concrete is the register, the domain, the thing she was given to carry, and it is impersonal as weather. But the fear of the fear — the eleven days of dread that she will not be able to climb, that she will fail, that she will be the seat that fell — that part is hers, and it is the crack the register has gotten its fingers into, and she cannot stop producing it because she is human and the stakes are the wall of the world.

I keep thinking if I were braver I could just walk up, she says, and for the first time the flat voice wavers, one hairline fracture before she presses it smooth again. But that's the trap, isn't it. The braver I try to be the more I'm thinking about being brave, and the more I think about it the more I'm feeling it, and feeling it is the leak. You can't white-knuckle your way back into a fear seat. The only people who can hold Fear are people who genuinely aren't afraid in the moment, and I haven't had a moment like that in eleven days. She finally looks at you, and her eyes are doing the thing the others' do — reading the two registers, the searching, the keyed thing the chair wants — but where the others look at you with hunger or caution, she looks at you the way a drowning person looks at something that floats. You came up the stairs feeling it, she says, and your hands were steady. I watched them. How are your hands steady.

The choice it leaves you
Tell her the truth about your steady hands — that the fear isn't getting purchase on you because the registers in you are suppressed, walled off, and a walled-off person feels the dread but can't fully connect to it.
It's the answer that gives her hope and it might be exactly the wrong hope. The thing that makes you useful to a Fear seat — that you can stand in dread without it reaching all the way in — is the same suppression that's been quietly killing the rest of you, and naming it as a feature is how you start believing the wall in you is a gift instead of a wound.
Don't explain your hands — ask her instead exactly what she'd need a brave person to do, mechanically, to get her back into the seat.
Going straight to logistics keeps you both out of the spiral, but it commits you before you know the price. Holders don't ask for help cheaply, and a fear seat about to fall is going to ask for something that involves you standing between her and the worst of what's on the third landing.
Someone has been feeding the stairs

You ask her the question you have learned to ask every weakening holder, because by now you have seen the shape twice and you do not believe in coincidence anymore: has anyone been kind to you, lately? Anyone soft-voiced. Anyone who turned up around the time it started getting worse, and helped, and made you feel a little better, and left you a little more afraid than before. Yuna goes very still in a new way, the stillness of someone fitting two things together that they had been keeping carefully apart. The man from the residents' association, she says slowly. He's not — I've never seen him at a meeting. He started coming by about two weeks ago. He brings me tea on the landing. He sits a few steps down, like you, and he's so calm, he's the calmest person I've ever met, and he asks me how the seat is doing and he listens, really listens, and when he leaves I always feel — She stops. I always feel worse. I thought it was just the contrast. You feel safe with him and then he goes and you're alone with it again. But that's not it, is it. That's not it at all.

It is not it. You have read the ledger now; you know the handwriting of this kind of kindness. Someone has been coming to the fourth seat with tea and a soft voice and an open ear, and every visit he has drawn her fear up to the surface and let her dwell in it and named it gently back to her, and then taken his calm away and left the fear standing — not to torment her, nothing so crude, but to train it, to teach the register in the concrete that its holder is afraid, to walk it down one landing at a time toward the moment the seat slips its mooring and Fear goes vacant beside a membrane already thin. It is the same architecture as the Rage seat in the bathhouse, the same patient cruelty wearing the face of help: not an attack the holders can fight, but a kindness they cannot refuse, aimed at thinning the wall of the world from the inside by emptying the chairs that hold it up. The Thirteenth Seat is vacant. Someone wants company for it on the wall that leans north.

He's coming tonight, Yuna says, and the flat voice has gone to a whisper because the understanding is itself a spike of the very thing leaking out of her. He said he'd come tonight to see how I was doing. He's always so worried about how I'm doing. She looks up the stairs, to the third landing where the grey is thickest, and then down past you to the lower flight where the orange Obon light from the south face barely reaches. If he comes and sits and does it again I won't have a fourth landing left. I'll have the plant room and then I'll have the roof and then I'll have nothing, and the seat will come off, and you felt what one stairwell's worth of it does to a person walking up. Imagine it with no holder. Imagine it loose over Kita-ku during Obon, with the doors already open for the dead. She takes a breath that shakes the whole length of it. So I need to know what you are before he gets here. Because I think you might be the only thing in this building that can stand on the landing between us and not break.

The choice it leaves you
Decide to be on the half-landing when the soft-voiced man arrives — let him do his kindness with you in the room, so you can see the technique and Yuna can watch it done to her from the outside.
Witnessing the provocation as it happens is how you learn to undo it — but it means letting a man who hollows out holders run his whole method on Yuna one more time, with her last landing on the line, on the gamble that seeing it will be worth surviving it.
Get her off the stairs before he comes — physically walk her down and out, break the appointment, deny him his session even if it means the seat goes unheld for the night.
Pulling her out tonight is the humane move and it cedes the domain: an unattended Fear seat over the thin ward during Obon is exactly the lurch the membrane plan is built to produce, and you'd be handing it to them clean to spare her one more bad hour.
Stay, but turn the soft-voiced man's own method back on him — plan to greet his kindness with a calm of your own and let Yuna see that the calmest man in the room is not always the safe one.
Beating him at his own register is satisfying and it drags you toward becoming the thing you're fighting — to out-calm a man who weaponises calm, you have to learn how he does it, and the keyed thing in you is exactly the apparatus that would learn it fastest and like it most.
Brave in the right place, on purpose

Here is the act, and it is not the one the stories would pick. You do not climb to the third landing and face down the fear like a thing with a throat you can cut. There is nothing up there to face. What Yuna needs is smaller and far harder: she needs to climb back into her own seat, and she cannot do it while she is afraid, and she will be afraid the whole way up — so you go up the stairs ahead of her, one step at a time, and you stand on each landing as you reach it and you take the fear that lives there into your own walled-off chest where it cannot get all the way in, and you hold it, so that for the length of one breath the stair she is climbing onto is the one place in the building where the dread is being carried by someone else. You are not removing it. You cannot remove it. You are being afraid in the right place, on purpose, a half-second ahead of her, so that she steps into ground that has just been held.

It is the worst climbing you have ever done. The fear on the second landing is bad and the third is worse and the dread coming off the plant-room door at the top is a physical weight that bows your neck, the body's whole ancient machinery screaming a danger that has no shape and no end, and the only thing that lets you keep your feet on it is the exact wound you have spent your life ashamed of — the suppression, the wall, the two registers held down so long they have gone half-numb, so that the fear reaches into you and finds the connection it wants already severed. You are useful here precisely because you are broken in this particular way, and somewhere under the dread a quiet voice you do not like the sound of is saying see, the wall is good, the wall is a gift, why would you ever take it down — and you climb anyway, and you keep that voice on the landing below you with the rest of what you're carrying. Behind you, one step down, Yuna climbs into the air you have just made bearable, and she is shaking, and she does not stop, and she does not need to be unafraid because for the first time in eleven days she is not afraid alone.

At the top, at the plant-room door, she puts her hand flat against the steel where the register is thickest, and you feel it happen — the current finding its wire again, the impersonal weather of Fear pouring back through its holder instead of pooling loose in the concrete, the whole stairwell exhaling a cold it had been hoarding. The dread does not vanish from the building; it is a domain, it is supposed to be there, fear is one of the true things the world is made of and someone has to carry it. But it goes back up into the plant room where it lives, and it stops coming down to meet her, because she is in the chair again and the chair does not flinch. Yuna stands with her palm on the door and her wrong-buttoned cardigan and her ruined shoes, and she breathes, all the way to the bottom of one breath, the first one in eleven days that does not feed the leak. Oh, she says, very softly, to the door. Oh. There you are. I'm sorry. I'm here. I'm not going anywhere.

The choice it leaves you
Tell her what the climb taught you — that you could only hold the fear because of the wall in you, and that you don't know yet whether that wall is the thing saving you or the thing eating you.
Saying it aloud to the one person who just watched the wall work makes it real, and real is dangerous: she will, gently and gratefully, tell you the wall is a gift, because tonight it saved her seat — and you will want very badly to believe the person you just rescued.
Say nothing about yourself — let her have the moment with her seat undivided, and carry the question about your own wall back down the stairs alone.
Giving her the moment clean is generous and it leaves you with the worst possible thing to be alone with on a dark stairwell: a freshly proven reason to keep the wall up, no one to argue you out of it, and a quiet voice that's been waiting all night for exactly this privacy.
The man who came with tea

He arrives while you are still at the top, and you hear him before you see him — the unhurried scuff of careful shoes on concrete, the small domestic clink of a flask against a cup, a man climbing into a stairwell that should be terrifying as calmly as if he were coming up to water the plants. He is unremarkable in the specific way the dangerous ones are: middle-aged, soft-faced, a cardigan of his own, kind eyes, the residents'-association lanyard he has no right to. He gets two landings up before he registers that the stairwell is wrong for him tonight — that the fear has gone home, that the holder is in her chair, that there is someone standing at the top who is not afraid and is watching him climb. He stops. He looks up at you and Yuna with an expression of such gentle, genuine-seeming concern that for one full second you understand exactly how he has gotten into so many rooms. Yuna-san, he says warmly, you're up so high tonight. I was worried. You shouldn't push yourself. And you watch her hear it — the worry, the push-yourself, the soft little weight laid on the fear to draw it back up — and you watch it not work, because she is in the seat now and the seat does not flinch, and the disappointment that crosses his kind face before he smooths it away tells you everything about what he actually came for.

He does not fight. They never do, this kind; fighting is for the registers, and he is not a register, he is a method. He simply absorbs that tonight's session is not available, recalculates with the frictionless speed of a man who has many other appointments, and begins, unhurried, to descend. I'm glad you have a friend, he says over his shoulder, mild as milk, and the worst part is that it sounds like he means it, that somewhere in the architecture of him there is something that does experience this as kindness even as it empties the chairs that hold the sky up. Everyone should have someone to be brave for them. It's so much harder alone. He is telling you he knows what you did and how, and he is filing it, the way the Healer's people file everything — that the fourth seat can be re-held by someone with a wall instead of a heart, that the keyed thing that walked up these stairs tonight is exactly the apparatus the work could use. You came to save a holder. You have also, without meaning to, shown the other side a new tool, and they were taking notes the whole time you climbed.

When he is gone — and he goes all the way, you make sure, you stand at the rail and watch the soft shoulders recede across the lit courtyard and out under the Obon lanterns until the dark takes him — Yuna comes and stands beside you at the top of the stairwell she could not climb an hour ago. The orange light from the south face throws long warm rectangles across the courtyard, the paper boxes burning low for the dead finding their way home, and on the north face, for the first time in eleven days, the fear is just weather in a plant room and a young woman is holding it. He'll go to the next one, she says quietly. Whoever's weakest. There's always a weakest. She is not wrong. Somewhere across the thin ward there is a fifth seat, a ninth, an eleventh, each one being visited by someone with a flask and a kind face and infinite patience, and you cannot be on every stairwell. But you were on this one. Thank you for being afraid up here, Yuna says, which is the strangest gratitude you have ever been given and the truest, and she means: thank you for not making me do it alone.

The choice it leaves you
Promise her you'll go after the man with the flask — follow the method back to whoever is running it, before he empties another chair.
It's the promise that matters and the promise that costs: chasing the method means walking straight up the chain toward the Healer's work, and a man who weaponises calm will be delighted to be followed by the one person whose wall makes them a candidate for the very thing they're hunting.
Promise her only the smaller, keepable thing — that you'll come back, that she won't have to be brave entirely alone again, and let the larger war stay unpromised.
The small promise is the one you can actually keep, which is exactly why it's the harder one to make — it ties you to a single stairwell in a thinning ward, and every weakest seat you can't reach will be a thing you chose this one over.
Promise nothing out loud — take what the climb taught you about your own wall and carry it toward the question that's been growing all night: whether the thing that makes you useful is the thing you most need to put down.
Refusing to promise keeps you honest and leaves her with no one named for the next bad night, and it points you at the most dangerous reckoning there is — because the answer to whether the wall is a gift or a wound is the answer to who you become before the seat that's really vacant gets filled.
The Hunger With No Address written
Given by Inoue Satoru (Hunger IX)

A holder with no fixed location, moving so the appetite he carries cannot settle. Find him before the campaign-demon's pressure tips the seat — and decide whether a man can be kept from what he holds.

The man who will not sit down

You find the ninth seat the way you find weather — by where the city bends around it. The bookseller's note gave you no address, because there is no address; it gave you a habit instead, a route that is never the same route twice, and a single line in his cramped hand: He eats so he does not have to stop, and he moves so the appetite cannot find the door. So you walk Ueno after dark in the second week of Obon, past the all-night ramen counters and the conveyor-sushi places still lit at one in the morning, and you look not for a face but for a kind of motion — and somewhere between the station and the park you start to feel it the way you felt the cold on the stairwell, an inward leaning, a low constant note under the festival noise: more. Not your more. A more that belongs to no particular thing, that does not resolve into wanting food or drink or company, that is simply appetite with the object filed off, reaching into you and trying your own emptiness for a handle.

He is at a standing counter eating gyoza, and he is already half-turned to leave before the plate is finished. A spare man in his fifties, neat in a way that has gone threadbare at the cuffs, with the specific economy of someone who owns only what he can carry and has learned to eat fast and walk faster. He does not sit. You will come to understand that he has not properly sat down in something like four years, that the stools and benches and chairs of the city are all very slightly his enemies, and that the one piece of furniture he genuinely fears is a bed. He clocks you the moment the appetite in the air touches you back — feels his own register find a foothold in a stranger — and his eyes go to you with the tired, practised wariness of a man who has been hunted by quieter things than you. You feel it, he says around the last gyoza, already paying, already moving. Then walk while you talk, because if I stand here much longer it learns this counter, and I like this counter, and I'd rather not have to give it up.

So you walk. He sets a pace that is not quite hurried and never once slackens, threading the festival crowds, the families with their lanterns coming back from the temple, the long warm tables of the night markets, and as he moves the more moves with him, a half-step behind, a thing on a leash that he keeps just barely ahead of by never, ever stopping. Inoue Satoru, he says, when you are clear of the counter and the appetite has unhooked from it. Ninth of the Thirteen. I carry Hunger. He says it the way another man might say he carries a bad heart — a fact about the body, load-bearing and permanent and not up for discussion. And before you ask the thing everyone asks: no, I cannot just put it down for one night and sleep. That is exactly the thing I cannot do. That is the whole job.

The choice it leaves you
Match his pace and tell him the truth about what you are — two registers, suppressed, searching — and let him feel what's keyed in you while you both keep moving.
Honesty buys a wary man's trust, but you'd be confirming the worst thing a Hunger seat could hope and dread: that the stranger walking beside it is carrying its own filed-off emptiness, the kind of lack the appetite would love nothing better than to settle into.
Say nothing about yourself yet — just keep up, keep quiet, and let him see you can hold his pace without flagging.
Earning his trust by endurance keeps your secret a little longer, but a man who reads appetite for a living will notice what you're NOT saying, and the silence will tell him you have something walled off — which to a hungry register reads less like discretion and more like a locked pantry.
Ask him the question he just told you everyone asks — why he can't simply stop for one night — and make him explain the seat.
You want the mechanism before you commit, but asking a man to describe the exact shape of the thing eating him, out loud, while it walks a half-step behind him, is how you find out whether naming it draws it closer. Some registers come when called.
What the ninth seat costs to hold

He explains it as you cross from Ueno toward Okachimachi, under the train arches where the late izakaya are still going, and he explains it walking, always walking, because to explain it standing still would be to demonstrate the problem. A holder doesn't satisfy the register, he says. You can't. Hunger isn't a wanting that gets a thing and stops — it's the wanting underneath all the wantings, the empty that's there before there's anything to fill it. My job isn't to feed it. My job is to keep it from finding out where it lives. He buys a skewer from a stall without breaking stride, eats it in four bites, drops the stick in a bin he has clearly used before. Every time I stop somewhere long enough, it learns the place. It writes the address down. And a Hunger seat with a fixed address starts pulling the empty toward it — out of the people nearby, out of the food that stops being enough, out of the holder, until the holder is the hungriest thing in the room and the room is starting to come apart. So I move. Four years now. The appetite doesn't get a door because I don't have one.

You understand, walking the warm midnight with a man who cannot stop, that this is the unglamorous physics of a seat — not a throne but a sentence, not power but a load you arrange your entire life around carrying without dropping.
