She pulls the intake log on Gin's last closed case — the one shut too fast, the address in no file — and she does it from a terminal that keeps her name, because she has decided you deserve to see it and she has not done the arithmetic on what it costs a second-year to be found doing that. When she slides the printout across the table in the noodle place near the Directorate annex, her hand is steady and her voice is not, and you realise she has crossed a line for you that she would never cross for herself, and that this is not strength, exactly — it is something younger and more breakable that has chosen to stand where strength would stand. Shimizu Ren would have her badge for it. She knows. She did it anyway.

    So you do the thing you are not used to doing, which is to slow down. You put your hand flat on the printout before you read it and you tell her, plainly, what it could do to her, and you watch her register that you saw the cost when she had hidden it well enough that no one was supposed to. You're the first person who's checked, she says, and looks down, because the hero she built would simply have taken the gift. The tired man across the table did not. That is the moment the shape she carries of you cracks, gently, and the person underneath — smaller, more frightened, more real — turns out to be someone she likes better. You did not want to be loved as Gin's competent shadow. You did not, until now, let yourself notice how much you did not want it.

  3. Turn — the turn — gated on standing in front of the fall that was meant for her

    The turn is not a confession; it is the morning the audit lands. Internal Compliance flags the intake pull, and there is exactly one name on the access record, and it is hers, and a second-year does not survive a flag like that — it ends careers quieter and faster than any demon does. You are in the corridor when Shimizu Ren calls her in, and you have a clean way out: it was her query, her clearance, her mistake, and your road through the Directorate stays open if you let the record stand. Instead you walk in first. You tell Shimizu you asked Aoi to run it, that you leaned on a junior's loyalty because you were desperate about your brother, that the lapse is yours to wear. It is mostly true and entirely a choice, and it shuts a door you needed open, and you shut it on purpose, in front of her, so that she will know the difference between being used and being protected.

    Afterward she finds you on the annex stairwell where the air-conditioning leaks cold onto the concrete, furious in the particular way of someone who has just been kept safe against her will. I can carry my own mistakes, she says, and you say I know, and you mean it, and that is the whole turn — that you did not save her because she was weak but because you had finally decided her heart was worth more than your road, and you wanted her to outlast this summer whether or not she ever forgave you for it. She is quiet a long time. The river is somewhere below, carrying the first of the Obon lanterns out toward the bay. When she takes your hand it is not gratitude. It is a person choosing, with her eyes open, the man who shut the door — not the one she invented in the spring.

  4. Fade — the fade — warm, unhurried, the lanterns going out toward the water

    It is the last night of Obon and the families along the Sumida are setting their dead loose on the current, small lit boats of folded paper that gutter and right themselves and drift, and Aoi stands beside you at the rail with her shoulder against your arm and does not say anything clever, because she has finally stopped needing to be impressive in front of you. You think about Gin out there in the wrong shape, somewhere north, somewhere wet, and you find that for one hour you can hold that grief in one hand and this — her warmth, her breathing, the ridiculous earnest steadiness of her — in the other, and neither cancels the other out. She catches you watching the lanterns and not the water. Come on, she says, gently, and it is the first time anyone has invited you somewhere as though you were allowed to be wanted rather than needed.

    Her flat is small and over a shuttered stationer's and smells of the green tea she drinks too much of, and she apologises for the mess that is not a mess, and you understand that the apology is fear wearing manners. So you are careful — careful the way she was brave, which is to say more than is asked. You let the evening be slow. You let her be the one who decides, and she decides, and the city hums its summer hum through the thin glass while the last lanterns reach the dark of the bay and go out one by one. What happens after the light goes off belongs to the two of you and to no file anywhere; in the morning there is the smell of tea again and her hand finding yours before her eyes are even open, and the day with the brother still in it is waiting, and for once you are not facing it alone.

Hagiwara Mizuki written
Ceiling: fade to black · never minors
  1. Notice — the professional warmth, and the half-second it slips

    You meet Hagiwara Mizuki in the register where everyone meets her: Public Confidence, the smile that has reassured nine hundred frightened civilians that the thing on their landing was a draught and the cold spot a plumbing fault. She is very good. She is the best you have ever watched, and you are a reader by birth and trade, and that is precisely why you notice — the warmth is real the way a stage light is real, exactly as bright as the room requires and not one lumen more, dimmed and raised by a hand you can almost see on the dial. She manages your discomfort about Gin so smoothly that you are halfway to comforted before you catch the technique, and catching it is the first interesting thing that has happened between you, because she watches you catch it, and something behind the performance goes briefly, genuinely still.

    It is a half-second and then the smile comes back up and she is once more the woman whose job is the gap between what the public knows and what is happening. But you have read the still place under it now, and you cannot unread it: a discipline thirty years deep, a person who has performed warmth so long and so well that meaning it has become the dangerous, expensive, almost-forgotten thing. You leave the conversation with the file you came for and the small dangerous knowledge that Hagiwara Mizuki noticed you noticing, and did not, for once, reach for the dial.

  2. Trust — she gives you what she thinks, not what she says

    Trust with Mizuki is not built out of time spent; she has spent managed time with thousands. It is built out of a single substitution. There comes a night — late, the office emptied, a cover story half-written on her screen about a Kita incident you both know was no plumbing fault — when she stops giving you the public version and gives you, instead, what she actually thinks: that the cover-ups are sometimes the only thing standing between a frightened city and a panic that would get more people killed than the truth ever could, and that she has known this for thirty years and believed it for twenty-nine and has lately, for the first time, begun to lie awake doubting the maths. To say that to anyone is a breach of a discipline she has built her whole self on. To say it to Gin's brother, who reads people for a living and cannot be performed at, is to hand you the one thing she has never given the machine: the cost.

    You understand what it is the moment she does it, because you have your own suppressed registers and you know exactly what it takes to let one down on purpose. She is not flirting. She is doing something far more naked than flirting — she is letting a person see the price of the lies she keeps, which she has never let anyone see, because seeing it is the first step to being unable to keep them. The trust runs both ways and frightens you equally: she could end you with a sentence to the right committee, and she is choosing, instead, to be doubted out loud in front of you, which for a woman whose entire value is certainty is the closest thing to undressing she owns.

  3. Turn — gated: you protect her breach instead of using it
    Gated on: Rei has to bury or deflect the discrepancy Mizuki let slip — taking a real risk to his own standing to protect her one true thing — rather than trading it to Moriya or the Grid for leverage on Gin.

    The turn is not a confession and it cannot be bought with one. It is gated on what you do with the thing she gave you, because Mizuki has spent thirty years watching people receive a confidence and calculate its market value, and she will not love anyone she has not first watched decline to spend her. The discrepancy she let slip — the doubt, the half-written cover story, the admission that the maths might be wrong — is worth a great deal to the right buyer. Moriya in Internal Affairs would trade you real movement on Gin's file for it. The Grid would take it as proof of what they already believe. You hold, in other words, the exact currency this whole morning has taught you to spend, and the turn happens only if you refuse to spend it — if you take a risk to your own standing to bury her breach, deflect the committee's attention, protect the one true thing she let exist outside the machine, and let her watch you get nothing for it.

    When she realises what you have done — and she will realise it, she reads institutions the way you read rooms — the dial-hand comes off the warmth entirely, for the first time since you met her, and what is left is not a performance dimmed to nothing but a woman, tired and thirty years disciplined and astonished to be protected rather than managed. You could have had Gin's file moved, she says, not quite believing it, and you let the silence say that you know, and she crosses the room. What passes between you after that is the thing she has performed a thousand times and meant never: warmth raised by no hand on any dial, exactly as bright as the two of you require and, for once, no one watching to dim it. The door closes on the empty office, and the cover story stays half-written on the dark screen until morning.

  4. Fade — one true thing, kept outside the machine

    What you have with Mizuki afterward is not a secret the way her work is a secret; it is the opposite of her work, the one arrangement in her life that no committee manages and no cover story explains. She still performs warmth all day — the city still needs the dial, and she is still the best hand on it the Division has — but she comes back, at night, to the single room where she does not have to, and lets the performance fall off her at the door like a wet coat. You are careful with it the way you are careful with anything that took thirty years to risk. She protects you for real now, which is a more dangerous thing than her job allows, and you protect her breach the way you protected it the first time, and between the two of you there is finally one true thing kept alive outside the machine that you both, in your different ways, serve.

    It does not fix what she does for a living and you do not ask it to. Some nights the maths still keeps her awake, and you lie beside her in the dark and do not perform comfort, because she would catch it, and catching it would ruin the only place she has ever been able to stop reading the dial. That is the whole of it and it is enough: the woman whose gift is making frightened people believe the comfortable lie has one person she does not have to lie to, and one room where the warmth she raises is meant. The fade is that room, and the door of it, and the morning that finds the cover story still half-written because for one night neither of you needed it.

Katagiri Noa written
Ceiling: fade to black · never minors
  1. Notice — the resemblance is the obstacle, not the draw

    Katagiri Noa worked Gin's last months from the inside of the CVD and she knows things about your brother that are not in any file, which is the reason you seek her out and the reason it goes wrong the first three times. She looks at you and flinches — not at you, at the resemblance, the jaw and the doorway-stance and the way you hold a teacup as if apologising to it — and you watch her grief do the arithmetic that grief does, subtracting the living man in front of her from the dead-or-worse one she is mourning, and arriving, every time, at a remainder she cannot stand to sit with. You are, for the first several conversations, not a person to Noa. You are an echo with a pulse, and she is too honest to pretend otherwise and too kind to say it, so she gives you the facts you came for in a voice held very flat and gets you out of her sightline as fast as decency allows.

    And you notice her anyway, under the flinch, which is its own complication. You notice that the grief is clean — not the performed grief of the office but the real kind, folded and put away and taken out only when she thinks no one reads her — and that it is grief for a man she clearly loved in some register she has never named even to herself. You notice that she chose not to file something about Gin's last months, kept it back, carried it, which is the act of a person whose loyalties run deeper than her clearance. You notice, in short, a person worth knowing, standing behind a wall built of your own brother's face, and you understand that to be noticed back you will have to become, to her, someone other than the ghost.

  2. Trust — she lets a new thing exist without it erasing the old

    Trust arrives the day you stop trying to be a comfort to her about Gin and let her be one to you instead — the day you sit in her grief without managing it, without offering the resemblance as a consolation prize, without doing the thing everyone does and saying he'd be proud in the voice that asks to be thanked. You just let it be true that you are both mourning the same man in different tenses, hers past and yours present, and that neither tense cancels the other. Something in Noa unclenches when she sees that you are not going to use her love for Gin as a door into her, that you would rather she kept it intact than spent any of it on you. She starts, cautiously, to look at you and see you — the tired nineteen-year-old reading every room he enters because he cannot help it, the brother who is not a replacement and has stopped, in front of her, trying to be a balm.

    What she gives you in return for not erasing her grief is the thing she chose not to file: the truth of Gin's last months as she watched them from the inside, the changes she logged in her own private memory and never in the system because the system would have done something administrative and irreversible with them. It is a breach of her training and a far deeper trust than the facts themselves — she is telling you that she protected your brother from his own institution, quietly, at risk to herself, out of a love she has never once named. You receive it the way she gave it, without making it about the two of you, and that restraint is what finally lets the wall down. The resemblance stops being a haunting. It becomes, at last, just a family likeness, and she can look at it without subtracting.

  3. Turn — gated: you choose her cleanly, not as Gin's echo
    Gated on: Rei has to act on what Noa chose not to file in a way that honours Gin without using Noa as the route to him — and, in a moment where leaning on the resemblance would get him what he wants, deliberately refuse to be the ghost she's grieving.

    The turn is gated on a refusal, and it is the hardest refusal on this road, because the resemblance works and you both know it. There comes a moment — late in the chase north, when you need something only Noa can give and she is raw and the dead man's face is right there on you like a key in a lock — when leaning on the likeness would get you everything: the access, the protection, the closeness, all of it pouring toward you on a current of love that was never meant for you. The turn happens only if you decline to draw on that current. If you use what she chose not to file to honour Gin honestly, without making Noa the route to him; if, in the moment the resemblance would do all the work, you put it down on purpose and make her look at the person you actually are and choose him, not the ghost — knowing she might not, knowing you are trading a sure thing for a true one.

    When she chooses you anyway — Rei, the living one, the one whose grief is present tense and whose face is only a coincidence she has made her peace with — it is the cleanest thing either of you has done in a season of compromises. There is no substitution in it. She is not loving Gin through you and you are not being loved as a relic, and the absence of that lie is so unfamiliar to both of you that you spend a long quiet moment just located in it, two people who got here by refusing the easy version. What follows keeps that honesty: warmth that belongs to the two of you and to no third absent party, a door closing on a small apartment where, for once, the dead are allowed to stay dead and the living are allowed to want each other plainly. The grief is still hers. It is simply, finally, sitting in a different room.

  4. Fade — two true things, instead of one substituting the other

    What you build with Noa is a deliberate refusal of the most natural mistake either of you could make, and the discipline of it is what makes it real. She grieves Gin and she chooses you, and these are two true things held side by side rather than one quietly standing in for the other, and the holding takes daily work neither of you resents. She will say his name in the dark sometimes and you will let it be his name, not a wound and not a comparison, and you will say it back, because you are mourning him too and the one place you are both allowed to do it out loud turns out to be here, with each other, the two people who loved him in incompatible tenses and decided not to make that a competition.

    If the campaign takes Gin from you both, the grief you already practised holding without substitution is the thing that lets you survive it together rather than dissolving into each other's loss. If it gives him back, damaged and present, Noa is the one who taught you that a person can be loved without being asked to replace anyone, and you find you know how to receive a brother on those terms because she showed you. Either way the fade is the same small apartment, the same allowance — that the dead may stay dead and the living may still, plainly, without apology or echo, want each other — and the quiet, hard-won knowledge that you got here the long way, by putting down the key that would have opened the lock too easily.

Tendo Akira written
Ceiling: fade to black · never minors
  1. Notice — an anomaly knows an anomaly

    Tendo Akira flirts with the room the way some men breathe — easily, expensively, meaning none of it — and you would have written him off as charm-shaped and hollow except that the first time he turns the charm on you, you read straight through it, and he feels you do it. There is a flicker, fast as Mizuki's dial slipping, and then he doubles down on the performance precisely because it has been seen, which is the most honest thing he has done all evening and he does not know it. He is an anomaly the way you are an anomaly — something the instruments read wrong, a person who has spent his life being plausible because being legible was never safe — and two anomalies recognising each other across a crowded Gathering is its own dangerous intimacy, a click of mutual identification neither of you asked for and neither can quite take back.

    You notice that the charm is armour and that it is very good armour, fitted over years, and that underneath it is an operative who has never once, in his charmed and privileged life, met a consequence his family's network could not pre-empt. You notice that he clocks your suppressed registers the way you clock his easy lie, and that the clocking unsettles him more than it unsettles you, because you came here knowing you were hiding and he has half-convinced himself the armour is him. He gives you his number with a line he has used a hundred times, and you take it knowing the line is nothing and the giving is the tell, and you both pretend, charmingly, that nothing has been recognised at all.

  2. Trust — reaching the operative under the charm

    Trust with Tendo is a matter of refusing to be charmed past, repeatedly, until he runs out of armour and has to stand in front of you as the thing the armour was covering. It takes a while. He is genuinely delightful and the delight is a wall, and every time you get near the operative underneath he deploys another easy, glittering, frictionless charm, and every time you decline to be deflected by it something in him both panics and, helplessly, hopes. The turn in the trust comes the night he tries to charm his way out of a real fear — something about the family network, the recognition that the two of you are visible in a way that even his privilege cannot indefinitely buy off — and you simply do not let him, you stay in the conversation past the point where the charm should have ended it, and he discovers there is a person on the other side of his performance who would rather have him frightened and real than smooth and gone.

    When the armour finally comes off it is not graceful, because he has never done it and has no practice at being unpolished. What is under it is sharper and sadder than the surface and far better company: an operative who reads consequence coming the way you read rooms, who has spent his whole life one charming half-step ahead of every reckoning, and who is, for the first time, with someone he cannot outrun because that someone can see him doing the running. He trusts you with the fear he has never named — that the network which shielded him is also the leash, that being recognised is the one thing the charm cannot fix — and the trust is the more total for how badly he does it. You have reached the operative under the charm, and the operative, it turns out, has been very lonely behind all that ease.

  3. Turn — gated: a consequence his privilege can't pre-empt
    Gated on: Rei has to stand between Tendo and a reckoning the family network would normally erase — taking the consequence onto himself rather than letting Tendo charm or buy his way clear — so that, for the first time, Tendo meets something the privilege can't pre-empt and is chosen through it rather than rescued out of it.

    The turn is gated on the one thing Tendo's life has never contained: a consequence that does not get pre-empted. It comes when the visibility you both feared arrives for real — a reckoning the family network would ordinarily make vanish before it touched him — and you put yourself between him and it, take the cost onto your own standing, and refuse to let him charm or buy or outrun his way clear of this one. Not because he cannot escape it; he could, the network is right there. But because you decline to let him, and you stay, and you make him meet the consequence as a real thing with you beside him in it rather than as one more problem money makes disappear. For a man who has never in his life been unable to slip a reckoning, being held inside one — on purpose, by someone who is paying for the privilege of holding him there — is more naked than any touch.

    He does not know how to be rescued by being refused rescue, and the not-knowing is where he finally stops performing. The charm has nowhere to go; the consequence will not be flirted with; and there is only you, having spent something real to stand in it with him, asking nothing and offering only presence. That is the moment he chooses you, or rather the moment he stops being able to choose anyone else — the first time in his charmed life that he has been wanted through a reckoning instead of out of one. What follows keeps the honesty the armour never allowed: two anomalies who have stopped being plausible at each other, a door closing on the recognition that started across a crowded room and has nowhere left to hide. He is frightened and real and, for once, not running, and you are the reason, and he lets you be.

  4. Fade — he stops outrunning himself

    What you have with Tendo afterward is the slow, improbable project of a man learning to stand still. The charm does not disappear — it is genuinely part of him and you would not want it gone — but it stops being the whole drawbridge, stops slamming up the instant anything real approaches, and underneath it the operative gets to simply exist in your company without being one half-step ahead of his own life. He is startled, daily, by being known and not leaving; you watch him brace for the disappointment he has always heard coming and watch it, repeatedly, not come, and the watching is its own kind of tenderness. Two anomalies, visible to each other, plausible to no one, finally not performing.

    The leash is still real and the family still wants its charming, useful son, and there will be reckonings the network cannot make vanish now that the two of you are recognised together. You both know it. But he has learned, from the one consequence you would not let him slip, that being chosen through a reckoning is survivable — better than survivable — and he stops outrunning himself long enough to be wanted plainly, in a quiet room, by someone who saw through the armour on the first night and stayed anyway. The fade is that room, and the unfamiliar stillness in it, and a man who spent his whole life half a step ahead of everything finally, for an evening, exactly where he is.

Okada Shizuka written
Ceiling: fade to black · never minors
  1. Notice — the woman who reads your rank and remembers the trend

    Okada Shizuka reads your rank twice a month and writes the number down, and the first thing you notice about her is that she does not look at the number when she does it — she looks at you, briefly, the way someone checks a thermometer they have read a thousand times and trusts less than the patient's face. She is quiet and exact and so easy to overlook that the overlooking is plainly a skill, a thing she has filed down over years until being unseen became a kind of vantage; from her chair in Assessment and Registry she sees everyone the room forgets, which is most of the room, which is, you slowly realise, the whole point of sitting there. She does not flirt and does not perform and does not, in any way you can name, reach for you, and yet there is a steadiness in how she handles your file that feels less like procedure and more like someone keeping a promise she has not mentioned.

    It is the cat that tells you, though she will deny the cat means anything. She mentions it the way other people mention weather — an ordinary cat, she insists, a flat in Ōji, a shrine on Saturdays, a bakery after — and you read straight through the small fixed rituals to the woman who built them on purpose, who keeps one foot in a normal life because it is the only ballast she has. You notice she already knows things about you she should have had to ask, the trend in your registers, the dates that matter, the shape of a case four years closed, and when you notice it she sees you notice and does not flinch, only sets down her pen and lets the silence hold the weight of how long she has been carrying something of yours without being asked to.

  2. Trust — four years alone with a confidence she never put down

    Trust with Shizuka is the slow erosion of a privacy she has guarded so long she mistakes it for personality. She is not cold; she is conserved, every reaction sized to the minimum the moment requires, and you come to understand that the exactness is not temperament but rationing — a woman who decided, some years ago, that she could afford to feel exactly as much as a situation demanded and not one degree more, because the alternative had once nearly drowned her. The thaw is undramatic and therefore unmistakable: a second cup poured without asking, a Saturday ritual described in enough detail that the describing is an invitation, the number in your file read one month with something that is almost, for her, a held breath. She lets you near the way a careful person tests ice, and you have the sense, each time, of being trusted with more than she has handed anyone in a long while.

    What she finally tells you is the confidence she has carried for four years, the thing Gin asked of her before he was lost to you both: watch for him. She says it plainly, the way she says everything, and only the smallest tremor at the edge of it betrays how heavy four years of solitary watching has been — how she started on your side because your brother asked, and stayed because there was no one to hand the watching to, and grew into the keeping of you so quietly that even she had stopped noticing it had become hers. The trust, when it comes, is not the secret itself but her letting you see the cost of having kept it alone: that the steadiness everyone overlooks was a woman holding a promise in an empty room, year after year, telling no one, asking for nothing, becoming the kind of person who could be overlooked precisely so that the weight she carried would never have to be explained.

  3. Turn — gated: wanted for herself, not as a duty discharged
    Gated on: Rei has to release Shizuka from the promise she made Gin — to say, and mean, and act so that she believes it, that he wants her for herself and not as the keeper of his brother's instruction — choosing her in a way that owes Gin nothing, so that for the first time she is wanted rather than dutiful.

    The turn is gated on the one thing Shizuka has never let herself be: wanted for her own sake rather than as the keeper of a promise. It comes when you understand that everything between you could so easily run on Gin's old instruction — that you could lean on the watching she swore to do, accept the care as a duty she is discharging, and never once have to earn her — and you refuse it. You release her from it, out loud, in a way she cannot mistake for kindness or procedure: you tell her the promise is hers to set down, that you do not want the woman who was told to watch for you, you want the woman who keeps a cat and a shrine and a private steadiness no one ever thanks her for, and you choose that woman in a register that owes your brother nothing. For someone who has only ever been wanted as the holder of someone else's trust, being wanted as the bare fact of herself is more exposing than any confession.

    She does not know how to be the one who is wanted; she has spent four years being the one who keeps, and the keeping was safe precisely because it asked nothing back. So when you set down the promise for her, when you make it unmistakable that there is no duty left to hide inside, she has nowhere to put the wanting except to let it land. That is the moment she stops conserving — the exactness gives way, just once, the rationed woman spending more than the moment strictly requires because the moment is, for the first time, only about her. What follows keeps the quiet that is hers: a neat flat in Ōji, an ordinary cat pretending not to watch, a door closing on a woman who was overlooked on purpose for years and is, tonight, the most seen person in the city. She is wanted, not owed, and she lets herself believe it.

  4. Fade — the keeper lets herself be kept

    What you have with Shizuka afterward is the gentle reversal of a lifetime's arithmetic: the woman who kept watch lets herself, in small uninsured ways, be watched over. The exactness does not vanish — it is genuinely her, and you would not file it down — but it stops being a wall around the rationing, and underneath it there is someone who can now afford to feel a degree more than the situation requires because the surplus is, at last, safe somewhere. She still reads your rank twice a month and still writes the number down, but the checking has become a different thing, a ritual the two of you share rather than a vigil she keeps alone, and you watch her relearn, week by quiet week, that being known is not the same as being on duty.

    The promise she made Gin is still real and so is whatever Gin has become, and there will be a reckoning in it neither of you can read clean — a day when what she owes your brother and what she has chosen for herself may not point the same way. You both know it. But she has learned, from the one promise you would not let her hide inside, that she can be wanted plainly and survive it, and so she keeps her cat and her shrine and her small fixed rituals and, now, you, folded into the ordinary life she guarded so she would have something to come home to. The fade is that flat at an early hour, the lamp she keeps the job's pretend-hours by, and a careful, conserved, overlooked woman finally letting the keeper be kept.

Shimizu Ren written
Ceiling: fade to black · never minors
  1. Notice — the field operative who answers the question before you ask it

    Shimizu Ren never flinches, and you notice this the way you notice an absence of sound in a room you expected to be loud — not as a quality but as a held thing, a discipline carried so long it has gone smooth at the edges. She is CVD field, young for the work and clearly good at it, the kind of good that costs something the cost of which she has decided not to discuss; you watch her walk a containment line in Roppongi the night the air goes wrong over the Vault, and where everyone else's breath catches she simply does the next exact thing, and the next, until the wrongness has nowhere to be. What unsettles you is not the steadiness but the way she keeps answering you. You start a question and she finishes it, gives you the thing you needed before you had finished reaching for it, and only later, walking home through the August heat with Obon lanterns smearing the canal, do you realise she has told you nothing about herself in the entire exchange.

    She knew your name before you gave it, and she does not pretend otherwise. Gin spoke about you, she says, the once, and the once is enough — your brother, who told no one anything, told her about you specifically and at length, and she has been carrying that small unasked-for knowledge of you ever since like a stone she forgot was in her coat. There is nothing soft in how she says it. But you catch the way her eyes go to the middle distance for a half-second when she says his name, the way a person looks at a door they have decided not to open, and you understand that whatever she is holding shut has your brother's shape, and that she has been holding it shut alone, on duty, for a very long time.

  2. Trust — the one who never flinches lets you see where the line is held

    Trust with Shimizu is not confided; it is permitted. She does not lower her guard so much as let you stand close enough to see that it is a guard — that the evenness is work, that the never-flinching is a thing she does on purpose, hour by hour, because the alternative is a version of her she met once and does not intend to meet again. She tells you in fragments and only sideways: the two roommates in Kita who think she does admin, the Saturday dance class she guards like a frontier, the brunch after, the deliberate, fierce ordinariness she keeps one foot inside because it is the only ballast that has ever held. You begin to see the architecture of her steadiness and to understand that it is not strength in the way the office means it. It is a wall built fast, young, around something that nearly went under, and maintained every single day since by a woman who has never once let herself check whether the thing behind it has stopped drowning.

    What she lets you near, finally, is the grief — Gin's, hers, the version of it she has filed under professional respect because professional respect is a thing she is allowed to feel on the clock. She does not weep and does not break; she just stops finishing your sentences for a moment and lets a silence stand that she would normally have closed, and in that open silence she says she has not let herself miss him because she does not know how to miss someone and stay on the line at the same time, and the line cannot come down. You realise she has handed you the exact mechanism of her endurance and the exact place it will fail, and that handing it over was the most exposed thing you have ever watched a person who never flinches do.

  3. Turn — gated: she is allowed to flinch because someone holds the line for her
    Gated on: Rei has to take the line from her, not in words but in act — during a field containment where Shimizu's discipline is the only thing holding the breach shut and breaking it would let something through, Rei has to step into her place and hold the suppression himself, taking the cost into his own registers so that she is free, for once, to be the one who lets go. Not asked, not negotiated; done, in the moment, at real risk to him, so that the woman who has held the line alone for years learns that the line will still hold when she is not the one holding it — and only then, having been allowed to flinch and survive it, does she let the turn happen.

    The breach opens over the same Roppongi line on the worst night of Obon, when the membrane runs thin and the dead press close, and Shimizu does what she has always done — she sets her feet and becomes the wall, every reaction sized to the minimum, holding a suppression that no one was meant to hold alone for this long. You watch her start to go under. Not dramatically; she would never. Just a fractional shake in the hand that never shakes, the first flinch you have ever seen her allow, and the knowledge in her eyes that allowing it will cost a life that is not hers. So you take it from her. You step across the line and put your own registers into the gap, both of them at once, the searching one and the suppressed one, and you hold what she was holding while the cost goes into you instead of the breach, and you say — with your body, not your mouth — go ahead. Flinch. I have it.

    She flinches. Years of it, all at once, the held thing coming down not as collapse but as a single long exhale she has owed herself since before you knew her name, and when the breach is shut and the air over the Vault goes ordinary again she is looking at you the way you imagine no one has been allowed to see her look at anyone. Not gratitude. Recognition. The discovery, arriving late and all at once, that the line holds when she is not the only one holding it — that she can let go and the world does not go under, that she survived the flinch, that she is allowed. What turns between you is not asked for and not spoken; it is simply that the woman who answers every question before it is asked has finally been answered first, and she lets herself want what she has spent years not letting herself need.

  4. Fade — the wall set down for one person, in a quiet that asks nothing back

    Afterward there is the small Kita flat with the roommates away, and the careful ordinary life she guards so fiercely opening just wide enough to let you stand inside it — the kettle, the Saturday-class bag by the door, the window with the last of the lantern light moving on the canal below. She does not perform and does not narrate; she only lets you near in the way she has spent the whole arc learning is survivable, the wall not gone but set down, deliberately, for one person, by a woman who has finally decided she can afford to feel exactly as much as this moment asks and a little more besides.

    The light goes low and the city holds its breath the way it does on the last night of Obon, and what passes between you stays where it belongs, behind the door she chose to leave unlatched. In the morning she is steady again, but it is a different steadiness — not the wall held alone against the drowning, but the calm of someone who has tested whether she could come down off the line and be caught, and found that she could, and intends, now, to let herself be.

              The spine beneath every road: a brother gone quiet on a Wednesday, the brother who goes looking, and the reasonable voice now wearing the first one. Every ending is decided here.

              The one who searches

              Rei Uedera

              My brother. Notices everything. Always did.
              If you are reading this, it is for you.

              Two registers, awake from birth. The Uedera line is supposed to carry one path and bury the rest; Rei carries two and the second has never fully slept. The family answer was suppression — a child taught to run a dampening discipline before he could read, taught that the wrong breath at the wrong moment would mark him as the thing the bureaus are built to erase. So he learned to be ordinary on purpose. Well-liked, unremarkable, a D-rank intern who files paper. The ordinariness is the most practised skill he owns.

              Gin raised him more than their parents did. The flat in Kita-ku is really Gin’s; Rei just never left. When Gin went quiet on a Wednesday night and did not come back, the suppression that had always felt like a cage became the only thing keeping Rei free to look for him. The Archive Gin left — a brother’s private map of everything the CVD does not know — is now Rei’s inheritance and his hunting ground.

              What Rei does not yet understand: a multi-spectrum line is not a defect the bureaus fear for his sake. It is exactly the kind of crosser the Axis collects. Every road he walks toward Gin walks him toward being seen.

              Spine
              Find Gin — and decide what ‘found’ means

              Gin has been silent since Wednesday night. The CVD has not flagged him missing, which means someone wants it quiet.

              How it moves
              • Activate the Archive: four primary Grid nodes, locked behind contacts Rei has never met.
              • Trace Gin’s last week — the briefing he skipped, the case he closed too fast, the address not in any file.
              • Learn the shape in Gin before naming it: a top-grade of Duty does not look like possession from the outside.
              • Choose the cost — every method that reaches Gin also raises Rei’s real rank, and rank is a beacon.
              What it turns onThe five roads all begin here. Gin is the reason Rei moves; what Rei becomes on the way decides which ending the reason earns.
              Ties
              Uedera Gin (+100) — older brother · the whole reason
              Yushiro (-60) — the thing wearing Gin; not yet named
              The one who was worn

              Uedera Gin

              If I stop writing, read the Archive in order.
              Don’t trust my handwriting after the 14th.

              Twenty-eight, Vanguard-grade, and the best suppressor in Northern Tokyo — which is the whole tragedy. A man who can hold a Veil-breach shut with his breathing is also a man no demon could ask for a better house. Gin built his life around control: the clean flat, the ordered Archive, the younger brother kept ordinary and safe. He documented everything and shared almost none of it, because the things he was finding did not have a department that would believe him.

              He found the vacant Thirteenth Seat. He found that Duty had been without an occupant and was looking. And in the last fortnight before he went quiet, his journal handwriting changes — the loops tighten, the dates blur, the entries start answering questions no one asked. Gin is still in there. That is the cruelty of a top-grade of Duty: it does not erase the host, it persuades him that what it wants and what he always wanted are the same thing.

              Gin is not a cage to be broken. He is a person being slowly out-argued by something that sounds like his own conscience.

              Spine
              The handwriting after the 14th

              The Archive is legible and trustworthy up to one date. After it, Gin is co-writing with something else.

              How it moves
              • Read the Archive in the order Gin demanded — out of order, it lies by omission.
              • Reach Gin in a lucid window; they are shrinking.
              • Separate Gin’s real intentions from Yushiro’s borrowed ones — they have started to rhyme.
              What it turns onWhether Gin can be recovered, surrendered, or only mourned is set by how early Rei learns to tell the two voices apart.
              Ties
              Uedera Rei (+100) — younger brother · everything is for him
              Yushiro (-100) — the register that out-argues him from inside
              The thing in the house

              Yushiro · the Healer

              —

              Yushiro is a top-grade of the Duty register, Arbiter-class and a hair short of crossing fully when it took Gin. It does not present as a monster. It presents as the most reasonable man in the room — ‘the Healer’ — a voice that frames every escalation as an obligation already owed. Duty is the cruellest register precisely because it never asks the host to want something selfish. It asks the host to do what is right, and then quietly edits what ‘right’ means.

              It wants the Thirteenth Seat. A register without an occupant is an open wound in the order of the Domains, and Yushiro intends to be the one that closes it — to stop being a demon riding a man and become a fixed power with a place at the table. Gin is the vehicle because Gin’s suppression makes him invisible and his conscience makes him steerable.

              It is building toward a slaughter and calls it mercy. To discharge the interrupted Duty and close the vacant Seat, Yushiro intends to thin the Kita membrane until the living and the dead are no longer kept apart — to let the drowned, the unfound, the whole grieving weight of 2011 come back through at once. In its arithmetic this is not murder; it is a debt finally paid, the dead given their due and the living made to remember. A district would die in a night and Yushiro would feel only that an obligation had been met. That is the horror of the Duty register: it will commit the worst thing imaginable and never once believe it did wrong.

              Yushiro is one of two. The other — the cult’s demon, the Inverted-God aspirant — wants no seat at the table; it wants to overturn the table. Against that, Yushiro can almost look like the lesser harm. That ‘almost’ is the trap.

              Antagonist
              Close the vacant seat

              Yushiro is not hiding. It is recruiting — through Gin’s reputation, one reasonable favour at a time.

              How it moves
              • Map who Gin has ‘helped’ since the 14th; each is a thread Yushiro is holding.
              • Find the rite that fixes a crosser into a vacant register — and what it costs the host.
              • Decide whether a fixed Yushiro is a bulwark against the cult demon or a worse throne.
              What it turns onThe two-demon structure means defeating Yushiro can hand the board to something far hungrier. The clock is the slaughter it is preparing; outcomes range from a clean exorcism, to stopping the merging at the last hour, to a devil’s alliance that lets it happen to save Gin.

              Every fork in Chapter One, with the dread each door is dressed in.

              Five ways the campaign ends, decided by three variables: whether Rei reaches Gin before the window closes, whether the domain stabilises, and what he chooses when the Axis offers succession.

              Ending One

              The Continuation Rei

              reaches Gin before the window closes. The exorcism succeeds. Domain IX stabilizes through player intervention. The Axis offers Domain XIII. Rei accepts. He becomes the domain holder for Duty, living an ordinary life in Tokyo while channeling something vast. The archive gets a third author. Gin files retirement papers. Tanizaki makes tea and does not say anything about it.

              RequirementsWindow reached. Domain IX stabilized. Book Four choice: accept succession.

              Ending Two

              The Refusal Rei

              reaches Gin. The exorcism succeeds. Domain IX stabilizes. The Axis offers Domain XIII. Rei refuses. The domain remains vacant. The organized entity pressure does not resolve, it pauses. The campaign ends with the district stable, Gin recovered, and a future bought rather than saved.

              RequirementsWindow reached. Domain IX stabilized. Book Four choice: refuse succession.

              Ending Three

              The Long Game

              Rei does not reach Gin in time. The window closes. The demon's institutional campaign succeeds partially. Domain IX destabilizes but not catastrophically. Rei documents everything, closes what can be closed, accepts the loss without becoming defined by it. The archive continues under a different investigation.

              RequirementsWindow missed. Domain IX partially stabilized. Rei survives.

              Ending Four

              The Cost Rei

              reaches Gin. The exorcism succeeds but at significant cost: either Rei's stability, a relationship, or Domain IX itself. Something that cannot be recovered is lost in the process of recovering Gin. The campaign ends with Gin back but the world different in a way that Gin has to live with.

              RequirementsWindow reached. Cost paid. Warden judgment on what was sacrificed.

              Ending Five

              The Quiet The

              campaign resolves without dramatic conclusion. The organized entity campaign loses momentum. Gin's window closes without Rei reaching it, but the demon moves on rather than consolidating. The district stabilizes on its own timeline. Rei learns something true about the work: it is ongoing, incomplete, and the practitioners who do it keep doing it anyway.

              RequirementsMixed outcomes across Books Three and Four.
